### 2026-07-16 — Flagged for Chief Architect: possible curve-purpose mismatch between Identity's single `public_key` field and Cryptography & Keys' Sign purpose-convention

**Decision (none — this is a flag, not a resolution):** This capability's implementation treats every `public_key` field in `identity.proto` (the identity root key and every device key) as an Ed25519 signing-capable key, verified via `crypto/ed25519.Verify` (see the BindDevice signing-payload entry above) — this is internally self-consistent and fully tested with real Ed25519 keypairs. However, Cryptography & Keys' concurrently-logged `Sign` decision (2026-07-16, "Sign RPC: Ed25519, purpose-namespaced key-type convention," this file) establishes that its reserved `"identity"` purpose — the purpose `GenerateIdentityKeyMaterial` uses, and the natural candidate for what a real mobile client would pass as `CreateIdentityRequest.public_key` — produces a **DH-capable X25519 key, not an Ed25519 signing key**; only a separate `"sign:"`-prefixed purpose produces an Ed25519 keypair, and that engineer's own tests prove an X25519 and Ed25519 public key derived from the same private bytes differ (they are not interchangeable). If a real mobile client submits its `"identity"`-purpose X25519 public key as `CreateIdentityRequest.public_key` unchanged, `ed25519.Verify` against it will never succeed for a genuine recovery-derived self-signed assertion, because that on-file key was never an Ed25519 key to begin with — this would silently break the entire recovery path (charter §3/§7's "no discretionary authority to reject a validly-signed recovery assertion" is moot if no such assertion can ever verify).

This capability-engineer does not have the authority to resolve this unilaterally — it sits across two independently-evolving, already-frozen-or-decided surfaces (Identity's frozen `identity.proto`, and Crypto's own purpose-convention decision) and is exactly the kind of cross-capability integration question that must route back through the Chief Architect rather than being quietly special-cased by either engineer. Two resolutions seem plausible without a proto change: (a) the mobile client generates and submits a second, `"sign:"`-purpose Ed25519 keypair specifically as `CreateIdentityRequest.public_key`/each device's public key — i.e. Identity's `public_key` field is understood to mean "this identity/device's authorization key," decoupled from whatever key(s) a future Messaging/Encryption composition uses for confidentiality — or (b) the charter's Art. 8 manifest language ("the cryptographic material needed to bind devices and let others encrypt to this identity," implying one key serves both roles) needs an explicit amendment acknowledging that binding-authorization and encryption require two distinct keys under the chosen algorithm suite, with `identity.proto` documented accordingly. Not blocking this capability's own implementation or its test suite (which uses real, valid Ed25519 keypairs throughout and does not depend on Crypto's actual output), but blocking for real end-to-end mobile↔backend integration.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (modularity — this capability depends only on published contracts and does not reach into or reinterpret another capability's internal purpose-convention unilaterally), Art. 7 (security — a recovery path that can never actually verify is a silent, disguised failure of the exact guarantee Art. 7 protects).

**Made by:** Identity capability-engineer, flagging for Chief Architect resolution.

### 2026-07-16 — Flagged for Chief Architect: `RevokeDeviceRequest` carries no authorization proof in the frozen contract

**Decision (none — this is a flag, not a resolution):** Unlike `BindDeviceRequest` (which carries `authorization_proof`), the frozen `identity.proto`'s `RevokeDeviceRequest` message has only `identity_ref` and `device_id` — no cryptographic or session proof that the caller is actually authorized to revoke that device. This capability's `RevokeDevice` (`service.go`) implements exactly what the frozen contract specifies: it looks up the device and removes it, with no signature check, and audit-logs the removal. This is not an oversight in this implementation — inventing an out-of-contract field or a bespoke session-auth check inside Identity would be exactly the kind of quiet interface drift this capability-engineer's mandate prohibits. In a real deployment this gap needs to be closed by an authenticated-caller check upstream of this RPC (e.g. a session/gateway layer that has already verified the caller controls one of the identity's bound devices) — that is a composition-layer concern, not something to solve by Identity growing a new field unilaterally.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security — a state-changing operation this sensitive needs an authorization boundary somewhere in the system, even if not inside this capability's own contract), Art. 10 (modularity — the capability engineer does not amend a frozen contract to patch a gap; the gap is raised instead).

**Made by:** Identity capability-engineer, flagging for Chief Architect resolution.

### 2026-07-16 — Finding: `check-audit-events.sh`/`check-export-paths.sh` crash (not just misreport) on a marker substring appearing in unrelated prose

**Decision (none — this is a finding about shared infrastructure, not a decision within this capability's authority):** Both `scripts/constitution/check-audit-events.sh` and `scripts/constitution/check-export-paths.sh` run under `set -euo pipefail`. Each script's marker-discovery loop locates every line containing the literal substring `ascend:mutates`/`ascend:persisted` via `grep -rn ... | xargs -r grep -Hn ...`, including matches inside ordinary prose comments that merely *mention* the marker convention (not an actual marker). For `check-export-paths.sh` specifically, when such a false-positive marker line is not immediately followed by a real `type X struct` declaration, the following line's `type_name=$(echo "$decl" | grep -oE '^type [A-Za-z0-9_]+ struct' | awk '{print $2}')` pipeline has its inner `grep -oE` exit 1 (no match) — under `pipefail`, this makes the whole pipeline (and, under `set -e`, the whole script) exit immediately, silently, with no violation message printed at all, rather than reaching the script's own intended `[ -z "$type_name" ] && continue` skip-and-move-on logic. This is a real bug (a false-positive marker aborts the entire check rather than being safely skipped or reported), not a false alarm — confirmed by reproducing it in isolation against a copy of each script scoped only to `services/api/internal/identity`, where it was triggered by this capability's own now-fixed prose in `identity_export_test.go`, and again against the full repo, where it is currently triggered by pre-existing prose in `services/api/internal/permissions/export.go`/`permissions_export_test.go` (unrelated to this capability, not this capability-engineer's file to edit).

This capability-engineer fixed only its own package's false-positive wording (rephrased `identity_export_test.go` and `audit.go` to describe the marker convention without repeating the exact literal substring) — confirmed clean via `bash scripts/constitution/check-audit-events.sh` and `check-export-paths.sh` scoped to `services/api/internal/identity` alone, both reporting `OK`. The underlying script fragility itself (pipefail against a `grep -oE`-with-no-match inside a command substitution) is shared infrastructure owned by Constitution Warden/Chief Architect, not this capability, and is left unfixed here deliberately rather than edited unilaterally — flagged so it is fixed once, centrally, rather than every capability-engineer independently discovering and locally patching around the same script bug.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (explainability — a mechanical check that crashes silently instead of reporting is itself an explainability gap in the enforcement layer), Art. 10 (modularity — shared CI infrastructure is out of this capability's authority to modify unilaterally).

**Made by:** Identity capability-engineer, flagging for Chief Architect / Constitution Warden.

### 2026-07-16 — Identity capability implemented; `go build`/`go test` pass; mechanical checks clean for this package

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/identity` is implemented end to end against the frozen `identity.proto` contract: all six RPCs (`CreateIdentity`, `BindDevice`, `RevokeDevice`, `ResolveIdentity`, `ListDevices`, `ExportIdentity`), an HTTP surface exported as `Mount(svc *Service) http.Handler` (a sub-router, mountable via `r.Mount("/prefix", identity.Mount(svc))` — main.go untouched, per this implementation wave's concurrency rules; this is a deliberate small deviation from the brief's literal `Mount(r chi.Router, svc *Service)` suggestion, still "an idiomatic Chi pattern" per the brief's own allowance, chosen so this package never needs a reference to the specific router instance main.go constructs), a `DATA_MANIFEST.md`, and a test suite covering every scenario named in this capability's "done" checklist: full `CreateIdentity → BindDevice (already-bound-device signature) → ListDevices → RevokeDevice` flow; `BindDevice` via the recovery-path self-signed assertion; a rejected `BindDevice` with a forged signature (and a separately-tested malformed/wrong-length proof); duplicate-device-key rejection; not-found handling for both identity and device; full `CreateIdentity` input validation; `ExportIdentity` producing a complete, independently-parseable JSON record; a `NoopAuditEmitter` failing loudly rather than silently; a real HTTP round-trip smoke test; and an integration test proving the real `internal/audit.Service` satisfies this package's `AuditEmitter` with zero adapter code. `go build ./...` and `go test ./...` both pass for the whole `services/api` module (`internal/audit`, `internal/identity`, `internal/permissions` — 3 packages, all green; verified output: `ok github.com/ascend/services/api/internal/audit`, `ok github.com/ascend/services/api/internal/identity`, `ok github.com/ascend/services/api/internal/permissions`). `gofmt -l` is clean. `scripts/constitution/check-data-manifests.sh`, `check-file-objects.sh`, and `check-modularity.sh` report `OK` for the full repo; `check-audit-events.sh` and `check-export-paths.sh` report zero findings attributable to this package when scoped to it in isolation (see the finding entry immediately above for why the full-repo run currently aborts on an unrelated, pre-existing issue in `internal/permissions`).

**Rationale:** Recorded as the completion marker for this implementation wave's Identity work, mirroring the reference pattern Permissions' and Cryptography & Keys' engineers established at their own completion entries — the full build/test/mechanical-check evidence is logged here rather than only asserted, so a guardian reviewing at the merge gate has a concrete, reproducible starting point, and so the two flagged cross-capability gaps above (public-key curve-purpose mismatch, `RevokeDeviceRequest`'s missing authorization proof) are not lost between this entry and the merge gate.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (explainability — this entry itself makes the implementation's actual verification state discoverable), Art. 9 (exportability — `ExportIdentityRecord`/`ExportDevice` verified end-to-end via dedicated round-trip tests), Art. 10 (modularity — verified no cross-capability internal import beyond the documented `internal/audit` type-alias, confirmed by `check-modularity.sh`).

**Made by:** Identity capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-16 — Audit storage: in-memory Store first pass, interface-isolated for a future durable backend

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/audit`'s persistence boundary is a small `Store` interface (`Append`, `Get`, `Query`, `All`) with exactly one implementation for this pass, `InMemoryStore` (`store.go`): a mutex-guarded, process-lifetime slice plus an ID-indexed map. `Append` is the only write path — there is deliberately no `Update` or `Delete` method on the interface at all, not merely an unimplemented one, so the append-only guarantee is structural in the interface shape, not just a convention the in-memory implementation happens to honor. `Service` (the RPC-facing type) depends only on `Store`, never on `InMemoryStore` directly, so a future Postgres-backed implementation (e.g. an append-only table with `UPDATE`/`DELETE` grants revoked at the DB-user level, to make "not even platform operators" real at the infrastructure layer, not just the Go layer) can be swapped in without touching `Service`, `Emit`, or any call site in another capability.

**Rationale:** No database wiring exists anywhere in `services/api` yet (no `internal/platform` DB package, no connection pooling, no migrations) — building real Postgres persistence for Audit alone, ahead of a repo-wide decision on how capabilities share a database connection/migration story, would be scope creep into infrastructure that isn't chartered to this capability. An in-memory store is sufficient to prove out and test the actual hard part of this capability (the hash-chain tamper-evidence design, the fail-closed permission gating, the export format) correctly and completely; the interface boundary means none of that logic needs to be revisited when real durable storage is wired in later — only a new `Store` implementation needs to be written and swapped via `NewService`/`SetDefault`. Flagged here explicitly as a known limitation, not a silent one: today's `InMemoryStore` does not survive a process restart, which is a real gap against the charter's "genuinely append-only, not just in convention" bar for a production deployment — tracked as a follow-up, not resolved by this pass.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 15 (minimize hidden state — the storage boundary and its current limitation are explicit, not implicit), Art. 10 (modularity — `Service` depends on the `Store` interface it owns, not a concrete database technology, so swapping storage never becomes another capability's problem).

**Made by:** Audit / Explainability capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-16 — Tamper-evidence mechanism: content-addressed SHA-256 hash chain, EventID is the hash

**Decision:** Every `AuditEvent`'s `EventID` is not a random UUID — it is the hex-encoded SHA-256 hash (`hash.go`'s `computeEventHash`) of a deterministic JSON encoding of the event's own fields (`actor`, `action`, `resource_type`, `resource_id`, `rule_reference`, `metadata`, `occurred_at_unix`) plus `PrevHash`, the `EventID` of the immediately-preceding event in global append order (empty string for the very first event ever emitted). Determinism across runs comes from `encoding/json`'s documented behavior of sorting `map[string]string` keys alphabetically when marshaling, so no hand-rolled canonicalization was needed. Because each event's identity is a function of its own content *and* the identity of the event before it, the events form a hash chain: `VerifyIntegrity` (`store.go`) walks a slice of events and, for each one, (a) recomputes its content hash and confirms it matches the stored `EventID`, and (b) confirms its `PrevHash` equals the previous event's `EventID`. Editing any field of any historical event, or removing/reordering an event, is detected by this walk — proven directly in `store_test.go`'s `TestVerifyIntegrity_DetectsTamperedField` (mutates a stored event's `Action` field post-hoc and confirms detection) and `TestVerifyIntegrity_DetectsBrokenLinkage` (removes a middle event and confirms the linkage break is detected).

**Rationale:** Charter §6 requires tamper-evidence "including by platform operators" — a mechanism that only prevents edits through this package's own API (e.g. simply omitting an `Update` method, as the `Store` interface also does) is necessary but not sufficient, because an operator with direct database access could still rewrite a row and no one would ever know. A hash chain makes such an edit *detectable* after the fact by anyone holding a copy of the trail (including the user themselves, via the export bundle — see the next entry), which is the concrete, checkable form of "not silently editable" the charter asks for. Content-addressing `EventID` itself (rather than a separate hash field alongside a random ID) was chosen over alternatives like a Merkle tree or per-batch signing: a Merkle tree would add complexity with no benefit for a single, strictly-ordered, single-writer-per-process event log (no need for parallel-branch proofs); a simple hash chain is the minimal mechanism that provides the required detection property and is easy for an external, non-Go reader of an exported bundle to independently re-verify (see `verification_note` in the export bundle, and the next entry).

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (explainability — a tampered trail is not really explainable; detectability is what makes the "why/when/by-what-rule" guarantee trustworthy rather than theater), Art. 1 (ownership — the user, not the platform, is the ultimate arbiter of whether their trail is intact, which requires them to be able to check it independently).

**Made by:** Audit / Explainability capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-16 — Explain template: synthesize only from the event's own fields, documented sentence shape

**Decision:** `Explain` (`explain.go`'s `renderExplanation`) builds a plain-language sentence entirely from `AuditEvent`'s own fields, using this shape: `"On <RFC3339 timestamp>, <actor> performed '<action>'[ on <resource_type> '<resource_id>'][, authorized by rule '<rule_reference>'].[ Additional context: <sorted metadata k=v pairs>.]"`. The resource clause is included only when at least one resource field is non-empty (not every action targets a specific resource); the rule clause only when `rule_reference` is non-empty; the metadata clause only when metadata is non-empty, with keys listed in sorted order for determinism (so the same event always explains identically, testable without flakiness). No field is invented, resolved, or looked up from another capability — `actor` and `rule_reference` are printed as their raw opaque strings (e.g. `identity:abc123`, `permissions.share_grant`), not resolved to a display name or a human policy description, because this capability does not import Identity or Permissions to do that resolution (charter §3's lazy-resolution/opaque-reference design) and fabricating a friendlier-looking sentence using data this function doesn't actually have would misrepresent what's known.

**Rationale:** Charter §3's own worked example ("this file's permissions changed on \<date\> because \<grantor\> shared it with \<subject\>") reads as if Explain has semantic knowledge of Permissions' grant model, but the charter's own task framing is explicit that, for this first pass, honest synthesis from the event's own fields is the correct bar — not fabricating specifics the capability doesn't have. Keeping the template a pure function of `AuditEvent` (no external calls, no I/O) also makes it trivially unit-testable and keeps `Explain`'s only failure modes "event not found" / "not authorized," never "the enrichment call to another capability failed." A future pass with real cross-capability semantic knowledge (e.g. resolving `actor` to a display name via Identity, or `rule_reference` to a human-readable policy description via Permissions) can enrich this without changing `renderExplanation`'s signature (`AuditEvent -> string`) or `Explain`'s contract.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (explainability — the literal purpose of this operation), Art. 15 (minimize hidden state — the template is documented in code and here, not an implicit convention).

**Made by:** Audit / Explainability capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-16 — Cross-subject visibility: fail-closed default, one documented permission action shared by Query/Explain/ExportAuditTrail, caller identity is a Go-level parameter outside the frozen wire messages

**Decision:** `Service.Query`, `Service.Explain`, and `Service.ExportAuditTrail` all take an explicit `callerActor string` parameter that is NOT part of the frozen `QueryRequest`/`ExplainRequest`/`ExportAuditTrailRequest` proto messages (none of which carry a distinct "who is asking" field, only a target `subject`/`identity_ref`/`event_id`). A caller may always see events where they are the actor/subject/identity_ref (`callerActor == filter.Subject`, `callerActor == event.Actor`, `callerActor == identityRef`) — unconditionally, per Art. 1. For any cross-subject request, all three operations check the same single `PermissionChecker.CheckPermission(callerActor, "audit:query_other", <target resource>)` call (constant `ActionQueryOther`) and deny (`ErrPermissionDenied`) unless it returns `true`. `NewService` defaults to `denyAllChecker{}` (never "allow all") whenever no real checker is injected — the fail-closed default the task brief and charter §6 both specify. The HTTP layer (`http.go`) supplies `callerActor` by reading a `X-Ascend-Actor` request header, explicitly documented as a placeholder for real session/token authentication (no auth middleware exists anywhere in `services/api` yet) rather than a real verified identity claim.

**Rationale:** The frozen contract's request messages don't model a caller distinct from the query's target subject, which makes sense for a contract drafted before session/auth wiring existed — but `Service`'s Go-level API needs to distinguish "whose trail" from "who's asking" to implement the charter's own stated visibility rule at all, so `callerActor` was added as a Go-only parameter (in-process callers, e.g. a future Permissions-aware caller, pass it directly; no wire-message shape was changed, so this doesn't touch the frozen contract). Reusing one `ActionQueryOther` action across all three operations, rather than inventing `audit:explain_other`/`audit:export_other` as separate actions, was a deliberate simplification: the charter names only `"audit:query_other"` as the example action, and inventing additional undocumented action names not grounded in any Permissions charter language would be scope creep into a decision that belongs to Permissions' own action-namespace design. This is flagged as a first-pass placeholder, not a final design — the true fix (a verified caller identity from a real session/auth layer, and possibly per-operation actions once Permissions defines its action namespace) is a follow-up once Identity/Permissions' auth story is wired end-to-end.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 1 (ownership — a user's own trail is unconditionally theirs), Art. 8/charter §6 "Visibility leakage" (a user must not learn facts about another user's private resources beyond what Permissions already allows them to know directly — enforced here by failing closed whenever no real checker is wired), Art. 10 (modularity — `PermissionChecker` is a locally-defined interface satisfied via dependency injection, never a direct import of `internal/permissions`).

**Made by:** Audit / Explainability capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-16 — Network-reachable Emit endpoint: HTTP+JSON under /v1/audit, for the mobile Crypto audit stub's eventual wiring

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/audit/http.go` exposes `Mount(r chi.Router, svc *Service)`, mounting four HTTP+JSON routes under `/v1/audit`: `POST /v1/audit/events` (Emit), `GET /v1/audit/events` (Query, via `subject`/`resource_type`/`resource_id`/`action`/`since`/`until` query params), `GET /v1/audit/events/{eventID}/explain` (Explain), and `GET /v1/audit/export` (ExportAuditTrail, via an `identity_ref` query param, defaulting to the caller if omitted). This is the network-reachable half of `Emit` the task brief calls for: `apps/mobile/src/capabilities/crypto/audit.ts`'s `logAuditEvent` stub (currently console-log-only) is the documented future caller of `POST /v1/audit/events`, though wiring that stub to this endpoint is explicitly out of scope for this pass — this entry records that the endpoint genuinely exists, is tested end-to-end (`http_test.go`), and is not itself a placeholder. `main.go` was NOT touched — mounting `audit.Mount` into the real server is the Chief Architect's job once all three parallel capabilities (Identity, Permissions, Audit) land, per the existing DI/wiring convention.

**Rationale:** REST-shaped HTTP+JSON (rather than standing up a real gRPC server) matches every other capability's stated implementation pattern so far (Chi router, hand-rolled JSON DTOs mirroring the frozen proto messages, since `buf generate` codegen isn't wired up yet per the 2026-07-16 "Identity, Permissions, Audit / Explainability interfaces frozen" entry) — introducing gRPC as the transport for exactly one capability's one network-facing surface, while every in-process Go caller uses a different (function-call) transport entirely, would add a second RPC stack to the repo for no benefit this pass actually needs. The `X-Ascend-Actor` header (see the entry above) is the one deliberately-flagged placeholder in this surface: it is not real authentication, and is documented as such at its declaration site in `http.go` and in the decision above, so it is not mistaken for a finished security boundary.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (explainability — this is the concrete mechanism, not just an aspiration, by which Cryptography & Keys' on-device stub can eventually reach a real audit substrate), Art. 10 (modularity — `Mount` is exported for the Chief Architect to wire into `main.go`; this package never touches `main.go` itself).

**Made by:** Audit / Explainability capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-16 — Metadata over-collection guard: bounded size limits plus explicit purpose-only convention in DATA_MANIFEST.md

**Decision:** `Emit`'s `validateMetadata` (`service.go`) rejects (`ErrMetadataTooLarge`) any call with more than 32 metadata keys, any key longer than 128 characters, or any value longer than 2048 characters. `services/api/internal/audit/DATA_MANIFEST.md` documents, per field, why it's collected, and states a standing constraint on `metadata` specifically: it may only describe *that* and *how* an action happened, never carry sensitive content itself (the same "record that a file was decrypted and by whom, never the plaintext" discipline named in charter §6), and recommends hashing potentially-identifying free-form strings before they reach metadata — citing Cryptography & Keys' own `auditFingerprint` precedent (2026-07-16, "Fix: hash SecureLocalStore key names before they reach audit metadata") as the model other capabilities calling `audit.Emit` should follow.

**Rationale:** Charter §6 names over-collection as the primary threat this capability's own design must actively guard against, precisely because it's the one place in the platform that naturally accumulates a summary of everything — "just log everything, we might need it" is the failure mode named explicitly. A hard size cap cannot fully enforce "no content, only facts" (a caller could still stuff a small amount of the wrong *kind* of data into a short string), so it is deliberately framed as a lightweight structural guard against the worst case (accidental large-content dumping, e.g. an entire file path list or a message body) plus a documented convention every future `audit.Emit` caller is expected to follow — the same split between "mechanically enforced where possible" and "reviewed at code-review time where it can't be" the wider Art. 8 discipline already uses elsewhere in this codebase.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 8 (privacy is the default; minimum data, documented purpose per field — this capability's own data manifest, applied to itself).

**Made by:** Audit / Explainability capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-16 — Found and fixed: literal marker text in prose comments crashes the shared mechanical checks; flagging a related crash in Permissions' code as out-of-scope

**Decision:** While verifying this capability against `scripts/constitution/check-audit-events.sh` and `check-export-paths.sh`, discovered that both scripts crash (exit 1, no violation output, no "OK") rather than merely reporting a false positive, whenever the literal marker text (`ascend:mutates` / `ascend:persisted`) appears in a prose comment that is not immediately followed by a real function/struct declaration. Root cause: both scripts run under `set -euo pipefail`, and each computes a value via a `var=$(cmd1 | grep -oE ... | awk ...)` command substitution; when the marker's next line isn't the expected declaration, `grep -oE` finds no match and exits 1, `pipefail` propagates that as the pipeline's exit status even though `awk` (the last command) exits 0, and `set -e` then terminates the whole script immediately, silently, mid-loop — no violation message, no partial results, just a bare exit 1. This capability's own first draft had four such prose occurrences (`package_default_test.go`'s and `service.go`'s explanatory references to the `ascend:mutates` convention, and `export.go`'s/`event_export_test.go`'s references to the `ascend:persisted` convention); all four were reworded to describe the convention without reproducing the literal trigger string, and `go build`/`go vet`/`go test` and both scripts (scoped via `grep` to this package's own directory) now confirm this package contains zero remaining stray occurrences of either marker string, outside its one genuine, correctly-placed marker comment (`types.go:30`, immediately preceding `type AuditEvent struct`).

Separately, `services/api/internal/permissions/export.go:45` (`// Not marked ascend:mutates (it does not change state) but still emits an audit event: ...`) and several files in `services/api/internal/identity` and `services/api/internal/permissions` (each grepped and confirmed, not modified) contain the same class of prose occurrence and independently trigger the identical crash when either script is run repo-wide. This capability-engineer did **not** edit those files — they belong to the concurrently-running Identity and Permissions capability-engineers, out of this capability's scope per the standing "never touch another capability's code" rule — and did not edit the shared scripts themselves, which are owned by Constitution Warden, not by any capability engineer.

**Rationale:** This is exactly the kind of cross-capability, shared-infrastructure issue that should be routed back through the Chief Architect rather than fixed unilaterally (fixing the Permissions file myself would violate "never touch another capability's code"; fixing the shared script myself would be acting outside this capability's ownership of `scripts/constitution/`, which belongs to Constitution Warden). Recording it here, now, so it doesn't get rediscovered independently by whichever capability engineer's merge gate runs next and burns time re-diagnosing the same `set -e`/`pipefail` interaction. Recommended fix for the shared scripts (for Constitution Warden to evaluate, not prescribed as binding): either require the marker to be the sole content of its comment line (a stricter, less ambiguous grep pattern, e.g. anchored to `^\s*//\s*ascend:(mutates|persisted)\s*$`) so prose mentions never match, or guard the two `var=$(...)` pipelines with `|| true` and an explicit empty check rather than letting `set -e` terminate the whole script on a single malformed marker.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (explainability — a mechanical check that crashes silently instead of reporting is itself an explainability gap in the platform's own tooling), Art. 10 (modularity — the fix stayed inside this capability's own directory; the cross-capability finding was surfaced, not unilaterally patched into other capabilities' files or the shared script).

**Made by:** Audit / Explainability capability-engineer. Flags a follow-up for the Chief Architect / Constitution Warden: harden `scripts/constitution/check-audit-events.sh` and `check-export-paths.sh` against this crash mode, and confirm whether Identity's/Permissions' own prose occurrences should be reworded (their capability engineers' call, or a Constitution Warden directive) before their next merge gate.

### 2026-07-16 — Audit / Explainability capability implemented; `go build`/`go test` pass

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/audit` is implemented end to end against the frozen `audit.proto` contract: all four RPCs (`Emit`, `Query`, `Explain`, `ExportAuditTrail`), an in-process `Emit(...)` package function every other backend capability calls directly, an HTTP surface mounted via an exported `Mount(r chi.Router, svc *Service)` (main.go untouched), a content-addressed hash-chain tamper-evidence design (`hash.go`/`store.go`), a `DATA_MANIFEST.md`, and a test suite covering: emit → query own trail; emit rejects missing actor/action and oversized metadata; cross-subject query/explain/export denied by the fail-closed default and allowed once a permitted `PermissionChecker` is wired (scoped, not blanket); `Explain` synthesizing a rationale purely from event fields; tamper-evidence proven directly (`VerifyIntegrity` detects both a mutated field and a broken chain linkage from a deleted event); the `// ascend:persisted`-required `ExportAuditEvent` function proven to round-trip through JSON and independently recompute its own hash; and an end-to-end HTTP test exercising Emit → Query → Explain → Export against a real `httptest.Server`. `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...`, and `go test ./...` all pass for the whole `services/api` module (including the concurrently-developed `identity`/`permissions` packages, which this capability does not depend on or modify). `gofmt -l` is clean for this package. `scripts/constitution/check-modularity.sh` and `check-data-manifests.sh` report zero violations attributable to this package (the latter's one remaining repo-wide violation is in Identity's manifest, out of this capability's scope); `check-audit-events.sh` and `check-export-paths.sh` are confirmed clean for this package specifically via targeted `grep` (see the entry above — both scripts crash repo-wide on pre-existing, unrelated marker-text occurrences in Identity's and Permissions' own files, which this capability-engineer neither introduced nor is authorized to fix).

**Rationale:** Recorded as the completion marker for this implementation wave's Audit / Explainability work, mirroring the reference pattern established at Cryptography & Keys' and Permissions' merge-gate entries — the full build/test/mechanical-check evidence is logged here rather than only asserted, so a guardian reviewing at the merge gate has a concrete, reproducible starting point, including the one known tooling gap (the shared script crash mode) that a guardian reviewing this package should not mistake for a real violation in this capability's own code.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (explainability — this entry itself makes the implementation's actual verification state discoverable), Art. 10 (modularity — verified no cross-capability internal import, confirmed by `check-modularity.sh`).

**Made by:** Audit / Explainability capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-16 — Fix: mechanical check scripts crashed on marker text inside prose, not just genuine markers

**Decision:** `scripts/constitution/check-audit-events.sh`, `check-export-paths.sh`, and `check-file-objects.sh` all located marker occurrences via a bare substring `grep` (e.g. `grep -rn ... 'ascend:mutates' .`), which matched the marker text anywhere it appeared — including inside ordinary explanatory comments like `// Not marked ascend:mutates (it does not change state)`. When a matched line wasn't actually followed by a real function/struct declaration, the downstream `type_name=$(... | grep -oE ... | awk ...)` / `func_name=$(...)` assignments failed (under `set -euo pipefail`, a mid-pipeline `grep -oE` finding no match propagates as the pipeline's exit status even though the trailing `awk` succeeds), and because a bare assignment isn't exempt from `set -e`, the whole script exited immediately and silently — no violation message, no "OK," just a bare non-zero exit. Two independent capability-engineers (Identity, Audit) hit and correctly flagged this rather than silently working around it. Fixed two ways: (1) the marker-detection `grep` is now anchored (`^[[:space:]]*//[[:space:]]*ascend:<marker>[[:space:]]*$`) so only a line that *is* the marker comment, not one that merely mentions it, is treated as a real marker; (2) the `type_name=`/`func_name=` extraction assignments are wrapped so a no-match downstream never kills the script outright, as defense in depth. Verified: `scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` now passes cleanly across all three newly-landed capabilities' code, and a fixture with a genuine violation (a `// ascend:mutates` function with no `audit.Emit(` call) is still correctly caught.

**Rationale:** A CI check that can be silently taken down by someone writing a normal English sentence that happens to contain the marker string is worse than no check at all — it fails exactly when the codebase is growing (more prose, more capabilities), and fails silently, which is the "explainability theater" failure mode Article 5 itself exists to prevent, applied to the tooling that enforces Article 5.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (the check itself must be trustworthy, not just present).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, resolving findings independently surfaced by the Identity and Audit / Explainability capability-engineers.

### 2026-07-16 — Known, tracked gap: no request/session authentication capability exists yet

**Decision:** Identity, Permissions, and Audit's frozen contracts all accept the acting identity (`identity_ref`/`subject`/`grantor`/`actor`) as a plain request field or, for Audit's HTTP surface, an unauthenticated `X-Ascend-Actor` header — nothing in any of the three verifies the caller actually *is* that identity. Concretely: `RevokeDeviceRequest` carries no proof field at all (unlike `BindDeviceRequest`), so as implemented, anyone who can reach the endpoint can revoke any device from any identity. This is not a defect introduced by any one capability-engineer — it's a genuine, charter-level gap: no Phase 1 charter defined a session/token/request-authentication capability, because the six charters were scoped around what each capability *does* once a caller's identity is already known, not how that caller's identity gets established and verified per-request. Identity's engineer correctly declined to invent an out-of-contract field unilaterally and routed it back here.

**This is tracked, not fixed, in this pass.** All three implementations are otherwise sound for what they were asked to build — internal logic, mutual composition via opaque references, and mechanical/guardian-checkable obligations — and adding authentication now would mean guessing at a design (tokens? signed requests? session cookies?) that deserves its own charter and its own Security Steward gate, the same rigor every other security-sensitive surface in this codebase has gotten. None of these three capabilities should be considered production-ready or exposed to a real network perimeter until that capability exists and is wired in front of them. Tracked as task "Charter a Session/Request Authentication capability."

**Rationale:** Recording this now, prominently, rather than letting three engineers' independent, correct "not mine to invent" calls quietly become an assumed non-issue. A gap that's tracked in one visible place is very different from the same gap discovered later by someone assuming it was already handled.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — an unauthenticated revoke/mutate path is a real ownership risk, not a hypothetical one), Art. 5 (explainability — this gap itself must be discoverable, not silently assumed away).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, consolidating findings independently surfaced by the Identity capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-16 — Fix: identity root key must be Ed25519 (signing-capable), not X25519

**Decision:** `generateIdentityKeyMaterial` and `restoreFromRecoveryPhrase` (`apps/mobile/src/capabilities/crypto/index.ts`) now derive the identity root public key via `ed25519.getPublicKey(privateKey)` instead of `x25519.getPublicKey(privateKey)`, and register the resulting handle under the reserved purpose `"sign:identity"` (a new `IDENTITY_KEY_PURPOSE` constant) instead of the bare `"identity"` string used previously. Under `keyPurpose.ts`'s existing `"sign:"`-prefix convention, this makes the identity key correctly signing-capable — `sign()` now accepts it, whereas before it always rejected it. `decrypt()`/`deriveSharedSecret()` now correctly reject the identity key (it's Ed25519, not X25519), which is the intended behavior: nothing in this module or its consumers calls those operations with the identity key specifically, only with separately-generated device/session keys from `GenerateKeyPair`. `mnemonic.ts` was updated to match: its doc comments and the `IDENTITY_HKDF_INFO` domain-separation string (`"ascend-crypto-v1:identity-x25519"` → `"ascend-crypto-v1:identity-ed25519"`) no longer claim a specific curve for the seed it derives — `deriveIdentityPrivateKeyFromPhrase` returns a curve-agnostic 32-byte seed; the caller (`index.ts`) decides which curve to apply it to. `ExportKeyMaterial`'s documented format and `keyPurpose.ts`'s convention comment were both corrected to state the identity key is `"sign:identity"`/Ed25519, not `"identity"`/X25519.

Test suite (`__tests__/crypto.test.ts`) updated to match: every test in the "Encrypt / Decrypt round trip" and "DeriveSharedSecret and ratchet forward secrecy / post-compromise security" describe blocks previously used `generateIdentityKeyMaterial()` results as stand-in DH-capable keys — that was always a test-fixture convenience, not a claim that identity keys are meant for encryption, but it happened to keep working only because the pre-fix bug made identity keys X25519. Both blocks now generate dedicated `generateKeyPair({ purpose: "device-session" })` keys instead, which is what those operations are actually meant to be called with. The "Sign" describe block's `"rejects a DH-capable (X25519) handle — including the identity key"` test had its identity-key assertion removed (that assertion encoded the bug, not a real invariant) and replaced with two new regression tests: one proving `sign()` now succeeds with a freshly generated identity key and the signature verifies via raw `@noble/curves` `ed25519.verify`, and a second proving the same for a `RestoreFromRecoveryPhrase`-restored identity — the exact recovery-path device-binding scenario Security Steward scrutinized hardest during chartering, because it is the only path that survives total device loss. Two new tests were also added confirming `decrypt()`/`deriveSharedSecret()` reject the identity key specifically (not just signing-purpose keys in general). `ExportKeyMaterial`'s two identity-related tests were updated to expect `purpose: "sign:identity"` and to re-derive the exported private key via `ed25519.getPublicKey` instead of `x25519.getPublicKey`.

**Rationale:** Identity's capability-engineer, implementing `BindDevice`, found that the recovery-path device-binding assertion — an already-bound device signing a binding request for a new device, using `Sign()` — could never actually be produced, because the identity key `GenerateIdentityKeyMaterial` returns was registered as a DH-capable (X25519) key, and `Sign()` (correctly, per its own contract) refuses any handle that isn't registered under a `"sign:"`-prefixed purpose. This was a real design defect shipped in the original `Sign` RPC implementation, not a caller error: the identity root key's actual job in this system — proving who a device/identity is, by signing device-binding assertions — is authentication, not confidentiality or key agreement, so it should have been Ed25519 from the moment `Sign` was added, before Identity ever tried to consume it. Coordinator confirmed the finding by reading the code directly (index.ts:67, :246, :162-168) before routing it back rather than accepting Identity's report at face value. This is treated with the same rigor as the ratchet forward-secrecy fix (2026-07-16, "DeriveSharedSecret initial handshake forward secrecy fix") because it has the identical shape: a security-relevant primitive that looked correct in isolation (Sign's own purpose-guard logic is right; GenerateIdentityKeyMaterial's derivation is right) but was wrong in composition, and wrong specifically on the one path — total-device-loss recovery — that exists precisely because every other safety net has already failed.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — a recovery mechanism that cannot actually produce the proof it exists to produce is not a recovery mechanism, it's an unmet promise), Art. 10 (modularity — Identity's `BindDevice` depends on Crypto's `Sign` contract behaving as documented; the defect was in this capability's implementation of that contract, not in Identity's usage of it), Art. 5 (explainability — the regression tests make "identity key can sign, including after recovery" a directly verifiable fact, not an assumption).

**Made by:** Cryptography & Keys capability-engineer, resolving a defect found by the Identity capability-engineer and confirmed by the Chief Architect.

---

### 2026-07-16 — Merge-gate review, wave 2 (Identity, Permissions, Audit / Explainability, Crypto Sign/curve additions)

**Decision:** Constitution Warden passed Identity, Audit / Explainability, and the Crypto & Keys additions with no reservations, and passed Permissions with one required (initially framed as non-blocking) fix: every `AuditEmitter.Emit(...)` call site in `services/api/internal/permissions/{service,export}.go` discards the returned error, unlike Identity's own pattern in this same wave, which fails loudly ("mutation succeeded but audit emit failed"). Constitution Warden also found a real, concrete failure scenario, not a hypothetical: `DefinePolicy` stuffs `default_rules` verbatim into audit metadata with no length guard, and Audit's `Emit` rejects metadata over 2048 chars — so an oversized policy string today causes `DefinePolicy` to report success while silently producing no audit record. Separately, Constitution Warden found Identity's `DATA_MANIFEST.md` omits `created_at_unix`, a field that is in fact persisted and exported. Security Steward passed Permissions, Audit, and the Crypto additions outright, but **vetoed Identity**: `BindDevice`'s signed message (`buildDeviceBindingMessage` in `sign.go`) has no freshness/anti-replay input (no nonce, timestamp, or device-topology hash) — so a captured `authorization_proof`, once valid, remains valid forever, and `RevokeDevice` does not actually invalidate it. A revoked device's original proof can be replayed at any later time to silently re-add it, with the server unable to refuse per its own "no discretionary rejection of a validly-signed proof" obligation. Most severe on the recovery path: the root identity key never rotates, so a leaked root-signed binding proof is a **permanent** standing resurrection capability, not a time-bounded one. Confirmed independently by an ephemeral red-team specialist Security Steward spawned. This is not the same issue as the already-tracked missing-request-authentication gap — it lives in the cryptographic content of the proof itself and is fixable entirely within Identity, no contract amendment needed.

**Also decided, adopted as binding policy, not a soft recommendation:** both guardians independently confirmed (by reading `main.go` directly) that none of Identity's/Permissions'/Audit's HTTP surfaces are currently wired into the running binary — zero network exposure today. Both conditioned their pass on that remaining true: **none of `identity.Mount`, `permissions.Mount`, or `audit.Mount` may be wired into `services/api/main.go`, and none of these three capabilities may be exposed on any reachable network perimeter (including shared staging), until a chartered, gated Session/Request Authentication capability exists and is wired in front of them.** Wiring any of the three into `main.go` before that capability lands requires a fresh Security Steward gate on that specific act, not implicit coverage under this entry.

**Fixes dispatched, this pass:** (1) Identity's capability-engineer — bind a freshness/anti-replay input into the signed binding message (e.g. a monotonic per-identity epoch counter or a hash of the current bound-device-ID set, invalidated by every `BindDevice`/`RevokeDevice`), plus a regression test proving bind→revoke→replay-the-same-proof is now rejected; also fix the `DATA_MANIFEST.md` gap. (2) Permissions' capability-engineer — check and handle every `AuditEmitter.Emit` call's returned error, matching Identity's established pattern. (3) Chief Architect — `scripts/constitution/check-audit-events.sh`'s function-*body* check (`grep -q 'audit\.Emit('`) is still an unanchored substring match, unlike the now-hardened marker-*line* check fixed earlier today; Constitution Warden proved via a fixture that a function with no real audit call but a stray comment mentioning `audit.Emit(...)` produces a false pass. Confirmed not currently exploited by any real code in this repo, but fixed anyway before it is.

**Rationale:** Both guardians did exactly the job the merge gate exists for — catching individually-plausible-looking primitives that are wrong in composition, on the one path (recovery after total device loss) that only matters because every other safety net already failed, mirroring the `DeriveSharedSecret` forward-secrecy finding from wave 1.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — a revoke that doesn't actually revoke is a disguised loss of control), Art. 5 (both the Permissions audit-swallowing gap and the tooling body-check gap), Art. 1 (the main.go exposure gate — the platform must not expose an unauthenticated mutation surface, even inadvertently, before the capability that would make it safe exists).

**Made by:** Security Steward (Identity veto), Constitution Warden (Permissions required fix, Identity manifest gap, tooling finding), Chief Architect (recording, dispatching fixes, adopting the main.go gate as binding policy).

---

### 2026-07-16 — Fix: check and handle `AuditEmitter.Emit`'s returned error at every Permissions call site

**Decision:** Every `s.audit.Emit(...)` call site in `services/api/internal/permissions/{service,export}.go` now checks the returned error, following the same split Identity's implementation already established: on a path where the state change (or, for `ExportPermissions`, the disclosure) has already taken effect — `GrantPermission`'s and `RevokePermission`'s success branches, `DefinePolicy`, and `ExportPermissions` — a failed emit now aborts the response with a wrapped error (`fmt.Errorf(".. but audit emit failed: %w", err)`) instead of returning a plain success with no audit record. On the three rejection branches where the function is already returning a more specific domain error and no state changed (`GrantPermission`'s `grant_denied`, `RevokePermission`'s `not_found` and `revoke_denied`), the audit error is still deliberately discarded (`_, _ = s.audit.Emit(...)`), matching Identity's `BindDevice` rejection-path precedent exactly — there is no silent-success hazard on a path that already returns failure, and swallowing there avoids one error message shadowing a more informative one. `fakeAudit` (`service_test.go`) gained a `failWith error` field: when set, `Emit` returns that error instead of recording an event, letting tests simulate a rejected emit (e.g. Audit's real 2048-char metadata-value limit) without importing `internal/audit`. Four new tests assert the fix: `TestGrantPermissionSurfacesAuditEmitFailure`, `TestRevokePermissionSurfacesAuditEmitFailure`, `TestExportPermissionsSurfacesAuditEmitFailure`, and — the concrete regression test for the exact scenario Constitution Warden identified — `TestDefinePolicySurfacesAuditEmitFailure`, which calls `DefinePolicy` with a 3000-character `default_rules` string (matching the real length that would trip Audit's `maxMetadataValLength = 2048` guard) and asserts the call now fails loudly (`errors.Is(err, errAuditRejected)`) instead of silently succeeding.

**Rationale:** Constitution Warden's wave-2 merge-gate finding (immediately above) was correct and concrete, not hypothetical: `fakeAudit.Emit` always returning `nil` meant this package's own test suite could not have caught a real Audit rejection, and `DefinePolicy` specifically had no guard of its own against the exact oversized-metadata case Audit's `Emit` is documented to reject. Mirroring Identity's exact success-fails-loudly / rejection-discards split (rather than inventing a different rule) keeps the two foundation capabilities' audit-error-handling behavior consistent, per Art. 16 ("consistency beats novelty") — a reviewer who has read one capability's pattern should not have to learn a second, differently-shaped convention for the other. `ExportPermissions` is included even though it does not mutate persisted state, matching Identity's `ExportIdentity` precedent (2026-07-16, "AuditEmitter dependency injection... `ExportIdentity` is marked `// ascend:mutates`... because charter §3/§4 explicitly requires export to be audit-logged"): a disclosure that already happened must not be reported as if its audit record landed when it didn't.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (explainability — a mutation or disclosure whose audit record silently failed to land is not actually explainable, regardless of what the caller was told), Art. 16 (consistency beats novelty — the same audit-error-handling shape as Identity's already-established pattern, not a bespoke third convention).

**Made by:** Permissions capability-engineer, resolving the Constitution Warden required fix in the "Merge-gate review, wave 2" entry above.

---

### 2026-07-16 — Fix: BindDevice replay — monotonic per-identity epoch bound into the signed message; `DATA_MANIFEST.md` gap closed

**Decision:** Resolving the Security Steward merge-gate veto (wave 2 entry above). `IdentityRecord` (`types.go`) gains a new persisted field, `Epoch int64`, starting at `0` at `CreateIdentity` and incremented by exactly 1 on every successful `BindDevice` and every successful `RevokeDevice` (`service.go`) — a strictly monotonic device-topology version counter, never decremented or reset. `buildDeviceBindingMessage` (`sign.go`) now takes a fourth argument, `epoch int64`, appended to the signed byte layout as a NUL-separated decimal string; `authorizesBinding` builds the verification message using `record.Epoch` as it stands *at verification time* — never a value the caller supplies — so a captured `authorization_proof` stops verifying the instant the identity's epoch advances for any reason, including a revoke. A monotonic counter was chosen over the veto's other named option (a hash of the current bound-device-ID set) after confirming the hash approach is insufficient for the exact required regression scenario: revoking device X returns the bound-device-ID set to bit-identical membership to what it was immediately before X was ever bound, so a hash-of-current-set check would let X's original proof verify again post-revoke — the epoch counter never revisits a prior value, so it does not have this gap. `export.go`'s `exportedIdentityRecord`/`ExportIdentityRecord` now include `epoch` in the export blob. `DATA_MANIFEST.md` gained two entries: `created_at_unix` (the Constitution Warden-identified gap — persisted and exported but previously undocumented) and `epoch` (documented as server-generated bookkeeping, not user-supplied data, consistent with the "every persisted/exported field is listed" discipline applied even to non-Art.-8 fields).

The required regression test, `TestBindDevice_RevokedDeviceOriginalProofReplayRejected` (`service_test.go`): binds device X at epoch 0, revokes X, replays the exact original, unmodified `authorization_proof` for X, and asserts `BindDevice` now returns `ErrInvalidSignature` and does not re-add the device — plus a same-test sanity check that a freshly-signed proof against the new current epoch (2, after one bind + one revoke) still succeeds, so the fix is proven not to be overbroad. All five pre-existing call sites that build a signing message for tests (`service_test.go` ×5, `http_test.go` ×1) were updated to pass `0` for epoch, since each corresponds to a fresh identity's first bind attempt.

Flagged, not resolved, in the same pass (documented in `sign.go`'s doc comment and not treated as blocking this fix): `identity.proto`'s response messages (`PublicIdentity`, `Device`, `ListDevicesResponse`) are frozen and expose no epoch field, so a caller cannot fetch "the current epoch" via any RPC before signing — the signer is expected to track it locally (0 at `CreateIdentity`, +1 per bind/revoke it itself observes). If a different device changed the topology in the interim, the signer's local epoch goes stale and its next `BindDevice` attempt correctly fails closed (`ErrInvalidSignature`) rather than silently succeeding on a wrong assumption — a fail-closed safety property, not a silent hole, but a real UX gap for genuine multi-device concurrent modification that a future contract revision (exposing epoch via `ResolveIdentity`/`ListDevices`) would need to close. This is a contract change outside this capability-engineer's authority to make unilaterally; routed to the Chief Architect.

**Verification:** `gofmt -l .` clean; `go build ./...` and `go vet ./...` clean for the whole `services/api` module; `go test ./...` passes for all three packages (`internal/audit`, `internal/identity` — including the new regression test — `internal/permissions`, the latter's own concurrent audit-error-handling fix landing independently and cleanly alongside this one). `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` reports `OK` across all five mechanical checks repo-wide (Art. 2, 5, 8, 9, 10) — including `check-data-manifests.sh`, confirming the `DATA_MANIFEST.md` fix, and `check-audit-events.sh`/`check-export-paths.sh`, now clean thanks to the Chief Architect's shared-script hardening fix landed earlier in this same wave.

**Rationale:** Security Steward's finding was correct and, on the recovery path specifically, severe: the root identity key never rotates, so an unfixed leak of a root-signed binding proof would have been a *permanent* standing resurrection capability, not a time-bounded one. Binding a monotonic counter into the signed message is the minimal mechanism that satisfies the stated requirement exactly ("any change to device topology, including a revoke, must invalidate all previously-signed proofs") without a contract amendment, since `authorization_proof` is opaque bytes at the `identity.proto` level and the byte layout inside it is this capability's own internal convention. Choosing the counter over the device-set-hash alternative was not a coin flip — it was verified against the specific required regression scenario first, and the hash alternative was found to fail it, which is recorded here so the choice reads as evidenced rather than asserted.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — a revoke that can be silently undone by a replayed proof is a disguised loss of control, exactly what Art. 7 forbids), Art. 5 (explainability — the fix and its regression test make "a revoked device's old proof no longer works" a directly verifiable fact, not an assumption; the remaining discoverability gap is documented, not hidden), Art. 10 (modularity — fixed entirely within Identity's own internal convention, no `identity.proto` amendment).

**Made by:** Identity capability-engineer, resolving the Security Steward merge-gate veto above.

---

### 2026-07-16 — Follow-up: epoch discoverability closed via contract amendment; response types now expose current/post-mutation epoch

**Decision:** Resolving the discoverability gap flagged in the entry immediately above. The Chief Architect amended `packages/contracts/proto/ascend/identity/v1/identity.proto` (`buf lint` clean): `PublicIdentity` gains `int64 epoch = 5` (so `CreateIdentity` and `ResolveIdentity` both expose the identity's current epoch), `BindDeviceResponse` gains `int64 epoch = 2` (the epoch *after* that bind), and `RevokeDeviceResponse` gains `int64 epoch = 1` (the epoch *after* that revoke) — the latter two so a client performing several binds in a row can chain off the previous response's epoch without a round-trip back through `ResolveIdentity` each time. This capability's hand-mirrored Go types (`types.go`) were updated to match: `PublicIdentity.Epoch`, `BindDeviceResponse.Epoch`, `RevokeDeviceResponse.Epoch`, all `int64`. `service.go`'s four response-construction sites now populate it from `record.Epoch`: `CreateIdentity`'s `PublicIdentity` (0 for a fresh identity), `ResolveIdentity`'s `PublicIdentity` (current value), `BindDevice`'s response (the local `record.Epoch` value, already incremented in-memory by the preceding `record.Epoch++` before this return), and `RevokeDevice`'s response (same pattern). No new validation or business logic was needed — the counter was already tracked; this only exposes it.

The regression test added for the replay fix, `TestBindDevice_RevokedDeviceOriginalProofReplayRejected` (`service_test.go`), was rewritten to read epoch values from response fields (`created.PublicIdentity.Epoch`, `bindResp.Epoch`, `revokeResp.Epoch`, `resolveResp.PublicIdentity.Epoch`) instead of the hardcoded `0`/`2` literals used in the prior pass, with explicit assertions that each equals the expected value (0 → 1 → 2) — this both keeps the test correct under future refactors and, more importantly, is now itself the executable proof that the discoverability fix works end-to-end (a caller really can fetch the correct epoch from `ResolveIdentity`/`BindDeviceResponse`/`RevokeDeviceResponse` and successfully sign against it, rather than that claim resting on documentation alone). The other five pre-existing test call sites that build a signing message still pass a literal `0`, left as-is since each is a fresh identity's first bind and `0` is both correct and simpler to read than threading a response field through for a value that doesn't change in those tests.

**Verification:** `gofmt -l .` clean; `go build ./...` and `go vet ./...` clean for the whole `services/api` module; `go test ./...` passes for all three packages (`internal/audit`, `internal/identity`, `internal/permissions`). `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` reports `OK` across all five mechanical checks repo-wide (Art. 2, 5, 8, 9, 10).

**Rationale:** This closes the one open item from the replay fix's own decision-log entry ("full resolution... is a contract change outside this capability-engineer's authority to make unilaterally; routed to the Chief Architect") — the fail-closed behavior for a stale local epoch is still correct and still applies when a caller doesn't refresh before signing (e.g. a genuinely concurrent modification from a second device), but a caller now has a real, contract-level way to fetch the authoritative current value first, which was the actual gap. Additive-only proto change (new field numbers, no renumbering, no removed fields) needed no other capability's cooperation and did not touch this capability's own request-shaped types (`CreateIdentityRequest`, `BindDeviceRequest`, `RevokeDeviceRequest` are unchanged) or its verification logic (`authorizesBinding` already only ever reads `record.Epoch` server-side; it never trusted a caller-supplied epoch, so there was nothing insecure to fix here — this was purely an exposure/discoverability gap, not a correctness one).

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (explainability — a caller can now discover, not just be told in documentation, the exact rule an authorization proof must satisfy), Art. 10 (modularity — the exposure is a frozen-contract field addition made by the Chief Architect, consumed here via the same hand-mirrored-types convention every response field already follows; no capability-internal coupling introduced), Art. 12 (the platform stays understandable — "sign against the epoch you can read off the response you just got" is a materially simpler mental model than "track this counter yourself").

**Made by:** Identity capability-engineer, implementing the Chief Architect's `identity.proto` amendment above.

### 2026-07-16 — Wave 2 complete: Identity, Permissions, Audit / Explainability move to `stable`

**Decision:** Security Steward re-reviewed the Identity replay fix specifically (scoped, not a full re-audit) and confirmed it closed: traced `IdentityRecord.Epoch`'s two write sites directly, confirmed no request field lets a caller influence the epoch checked against, re-ran the adversarial regression test directly rather than trusting the report, and confirmed the epoch-discoverability follow-up genuinely exercises `CreateIdentity → BindDevice → RevokeDevice → ResolveIdentity` with real response values, not hardcoded ones. With this, all four items in wave 2 (Identity, Permissions, Audit / Explainability, and the Crypto & Keys Sign/curve additions) have passed both guardians. `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` moves Identity, Permissions, and Audit / Explainability from `frozen` to `stable`, and each charter's §8 gained an "Implementation merge gate" section recording the wave-2 verdicts, mirroring Cryptography & Keys' pattern from wave 1.

**Standing, binding condition carried forward unchanged from the wave-2 review entry:** none of the three are wired into `services/api/main.go`, and none may be exposed on any reachable network perimeter, until a chartered Session/Request Authentication capability exists — recorded directly in the registry's Notes section now, not only in this log, so it can't be missed by skimming the registry alone.

**What this wave found and fixed, for the record:** a missing `Sign` primitive in Cryptography & Keys (recovery-path device binding couldn't produce a proof at all); the identity root key being generated on the wrong curve (X25519 instead of Ed25519, making it unusable with `Sign`); a replay vulnerability in `BindDevice` (Security Steward veto, fixed with a monotonic epoch); an epoch-discoverability gap in the fix itself (client had no way to learn what to sign against); a silent audit-failure-swallowing bug in Permissions; a missing `DATA_MANIFEST.md` field in Identity; and a real crash bug in the mechanical CI scripts themselves, found independently by two capability-engineers. Every one of these was caught before merge, by either a capability-engineer's own diligence or a guardian's review — none reached `stable` code silently.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 17 (asked and re-asked at every stage — each fix demonstrably increased, not just preserved, user ownership), Art. 5 (the full sequence, including two vetoes and their fixes, is discoverable in this log), Art. 10 (modularity held throughout — three capability-engineers worked concurrently with zero internal cross-imports and zero merge conflicts in `main.go`, which none of them touched).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, recording the wave-2 outcome.

### 2026-07-16 — TypeScript-side mechanical checks added; real codegen wired

**Decision:** Two follow-ups from the wave-2 review closed. (1) `scripts/constitution/check-audit-events-ts.sh` added — the TypeScript equivalent of `check-audit-events.sh`, requiring any exported function marked `// ascend:mutates` to call `logAuditEvent(` in its body, scanning `apps/mobile/src/capabilities/**` and `services/ai/capabilities/**`, excluding test files, and anchored/comment-stripped from the start (learning directly from the Go-side false-pass/crash bugs found earlier today, not repeating them). `check-data-manifests.sh` extended to also scan `apps/mobile/src/capabilities/*/` — it immediately caught a real, pre-existing gap: Cryptography & Keys never had a `DATA_MANIFEST.md`. No TS equivalent of `check-export-paths.sh`/`check-file-objects.sh` was added — no TS capability persists data the way a Go struct backed by a store does, so a check there would have zero real signal to validate; documented as deliberately deferred, not forgotten, in `scripts/constitution/README.md`. Cryptography & Keys' engineer retrofitted the missing manifest and added markers to its six real mutating functions (`generateIdentityKeyMaterial`, `generateKeyPair`, `sign`, `deriveSharedSecret`, `restoreFromRecoveryPhrase`, `exportKeyMaterial`), correctly leaving `encrypt`/`decrypt` unmarked per the charter's existing scope.

(2) `buf generate` now works end to end: all four frozen contracts gained an `option go_package` (missing, which is why generation previously would have failed — Go codegen requires it), and `buf generate` (via Buf's remote plugin execution, no local protoc/plugin installation needed) produces real Go structs + gRPC stubs (`services/api/gen/go/`), TypeScript message types (`packages/contracts/gen/ts/`), and Python types (`services/ai/gen/python/`) — all gitignored, regenerated from source. `services/api/go.mod` gained `google.golang.org/protobuf` and `google.golang.org/grpc` as real dependencies (previously absent, since nothing had generated gRPC code to import them yet). Verified: the generated packages build cleanly alongside all three existing hand-written capability packages with zero conflicts (different import paths — `gen/go/...` vs `internal/...`). Deliberately **not** done: migrating the four already-`stable` capabilities' hand-mirrored types to the generated ones — they were built before this toolchain existed under explicit "no codegen yet" instructions, are guardian-gated and working, and migrating them now would be pure churn with no functional benefit. Every capability chartered from this point forward (Storage, File Objects, Session / Request Authentication) implements directly against generated types, per `packages/contracts/README.md`'s updated workflow section.

**Rationale:** Both were tracked, not urgent, gaps — but real capability code now exists on both sides (TS and Go) to actually validate against, and letting a "TS mechanical checks" gap or a "codegen doesn't actually run" gap persist while more capabilities get built on top would only make the eventual fix more disruptive.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (the TS check gives Cryptography & Keys the same audit-event guarantee Go capabilities already have, mechanically, not just by convention), Art. 8 (the manifest scan extension found a real, previously-invisible gap), Art. 10 (generated code is now the enforced source of truth for new capabilities' cross-language type agreement, closing the loop the Protobuf/Buf decision opened on 2026-07-06).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, with the Cryptography & Keys capability-engineer implementing the retrofit.

### 2026-07-17 — Storage interface frozen; crypto-shred fallback resolved as a Storage-owned wrapping key, not the content key

**Decision:** `docs/capabilities/storage.charter.md` §3 is translated into `packages/contracts/proto/ascend/storage/v1/storage.proto` and frozen (`buf lint` clean). Storage is implemented as a normal Go backend service (`services/api/internal/storage`), like Identity/Permissions/Audit, now that all three of its dependencies are `stable`. A real architectural gap was found and resolved before spawning: the charter's `DeleteBlob` crypto-shred fallback (2026-07-06, "Storage gains an explicit deletion primitive") was written assuming Storage could destroy "the blob's encryption key" — but per the same charter's own boundary, Storage never holds the end-to-end content key at all (the client encrypts via Cryptography & Keys before Storage ever sees the bytes), so Storage literally cannot shred a key it never had. Resolved as: for backends where physical byte-level erasure can't be guaranteed, Storage wraps the already-opaque, client-encrypted blob in a second, outer envelope using a key Storage itself generates and holds (Go standard library AEAD, e.g. `chacha20poly1305`) — destroying that self-owned wrapping key is something Storage can actually do. This outer wrapping is a backend-only operational concern, never a second layer of end-to-end encryption, and must never be presented to a user as one.

**Rationale:** This is the same class of gap as Crypto's missing `Sign` RPC and Identity's replay vulnerability — two individually-reasonable decisions (client-only content encryption; Storage performs crypto-shred) that were never checked against each other in composition. Catching it before implementation avoids either a `DeleteBlob` that silently can't fulfill its own stated guarantee, or a capability-engineer inventing a resolution unilaterally.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 15 (minimize hidden state — a wrapping key a user is never told about must still be described honestly in the charter/decision log, not left implicit), Art. 1 (deletion must be real — this is what makes it actually achievable for the backends that need it), Art. 10 (Storage's own wrapping key stays entirely within Storage's boundary, never touching Cryptography & Keys' content-key domain).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, resolving a gap found while preparing to spawn the Storage capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-17 — Session / Request Authentication chartered and gated

**Decision:** `docs/capabilities/session-authentication.charter.md` is written and passes all three guardian gates after one amendment round. The charter closes the standing gap logged 2026-07-16 (Identity/Permissions/Audit accepting unauthenticated caller-identity fields) by proving which bound device of which identity is making a request — `GetSessionChallenge` + `IssueSession` (fresh, single-use-nonce, domain-separated signed proof of device-key possession), `ValidateSession` (server-side lookup, not a self-contained token, so revocation is real and immediate), `RevokeSession`/`RevokeAllSessions` (the latter authorized via the caller's own `caller_session_token`, never a bare `identity_ref`, which is a public value), `ListActiveSessions`, and `ExportSessions`. All three guardians initially blocked, each on a real, specific gap rather than a style objection: Constitution Warden — freshness asserted in prose but not actually in §3, and Art. 9 covering only "no lock-in," not export; Experience Guardian — the Devices/Sessions UI composition question deferred as "not a charter one" when it was exactly the judgment call the gate exists to make; Security Steward — the same freshness gap, plus `RevokeAllSessions`/`ListActiveSessions` taking a bare `identity_ref` (an anonymous account-lockout/fingerprinting primitive, since `identity_ref` is public via Identity's own `ResolveIdentity`), plus an unresolved renewal-mechanism ambiguity that could have silently defeated the "short token lifetime bounds leak exposure" claim. All three closed in one amendment pass; registry status moves `chartering` → `gated`.

**Rationale:** This charter was written to actively incorporate the two hardest lessons from wave 2 (Identity's replay veto, and the general pattern of asserting a security property in prose without wiring it into the interface) rather than risk repeating them — and the guardian gate still caught three real gaps anyway, which is exactly why the gate exists at the charter stage rather than being trusted to "we already learned this lesson."

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 17 (asked and answered concretely — this capability is what makes several already-shipped capabilities' ownership guarantees enforceable, not speculative scope), Art. 7, Art. 9, Art. 12/13 (per each guardian's respective findings above).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, with Constitution Warden, Experience Guardian, and Security Steward gating.

**Decision:** `docs/capabilities/session-authentication.charter.md` §3 is translated into `packages/contracts/proto/ascend/sessionauth/v1/sessionauth.proto` and frozen (`buf lint` clean across all six contracts). Implemented as a normal Go backend service (`services/api/internal/sessionauth`), same pattern as Identity/Permissions/Audit/Storage. The proto encodes the charter's hard-won specifics directly rather than leaving them to prose: `GetSessionChallenge` as its own RPC, `IssueSession.proof` signing a canonical domain-separated message documented in the file header, `RevokeAllSessions`/`ListActiveSessions`/`ExportSessions` all keyed on `caller_session_token` rather than a bare `identity_ref`.

### 2026-07-17 — Session / Request Authentication implementation: hand-mirrored types, not generated

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/sessionauth` is implemented against a hand-written mirror of `sessionauth.proto` (types.go), not generated bindings. Checked directly before starting (`services/api/gen/go/ascend/sessionauth` does not exist, unlike the sibling `gen/go/ascend/{identity,audit,permissions,crypto}` directories which do) — the spawn brief's claim that `buf generate` had already produced this contract's Go bindings did not hold in the actual working tree, so this capability follows the same "temporary hand-mirror" precedent Identity/Permissions established (2026-07-16, "Identity, Permissions, Audit / Explainability interfaces frozen..."), field-for-field against the frozen `.proto`, camelCase JSON tags matching protojson's default output so the HTTP surface won't need to change shape once real codegen lands.

**Rationale:** Consistency with the rest of this implementation wave — inventing a second convention (or blocking on a codegen gap outside this capability's authority to fix) would be worse than following the already-established, already-logged precedent.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (modularity — contract is still the only interface this package builds against, mirrored not reinterpreted), Art. 16 (consistency beats novelty).

**Made by:** Session / Request Authentication capability-engineer.
