### 2026-07-17 — Session / Request Authentication: strict DI, no import of internal/audit even though check-modularity.sh exempts it

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/sessionauth` defines its own local `ResourceRef` struct (types.go) rather than importing `services/api/internal/audit` for `audit.ResourceRef`, unlike Identity's later-adopted pattern (2026-07-16, "AuditEmitter wiring... type alias to audit.ResourceRef"). `AuditEmitter`/`DeviceResolver` are both small locally-defined interfaces satisfied via constructor injection (`NewService(devices DeviceResolver, emitter AuditEmitter) *Service`), with the audit seam held in a field literally named `audit` so `s.audit.Emit(...)` satisfies `scripts/constitution/check-audit-events.sh`'s textual grep for `audit.Emit(` without ever writing `import ".../internal/audit"`.

**Rationale:** This capability's spawn brief was explicit and stricter than the mechanical modularity check's own exemption list ("Never import services/api/internal/identity, internal/permissions, internal/audit, or internal/storage directly"), so it is followed literally even though `check-modularity.sh` would not have flagged an `internal/audit` import (audit is a shared/exempt package there). Every call site checks and handles `s.audit.Emit`'s returned error rather than discarding it (`fmt.Errorf("... but audit emit failed: %w", err)` on every success-path call, verified by `TestIssueSession_AuditEmitErrorIsSurfaced_NotDiscarded`) — the brief specifically flagged that Permissions' engineer got this wrong initially and had to fix it after a guardian caught it; this capability got it right on the first pass instead.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (modularity — no cross-capability internal import at all, stricter than the mechanical floor), Art. 5 (explainability — an audit failure must be surfaced, never silently dropped).

**Made by:** Session / Request Authentication capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-17 — Session / Request Authentication: token/nonce lifetimes, entropy sizes, and two-store design

**Decision:** `DefaultSessionLifetime = 30 minutes`, `DefaultChallengeNonceLifetime = 2 minutes` (service.go). `session_token` is 32 CSPRNG-sourced bytes, `challenge_nonce` is 24 CSPRNG-sourced bytes, both base64url-encoded via a single `crypto/rand.Read` entry point (token.go, mirroring identity/idgen.go's identical "one audited CSPRNG call site, panic if it fails" precedent). Storage is two separate in-memory, mutex-guarded maps (`nonceStore` keyed by nonce, `sessionStore` keyed by session_token — store.go), not one shared store: a consumed nonce is deleted outright rather than marked-and-kept (nothing to export, per DATA_MANIFEST.md's "not retained once consumed or expired"), while a session persists (active or expired) until explicitly revoked, so the two have different enough lifetimes/shapes that sharing one map/mutex would only couple two operations that don't need to serialize against each other.

**Rationale:** 30 minutes is short enough to genuinely bound a leaked token's blast radius (charter §6's core claim) while being long enough that automatic background renewal (fresh `GetSessionChallenge`+`IssueSession`, per charter §5/§6) doesn't need sub-minute-frequency re-signing traffic from a real client. 2 minutes for the nonce is generous slack for one real round trip (request challenge, sign, call IssueSession) while remaining "seconds/low minutes" per the charter's own data-manifest guidance. Both are within the charter's explicitly left-open range (§7: "minutes-to-low-hours, not days" for sessions; implementation-engineer discretion for the nonce) and are logged here as the concrete numeric answer, not left implicit.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security — short lifetime bounds leak exposure, a real not aspirational guarantee per charter §6), Art. 8 (privacy — nonce fields not retained past consumption/expiry), Art. 15 (minimize hidden state — the store-split rationale is stated here, not left implicit).

**Made by:** Session / Request Authentication capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-17 — Session / Request Authentication: nonce anti-replay via peek-then-atomic-consume, verified under concurrency

**Decision:** `IssueSession` (service.go) implements charter §3's five ordered steps exactly: (1) `nonceStore.peek` — a fast, non-mutating existence/expiry/binding check used only to fail fast before touching the DeviceResolver or verifying a signature; (2) `DeviceResolver.ResolveDevicePublicKey`; (3) `ed25519.Verify` against the canonical domain-separated message (sign.go, byte-for-byte the layout the frozen `sessionauth.proto` file header already specifies, NUL-separated fields matching `identity/sign.go`'s `buildDeviceBindingMessage` construction style); (4) `nonceStore.consumeIfValid` — a single mutex-guarded critical section that re-validates and deletes the nonce atomically, called only AFTER step 3 succeeds; (5) session creation. Because verification (step 3) is a pure function with no store-mutation side effect, two goroutines racing the identical captured `(nonce, proof)` pair can both pass steps 1-3, but only one can ever win the atomic step-4 consume — proven directly by `TestIssueSession_ReplayRejected_ConcurrentDuplicateRequestsOnlyOneWins` (25 goroutines firing the exact same request; asserts exactly 1 success), in addition to the simpler sequential-replay test.

**Rationale:** The charter's own language ("must happen atomically with, or immediately after, successful verification, such that a captured, already-used IssueSession request can never succeed twice, even under concurrent replay attempts") is a concurrency claim, not just a logical-ordering one — a sequential "call it twice" test cannot by itself distinguish a genuinely atomic consume from a check-then-act race that only manifests when two requests are in flight simultaneously, so a dedicated concurrent test was added rather than treating the sequential replay test as sufficient evidence of the atomicity property.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security — the anti-replay guarantee must hold under real concurrent adversarial conditions, not just in a single-threaded test), Art. 1 (ownership — the only path to a session is a fresh, unforgeable device signature).

**Made by:** Session / Request Authentication capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-17 — Session / Request Authentication: ValidateSession anomaly threshold, and hashing session_token before it ever reaches the audit trail

**Decision:** `anomaly.go`'s `failureTracker` emits the charter §4 "repeated ValidateSession failures" audit signal (`sessionauth.validate_session_anomaly`) after 5 failures for the same session_token within a 1-minute sliding window, resetting the window on trip so a sustained failure stream emits the signal once per window rather than on every subsequent failure. Every place a session_token might otherwise reach an audit call (the anomaly event's resource ID, `RevokeSession`'s and `RevokeAllSessions`' rejection-path events for an unknown/invalid caller token) uses `tokenFingerprint` (token.go — truncated SHA-256, hex-encoded) instead of the raw token, mirroring Cryptography & Keys' `auditFingerprint` precedent (2026-07-16, "Fix: hash SecureLocalStore key names before they reach audit metadata"). `TestValidateSession_RepeatedFailuresEmitAnomalyAuditEvent` asserts both the trip count and that the raw bad token never appears in the emitted event's resource ID.

**Rationale:** 5/minute is well above what ordinary clock skew or a slightly-stale cached token re-presented once or twice after `RevokeSession`/natural expiry would produce, while tight enough to catch a script probing a stolen or guessed token — charter §7 explicitly leaves the exact threshold to this capability engineer. Hashing the token before it reaches any audit call site is a structural requirement, not a style preference: `session_token` is explicitly documented in DATA_MANIFEST.md as a live bearer credential that must never appear verbatim outside the session store itself (the audit trail is not a secret store), so logging it raw in a "someone is trying stolen tokens" signal would be actively counterproductive — it would hand the very credential being investigated to anyone who can read the audit trail.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (explainability — "has someone been trying to use a stolen/expired session as me" must be answerable without flooding the trail with routine-success noise), Art. 8 (privacy — a sensitive value is hashed before it reaches audit metadata, never logged verbatim).

**Made by:** Session / Request Authentication capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-17 — Session / Request Authentication: ExportSessions structurally cannot contain session_token

**Decision:** `export.go`'s `exportedSession` struct (backing both `ExportSession`, the Art. 9 mechanical per-type export function, and `ExportSessionsBundle`, which backs the `ExportSessions` RPC) has no field for `session_token` at all — not merely an omission at the call site, but a type that has nowhere to put it. `TestExportSession_RoundTripsAndExcludesSessionToken`, `TestExportSessionsBundle_RoundTripsAndExcludesSessionToken`, and `TestExportSessions_NeverContainsSessionTokenValue` (via the real `Service.ExportSessions` RPC path, two live sessions) all assert the exported bytes never contain the literal token value.

**Rationale:** Charter §3/§6 and DATA_MANIFEST.md are explicit that `session_token` is a live bearer credential, not archival data, and that including it in an export would hand out a valid, unrevoked session to whoever holds the export file — a live-authentication capability re-exposing active credentials through its own export path would itself violate Art. 7, not fulfill Art. 9. Making the exclusion structural (no field exists) rather than a discipline convention (a field that call sites must remember to blank out) closes the class of mistake where a future edit adds a field back "for completeness" without re-deriving why it was excluded in the first place.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 9 (export — every other persisted field is genuinely exportable; this one is deliberately not, and the reason is documented rather than the exclusion being silent), Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — an export must not itself become a live-credential leak vector), Art. 8 (privacy — session_token's documented purpose in DATA_MANIFEST.md is scoped to being the bearer credential itself, not archival data).

**Made by:** Session / Request Authentication capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-17 — Session / Request Authentication: implementation complete, ready for merge-gate review

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/sessionauth` implements all seven `SessionAuthService` RPCs against the frozen contract, with `Mount(r chi.Router, svc *Service)` (matching Audit's chi.Router-accepting signature, per this capability's spawn brief) left uncalled from `services/api/main.go` — per the brief and the standing gate recorded 2026-07-16/2026-07-17, wiring this capability (and the three it unblocks) onto a real network path remains a separate, later act requiring its own fresh Security Steward gate. `go build ./...` and `go test ./...` (whole `services/api` module) both pass, including the two required charter-driven proof tests beyond ordinary coverage: a concurrent-replay test (25 goroutines racing the identical captured `IssueSession` request; exactly 1 winner) and a revoked-device test (fresh nonce, otherwise-valid signature, device resolver reports not-found — `ErrDeviceNotBound`, session never issued). `DATA_MANIFEST.md` documents all eight persisted/protocol fields plus the non-persisted `failureTracker` counter. A pre-existing, unrelated failure was observed once in `internal/storage`'s `TestDeleteBlob_CryptoShredPath` on an initial full-suite run and did not reproduce on a second run in the same session — flagged here as a possible flake in a capability this engineer never touched, not a regression introduced by this work (confirmed via `git status`: `services/api/internal/storage` was untracked/pre-existing before this task began).

**Rationale:** Recording the full implementation pass in one entry per the "log every non-trivial choice" convention, and flagging the observed-then-non-reproducing storage test result explicitly rather than silently ignoring it, per Art. 15 (no hidden state, including "a test flaked and I said nothing").

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 1 (ownership — the only way to obtain a session is a fresh device signature, verified end to end), Art. 5 (explainability — IssueSession/RevokeSession/RevokeAllSessions audited per charter §4, routine ValidateSession successes deliberately not), Art. 7 (revocation is a real, immediate, server-side deletion), Art. 8 (DATA_MANIFEST.md complete, mechanical check passes), Art. 9 (ExportSessions + RevokeAllSessions, export mechanically verified per-type and per-bundle), Art. 10 (no direct import of any sibling capability's internal package; DI throughout).

**Made by:** Session / Request Authentication capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-17 — Storage: hand-mirrored types, not buf-generated

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/storage/types.go` hand-mirrors `storage.proto`'s messages field-for-field, the same pattern Identity/Permissions/Audit/Cryptography & Keys used, rather than importing `buf generate`'s output (`packages/contracts/README.md`'s workflow step 4, which names Storage as one of the capabilities expected to use generated types going forward).

**Rationale:** `buf generate`'s Go/TS/Python codegen (`packages/contracts/buf.gen.yaml`) uses Buf's remote plugin execution, which this capability-engineer's implementation environment could reach only by first provisioning a `buf` CLI (not present) and depending on that remote execution path succeeding and being authorized for this environment — an unnecessary source of failure for a decision the charter itself says is the engineer's choice ("your choice, both are fine, but if you use generated types say so"). Hand-mirroring is a known-working pattern with four prior capabilities as precedent (and, as of this same implementation wave, a fifth — Session / Request Authentication independently made the identical choice, see its 2026-07-17 "hand-mirrored types, not generated" entry above, for a related but distinct reason: its expected `gen/go/ascend/sessionauth` directory did not exist in the working tree either), keeps this capability's implementation independent of remote codegen availability, and is trivially swappable later once codegen is exercised routinely in this environment. No functional difference to any consumer: the wire shape mirrored here is field-for-field identical to `storage.proto`.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (modularity — the frozen `.proto` remains the source of truth this mirror is checked against; no capability-invented field was added).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-17 — Storage backend: local filesystem, two named policies, real physical delete

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/storage/backend.go` defines a `Backend` interface (`Store`/`Retrieve`/`Delete`/`SupportsPhysicalDelete`/`Location`) and one concrete implementation, `FilesystemBackend`, storing each blob as one file under a root directory (`DefaultDataDir`, computed relative to the package's own source location via `runtime.Caller`, not the process's working directory — so behavior is identical regardless of where the binary is launched from). `services/api/internal/storage/default.go`'s `NewFilesystemService` (the production-shaped constructor, not wired into `main.go` — see the standing rule below) registers **two** named policies, `local-default` and `local-secondary`, both backed by real `FilesystemBackend` instances rooted at different subdirectories of `.data/` (gitignored). `ListStoragePolicies` only ever advertises policies actually registered on a `Service` instance, each backed by a real, working `Backend` (enforced at `NewService` construction time, not left as a runtime possibility) — so it never advertises a location choice this instance cannot currently honor (charter §5/§6).

**Rationale:** No S3-compatible object store is deployed anywhere in this environment (per this capability's spawn brief), and a real local filesystem is the simplest backend that genuinely, physically supports true byte-level erasure — the "primary, preferred" `DeleteBlob` path the charter requires implementing for real, not simulated (`TestFilesystemBackend_StoreRetrieveDelete` asserts the file is gone from disk via `os.Stat`, not merely unreachable through this package's API). Two named policies (rather than one) were chosen specifically so `MoveBlob`'s atomicity/no-dual-copy guarantee could be exercised and tested against a **real** backend pair, not only a fake one (`TestNewFilesystemService_EndToEnd` moves a blob between them over real disk I/O) — both are genuinely, currently working local-filesystem locations, so advertising both is honest, not aspirational. `Backend` is a narrow interface specifically so a future S3-compatible (or self-hosted) implementation is a drop-in swap with no change to `Service`, `Store`, or any RPC handler, per charter §7's explicit constraint that the policy abstraction must not privilege one backend.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 1 (deletion must be real — physical delete implemented and tested against a real filesystem, not simulated), Art. 15 (no data in an unqueryable location — `Location()` and `GetStorageLocation` are backed by a real, inspectable path), Art. 10 (modularity — `Backend` is the swap seam for a future object-storage implementation).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-17 — Storage wrapping-key construction: AES-256-GCM (stdlib), per-blob keys, never persisted or exported

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/storage/wrap.go` implements the outer, Storage-owned wrapping envelope (resolved design per the 2026-07-17 "Storage interface frozen; crypto-shred fallback resolved..." entry above) using Go's standard library `crypto/aes` + `crypto/cipher` (AES-256-GCM), not `golang.org/x/crypto/chacha20poly1305`. Every blob gets its own freshly generated 256-bit key from `crypto/rand` (`generateWrappingKey`) — never one shared, service-wide wrapping key. Keys are held only in `Store`'s `wrappingKeys` map (`store.go`), a structure deliberately separate from the exported/persisted `BlobRecord` type, and are zeroed (`zeroBytes`) before being dropped on destruction.

`TestDeleteBlob_CryptoShredPath` (`service_test.go`) proves the guarantee directly rather than asserting it: it captures the wrapping key via a test-only accessor and independently decrypts the raw sealed bytes sitting in the fake backend *before* deletion (proving the key/ciphertext relationship is real); calls `DeleteBlob` against a backend whose `SupportsPhysicalDelete()` is `false`; then asserts the key is genuinely absent from the store afterward, the sealed bytes are still physically present in that backend (this is the whole premise of needing the fallback), and that opening those still-present bytes with the correct key's own length but zeroed content fails. Getting this test to actually exercise the fallback required fixing `fakes_test.go`'s `fakeBackend.Delete`, which originally deleted its in-memory bytes unconditionally regardless of `SupportsPhysicalDelete` — a real bug in the test double (not the production code), since it made the crypto-shred branch's "bytes may still physically remain" premise untestable. **This is very likely the same failure the Session / Request Authentication capability-engineer observed and logged as a possible flake in its own 2026-07-17 "implementation complete" entry** (an `internal/storage` failure seen once on a full-suite run, not reproducing on a second run) — the timing lines up with this fix landing mid-session; recorded here to set the record straight: it was a real, since-fixed bug in this package's own test fake, not an intermittent flake, and not present in the merged result.

**Rationale:** Stdlib AES-GCM was chosen over `chacha20poly1305` (this capability's spawn brief explicitly allowed either) specifically to avoid adding `golang.org/x/crypto` as a new `go.mod` dependency for a purely backend-internal, non-end-to-end concern — `services/api`'s existing dependency set (`chi`, `grpc`, `protobuf`) has no crypto library dependency yet, and AES-GCM is sufficient for an operational, non-network-facing envelope (this key is never transmitted or exposed to a network peer, and each key seals exactly one blob, once, so XChaCha20's extended-nonce motivation — see Cryptography & Keys' 2026-07-16 rationale — does not apply here). Per-blob (rather than one global) keys were chosen so that destroying one blob's key never affects any other blob — a global key would mean the first crypto-shred delete on any blob under a non-physical-delete backend would either have to shred every other blob sharing that key too, or never actually be destroyed at all. Keeping keys in a map structurally separate from `BlobRecord` (rather than a struct field with a `json:"-"` tag) is defense in depth: a future change to `BlobRecord`'s export path cannot accidentally leak a wrapping key merely by forgetting a tag, because the key was never reachable from that type at all.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — but this key is explicitly NOT end-to-end content encryption and must never be described to a user as a second layer of it; documented in `wrap.go`'s doc comment, `DATA_MANIFEST.md`, and here), Art. 15 (minimize hidden state — the wrapping key's existence, purpose, and non-end-to-end status are documented, not left implicit; the test-fake bug and its fix are also documented here rather than left as an unexplained coincidence with another capability's log entry), Art. 8 (privacy — the key itself is explicitly excluded from `DATA_MANIFEST.md`'s collected-fields list with a stated reason, not silently omitted).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-17 — Storage: CheckPermission action-name convention, extended to MoveBlob/DeleteBlob beyond the charter's literal text

**Decision:** `RetrieveBlob`, `MoveBlob`, and `DeleteBlob` all call `PermissionChecker.CheckPermission(requesting_subject, action, "blob", blob_ref)` before taking effect, using `resourceType = "blob"` and one of three action constants (`types.go`): `ActionRetrieveBlob = "storage.retrieve_blob"`, `ActionMoveBlob = "storage.move_blob"`, `ActionDeleteBlob = "storage.delete_blob"`. This capability's spawn brief and the charter's own §3 text explicitly require the permission-check gate only for `RetrieveBlob` ("`RetrieveBlob` must call ... `CheckPermission` ... before returning data"); gating `MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob` the same way goes beyond that literal text.

**Rationale:** Both `MoveBlobRequest` and `DeleteBlobRequest` carry a `requesting_subject` field in the frozen contract — the same field `RetrieveBlobRequest` carries specifically so it can be permission-checked — and both are irreversible or user-visible-consequential actions (an unauthorized `DeleteBlob` would let anyone who merely knows a `blob_ref` permanently destroy someone else's data; an unauthorized `MoveBlob` would let anyone silently relocate it). Leaving them ungated while gating only `RetrieveBlob` would be an internal inconsistency this capability-engineer judged indefensible under Art. 7 ("security must never reduce ownership") given the contract already carries the exact field needed to gate them the same way, at no cost to any legitimate caller and no change to the frozen wire contract (the gate is an internal authorization check, not a new RPC field). This is flagged explicitly, rather than silently done, because it is a deliberate reading of intent beyond the literal charter text — if the Chief Architect judges this incorrect (e.g. if some composition layer needs `MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob` callable without a corresponding Permissions grant existing yet), it is a one-line reversion in `service.go`, not a contract change.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership), Art. 1 (ownership — only the owner, or someone the owner granted access to, may relocate or destroy their data), Art. 10 (the gate is enforced via the same injected `PermissionChecker` interface already used for `RetrieveBlob`, no new cross-capability dependency).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer. Flagged for Chief Architect awareness as a judgment call beyond the charter's literal text, not a contract change.

---

### 2026-07-17 — Storage: StoreBlob rolls back on audit-emit failure, diverging from the Permissions/Identity precedent

**Decision:** `StoreBlob` (`service.go`) is the one mutation in this capability that does NOT follow Permissions'/Identity's established "audit emit failed after a mutation already landed → return an error, but leave the mutation committed" pattern (see e.g. `internal/permissions/service.go`'s `GrantPermission`/`RevokePermission`, and this log's wave-2 entries). Instead, if the audit emit fails after `StoreBlob` has already written the sealed bytes to a `Backend` and recorded a `BlobRecord`, it deletes the backend write and the metadata record (and destroys the just-generated wrapping key) before returning the error — a true rollback, not merely a loud failure. `MoveBlob` and `DeleteBlob` both keep the original precedent (mutation stands, error surfaces loudly) — this divergence is specific to `StoreBlob`.

**Rationale:** `StoreBlobResponse.blob_ref` is the ONLY handle its owner will ever receive to a newly stored blob — there is no `List`/`Query` RPC in this contract that could later recover an orphaned `blob_ref` the caller never got. If the audit emit fails and the write is left committed anyway (matching the Permissions/Identity precedent), the result is a blob that genuinely, permanently exists, consumes storage, and is attributed to a real owner, but that owner has no way to ever learn its `blob_ref`, retrieve it, move it, or delete it — a strictly worse Art. 15 hidden-state hazard than the cases the existing precedent tolerates (a denied/failed Permissions grant, for instance, has no such "only ever reachable via this one response" property; the caller already knows what they attempted and can retry). `MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob` don't have this problem — their blob remains reachable via the same, already-known `blob_ref` regardless of whether the audit emit for that specific call succeeded — so they correctly keep the original, less destructive precedent.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 15 (minimize hidden state — an unreachable, un-audited blob would be exactly the kind of hidden state this article forbids), Art. 5 (explainability — every important action, including a rolled-back one, must be discoverable; the rollback itself is not separately audited because the mutation it undoes was never durably observable to begin with), Art. 1 (ownership — a blob the owner can never reach is not really theirs in any usable sense).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.

Notably: Constitution Warden confirmed the engineer's decision to decline `check-modularity.sh`'s `internal/audit` direct-import exemption (using the same small-local-interface DI pattern as every cross-capability dependency instead) and recommended this become the standard going forward, not an optional flourish, for any future capability. It does not, by itself, lift the `main.go` wiring gate; that remains a separate act requiring its own fresh Security Steward review per the existing standing rule.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 17 (the precondition this capability exists to satisfy for three already-shipped capabilities is now real), Art. 5, Art. 10 (the DI-discipline recommendation above).

### 2026-07-17 — Storage: implementation complete, ready for merge-gate review

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/storage` implements all seven `StorageService` RPCs against the frozen contract: `StoreBlob`, `RetrieveBlob` (permission-gated per charter §3), `MoveBlob` (atomic, no-dual-copy, tested in both the success and the old-copy-delete-fails-and-rolls-back directions), `DeleteBlob` (both the physical-delete and crypto-shred paths implemented and independently proven, not merely asserted — see the wrapping-key entry above), `GetStorageLocation` (answerable for live and deleted blobs alike), `ListStoragePolicies` (only ever advertises currently-honored policies), and `ExportAllBlobs` (versioned, self-describing, bundles live content and honest deleted-blob tombstones). `Mount(r chi.Router, svc *Service)` (`http.go`) is implemented and tested (`http_test.go`, via `httptest`) but deliberately left uncalled from `services/api/main.go`, per this capability's spawn brief and the standing rule recorded 2026-07-16/2026-07-17 (Storage's `RetrieveBlob`/`MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob` all take an unauthenticated `requesting_subject` field, the same shape the rule targets — Session / Request Authentication chartering/freezing concurrently with this implementation does not itself change that Storage isn't wired to a real network path yet; that remains a separate later act). `DATA_MANIFEST.md` documents all persisted `BlobRecord` fields, including the tombstone fields, and explicitly documents the wrapping key as excluded with a stated reason.

**Verification:** `gofmt -l ./internal/storage` clean. `go build ./...` and `go vet ./...` clean for the whole `services/api` module. `go test ./...` passes for all five packages currently present (`internal/audit`, `internal/identity`, `internal/permissions`, `internal/sessionauth`, `internal/storage`) — 30 tests in `internal/storage` alone, including the crypto-shred and physical-delete proofs, the atomic-move and rollback-on-delete-failure proofs, and the audit-emit-failure proofs (withhold-on-read, roll-back-on-write). `bash scripts/constitution/check-audit-events.sh`, `check-export-paths.sh`, `check-data-manifests.sh`, and `check-modularity.sh` all report `OK` repo-wide.

**Rationale:** Recording the full implementation pass in one entry per the "log every non-trivial choice" convention, matching the precedent every other capability in this wave (Cryptography & Keys, Identity/Permissions/Audit, Session / Request Authentication) used for its own "implementation complete" entry.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 1 (ownership — location changes and deletions are explicit, owner-attributed actions), Art. 7 (encryption boundary respected — Storage never observes plaintext; the wrapping key is documented as non-end-to-end), Art. 8 (`DATA_MANIFEST.md` complete, mechanical check passes), Art. 9 (`ExportAllBlobs` genuinely usable, versioned, self-describing), Art. 10 (no direct import of any sibling capability's internal package; `PermissionChecker`/`AuditEmitter` DI throughout), Art. 15 (deletion mechanism and location are always inspectable, including for deleted blobs).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-17 — Storage's CheckPermission scope extension confirmed sound; precedent recorded in the charter template

**Decision:** Constitution Warden's merge-gate review confirmed the Storage capability-engineer's self-flagged extension of `CheckPermission` gating to `MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob` (beyond the charter's original text, which named only `RetrieveBlob`) was correct engineering judgment, not scope creep: it strictly increases the access-control guarantee, required no wire-contract change (`requesting_subject` already existed on both request messages in the frozen `.proto`), and was flagged explicitly rather than silently merged. `docs/capabilities/storage.charter.md` §3's Consumes line is amended to state this. A general precedent is now recorded in `docs/capabilities/_TEMPLATE.charter.md` §3, distinguishing this narrow, self-flagging-permitted case (extending an authorization check using a field the frozen contract already carries) from wire-contract extensions (new fields, new RPCs, changed shapes), which always require Chief Architect amendment first — the same distinction that made Crypto's `Sign` addition and Storage's own crypto-shred wrapping-key design Chief Architect calls, not capability-engineer ones.

**Rationale:** Without this distinction stated durably, a future capability-engineer could over-read "Storage extended its own scope and it was fine" as broader license than what actually happened. Recording it in the template — the one document every future charter starts from — puts the boundary where the next engineer will actually see it, not just in a decision-log entry they'd have to know to look for.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (modularity — the wire contract remains the one place cross-capability dependencies are pinned; this precedent protects that, it doesn't erode it), Art. 17 (the extension itself increased ownership, which is exactly the bar for whether a self-flagged judgment call was the right one).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, resolving Constitution Warden's required follow-up on the Storage merge-gate review.

### 2026-07-17 — Storage passes merge gate; capability moves to stable; two contract gaps tracked

**Decision:** Security Steward independently verified the crypto-shred guarantee is real (captured the wrapping key before deletion, proved it correctly decrypts the stored ciphertext, then proved the key is genuinely destroyed afterward and the remaining bytes unrecoverable — not merely asserted), confirmed the wrapping key is structurally unable to leak through export (no field exists to hold it), and independently ruled the `CheckPermission` extension to `MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob` a genuine security improvement rather than scope creep, agreeing with Constitution Warden. `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` moves Storage from `frozen` to `stable`. Two real, non-blocking gaps were found and are tracked, not fixed in this pass: `ExportAllBlobsRequest` and `GetStorageLocationRequest` (frozen contract) carry no caller-identity field at all, so once wired to a live network path, either would let a caller who merely knows a public `identity_ref` (or, for the second, a `blob_ref`) read data or metadata they don't own, with no permission check possible — this is a frozen-contract gap, not an implementation defect (the capability-engineer had no field to gate against and no authority to add one unilaterally). Required before `Mount` is ever wired into `main.go` or any real network perimeter, tracked as a task alongside the already-logged `RevokeDeviceRequest` gap. Separately, Security Steward recommended `DeleteBlob`/`MoveBlob` receive the same concurrency-hardening + `-race`-verified testing that Session/Request Authentication's nonce consumption got, before Storage goes live — also tracked, not blocking.

**Rationale:** Both gaps are real but share the same shape as every other unauthenticated-caller-field gap already tracked platform-wide (2026-07-16, "no request/session authentication capability exists yet," now partially closed by Session / Request Authentication's own merge gate) — nothing is wired to a network path yet, so there's no live exposure today, and fixing these two specific fields now, before the broader wiring work happens, is cheaper than rediscovering them later.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (an unauthenticated bulk-export path is a real ownership risk once exposed, not a hypothetical one), Art. 1 (deletion/crypto-shred guarantee is real, independently verified), Art. 17 (the `CheckPermission` extension increased ownership; recorded, not merely allowed to stand).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, recording the joint Constitution Warden / Security Steward merge-gate outcome.

---

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects: pre-freeze interface amendment closes a missing content-retrieval path

**Decision:** Before freezing File Objects' interface (the last un-frozen Phase 1 capability, now unblocked since Storage/Permissions/Identity/Crypto/Audit are all `stable`), a review of `docs/capabilities/file-objects.charter.md` §3 found the original charter had **no RPC that ever returns a file's actual content bytes back to a caller** — `CreateFileObject`/`CreateVersion` wrote content, `GetFileMetadata`/`GetFileHistory` read metadata/events, but nothing read content back. A file object no capability can ever read is not a usable primitive. §3 is amended to add `GetFileContent(file_object_id, requesting_subject, version_ref?)` (delegates to Storage's `RetrieveBlob`, gated on `CheckPermission`) and `ListVersions(file_object_id, requesting_subject)` (discovers what `version_ref` values exist — meaningless to omit once `GetFileContent` accepts one). Every operation in §3 gained an explicit `requesting_subject` parameter, matching the convention every other capability's frozen contract already uses. §4's Art. 5 obligation was corrected against the *actual* Permissions implementation rather than assumed: `Permissions.CheckPermission` (verified directly in `services/api/internal/permissions/service.go`) has no audit side effect of its own, so a denied read is not automatically audited by Permissions — `GetFileContent` must emit its own audit event on denial. §6's threat model gained an explicit requirement that `CheckPermission` gates every version of a file, not just the current one (a stale-version bypass would otherwise be a real, not theoretical, gap), and a note that `ExportFile` must retrieve content via Storage's already-gated per-blob `RetrieveBlob`, not its bulk `ExportAllBlobs` — which has its own tracked, unrelated caller-identity gap (see the immediately preceding decision-log entry) that File Objects must not inherit.

**Rationale:** This is the same category of gap as Crypto's missing `Sign` RPC and Identity's `BindDevice` replay vulnerability — a charter that read as complete but had a real hole, caught before an engineer built against it rather than after. Catching it now, at freeze time, is the cheapest point; the original charter passed all three guardian gates on 2026-07-06 without anyone (including this org's own guardians) noticing the interface never actually let a file be read back — worth remembering as a general lesson: guardian review catches constitutional/security/UX problems in a stated interface, not omissions from it, so re-reading an old charter against "does this interface actually do the thing it says it does" before freeze remains the Chief Architect's job specifically.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 2 (a file object that can't be read back doesn't meaningfully carry "identity, ownership... lifecycle" — a File Objects capability with no read path fails its own purpose), Art. 5 (the audit-obligation correction), Art. 7 (the all-versions `CheckPermission` gating requirement), Art. 10 (the `ExportFile`/Storage-gap-avoidance requirement).

**Made by:** Chief Architect. All three guardians re-review the amended charter before freeze — see the next decision-log entry for the outcome.

---

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects re-gate: Experience Guardian and Security Steward pass; Constitution Warden finds a real end-to-end composition gap

**Decision:** Experience Guardian initially blocked (§5 didn't commit to a zero-config default for the new read path, and left the `GetFileHistory`/`ListVersions` composition question unstated — the same shape of gap Session/Auth's charter was blocked for), then passed after §5 was amended to commit to default-to-current-version-on-open and one merged history/version timeline with no separate Versions screen. Security Steward passed the amended charter outright on its first re-gate pass, independently confirming (against the real Storage/Permissions implementations, not the charter's prose) that the `GetFileContent` per-version bypass is closed, `ExportFile` genuinely avoids Storage's tracked ungated-bulk-export gap, `SetFilePermissions`' delegation can't become a privilege-escalation side channel, and `DeleteFileObject`'s content-unreachability guarantee holds independent of permission-record state.

**Constitution Warden blocked with a real, load-bearing finding, found by reading the actual code rather than the charter's claims:** `GetFileContent`/`ExportFile` delegate to Storage's `RetrieveBlob`, which performs its **own independent** `CheckPermission` against resource type `"blob"` (`services/api/internal/storage/types.go`, `resourceTypeBlob`/`ActionRetrieveBlob = "storage.retrieve_blob"`) — a separate Permissions resource namespace from anything File Objects checks. Traced end to end: nothing in either capability's contract ever establishes a `"blob"`-level Permissions record (`Storage.StoreBlob` never calls `DefinePolicy` or `GrantPermission` for `"blob"`), so as originally specified, Storage's own check would fail closed on every `RetrieveBlob` call regardless of what File Objects' own permission decision was — the entire read path this amendment exists to add could not have worked. Two bad fixes were explicitly ruled out: an undocumented, engineer-invented blob-grant-bootstrapping scheme (Art. 10 — the charter must own cross-capability composition, not leave it to be discovered mid-implementation), and passing a synthetic/service subject to Storage instead of the true caller (the confused-deputy pattern Permissions' own charter §6 forbids). Also found: the Art. 2 mechanical check (`check-file-objects.sh`) requires a field-name substring match for "History" on any `// ascend:file-object`-marked struct, but the charter's described data model (history lives in Audit) would plausibly fail that check as written; the self-emit-on-denial audit obligation was inconsistently scoped to only `GetFileContent` when its own reasoning applies equally to `ListVersions`/`GetFileMetadata`; and `ExportFile`'s multi-version content bundling wasn't explicitly held to the same per-version gating discipline `GetFileContent` was.

**Resolved:** §3/§4/§6 amended with a full design — File Objects owns exactly one primary Permissions resource, `"file_object"`, with exactly two defined actions (`fileobjects.read`, `fileobjects.write`), and transparently mirrors matching `"blob"`-level grants underneath at `CreateFileObject` (bootstrap), `CreateVersion` (propagate existing grants to new versions), and `SetFilePermissions` (mirror every grant/revoke onto every existing version's blob). This is bookkeeping via Permissions' own `GrantPermission`/`RevokePermission` calls, not a second access-control decision — Storage's own blob-level check remains real defense-in-depth, never disabled or routed around, and the confused-deputy risk is avoided because File Objects always passes the true `requesting_subject` through to Storage, never a synthetic identity. The Art. 2 struct requirement and the audit-scoping/`ExportFile`-per-version gaps are also closed in the same pass (§4, §6). Sent back to Constitution Warden for re-review of this design specifically.

**Rationale:** This is the deepest architectural finding of the whole Phase 1 build so far — not a missing field or an unaudited call, but two already-`stable`, already-guardian-approved capabilities (File Objects' design, Storage's frozen contract) that individually make sense but don't compose without an explicit bridging design. Worth recording as a general lesson alongside the Crypto/Identity/Storage findings: composing two capabilities that each independently gate access on their own resource type is not free — the composing capability must own keeping both resource namespaces in sync, and that ownership must be stated in the charter, not discovered by an implementing engineer.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (modularity — the composition boundary between File Objects and Storage's independent permission checks now has an explicit, charter-owned design), Art. 7 (no confused-deputy shortcut was taken to make the read path "just work"), Art. 1 (a revoke must actually revoke access at every layer that could otherwise still grant it), Art. 2 (the mechanical-check fix).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, resolving Constitution Warden's blocking finding.

---

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects re-gate, round 2: policy-registration gap traced to a pre-existing defect in Storage; ownership-bootstrap hazard closed

**Decision:** Constitution Warden's second pass on the dual-layer permission design confirmed points 2-4 (Art. 2, Art. 5, `ExportFile` gating) as genuinely resolved, but found the design still didn't clear its own first gate: `Permissions.CheckPermission` fails closed on `!hasPolicy(resource_type)` *before* the owner/grant check the design spent its effort establishing — and nothing anywhere calls `Permissions.DefinePolicy` for `"file_object"` or `"blob"` in real service wiring (confirmed by a full-tree grep; only Permissions' own unit tests call it). Tracing this further: **this is a pre-existing, latent defect in Storage's own already-`stable` implementation**, not something File Objects introduced — `Storage.RetrieveBlob`/`MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob` all call `CheckPermission` against `"blob"`, but Storage's own `NewFilesystemService` never registers that resource type's policy, so against a real (non-fake) Permissions instance, every call would fail closed, including for the blob's own owner. Invisible until now because nothing has been wired to a real Permissions instance and every capability's own tests use fakes. Separately, Constitution Warden found a genuine ownership-bootstrap hazard in the mirroring design itself: `CreateVersion`'s grant-mirroring loop, if it used an unspecified/naive grantor identity, could let a non-owner collaborator (holding only `fileobjects.write`) become a *new* blob's permanent, unrevocable Permissions-owner via `bootstrap_owner` semantics (Permissions' `Store` has no owner-transfer mechanism) — a real Art. 1/7 ownership-integrity violation.

**Resolved:** §6 point 0 (new) states each capability registers its own resource type's default policy at its own construction — File Objects registers `"file_object"`, and Storage (separately, tracked as task "Fix Storage: register 'blob' Permissions policy at construction") registers `"blob"`; File Objects' merge gate is stated to depend on the Storage fix landing first, and File Objects must not work around the gap by registering `"blob"` on Storage's behalf (that would be the exact Art. 10 encroachment the finding warned against). §6 point 3 now requires `CreateVersion`'s mirroring loop to grant the file object's actual owner first, deterministically, on every new blob, before mirroring any other subject's grant — closing the bootstrap-ownership hazard. §6 point 4 now requires `SetFilePermissions`' revoke-mirroring loop to fail loudly, not report success, on partial failure. The Storage fix itself is dispatched to a capability-engineer in parallel (this is additive, non-breaking — one `DefinePolicy` call in Storage's constructor plus a test proving it — not a design change to Storage's existing guarantees, so a fast Security Steward confirmation rather than a full re-gate is appropriate once it lands).

**Rationale:** Worth stating plainly: this second finding is the same *class* of lesson as the first (a real gap only surfaces when you trace actual code, not charter prose), but one level deeper — it wasn't caught at Storage's own merge gate because nothing at that point needed the full chain (real Permissions instance, real policy registration, real grant bootstrap) to actually work end-to-end. File Objects is the first capability whose correctness depends on that full chain, which is exactly why chartering it surfaced a defect two capabilities upstream. This is a strong argument for the standing practice (already followed) of re-verifying composition end-to-end at each new capability's charter gate, not just trusting that already-`stable` capabilities compose correctly by virtue of each having individually passed its own gate.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (each capability owns registering its own resource type's default policy — a clean, generalizable boundary rule, not just a one-off fix), Art. 1/Art. 7 (the ownership-bootstrap hazard directly threatened the "owner" concept's integrity), Art. 5 (this entire chain of findings is recorded here specifically so a future capability hitting the same composition pattern doesn't have to rediscover it).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, resolving Constitution Warden's second-round blocking finding; Storage fix dispatched to a capability-engineer in parallel.

---

### 2026-07-17 — Storage: register 'blob' Permissions policy at construction (closing a real fail-closed gap)

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/storage`'s `PermissionChecker` interface (`types.go`) gains a second method, `DefinePolicy(resourceType, defaultRules string) error`, alongside the existing `CheckPermission`. `NewService` (`service.go`) now calls `checker.DefinePolicy(resourceTypeBlob, blobDefaultRules)` once, synchronously, before returning — before any caller of the returned `*Service` can reach a `CheckPermission` call. `blobDefaultRules` is a new constant, `"deny_by_default_owner_and_explicit_grant_only"` (`types.go`), the free-form `default_rules` string this capability registers for its one resource type, `"blob"`. If the `DefinePolicy` call itself fails, `NewService` returns a construction-time error rather than returning a `Service` that would silently proceed unregistered. `NewFilesystemService` (`default.go`) needed no changes — it already delegates to `NewService`, so the fix is a single choke point covering every construction path.

**Rationale:** `Permissions.CheckPermission` (`services/api/internal/permissions/service.go`) fails closed on `!hasPolicy(resource_type)` *before* its owner/grant lookup runs. `RetrieveBlob`/`MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob` all call `CheckPermission` against `resourceType = "blob"`, but nothing in this package (or anywhere in `services/api`'s real service wiring — confirmed by a full-tree grep) ever called `Permissions.DefinePolicy("blob", ...)`. Against a real, non-fake `Permissions.Service`, every one of those calls would therefore be denied, including for a blob's own true owner — a real, load-bearing defect, first traced during File Objects' second charter re-gate (see the immediately preceding decision-log entry) when composing Storage's already-`stable`, already-guardian-approved contract against a real Permissions instance for the first time. It was invisible until now because every existing test in this package (and every other capability's) uses a fake `PermissionChecker`/`Permissions.Service`, none of which replicate the real fail-closed-on-unregistered-type behavior. `NewService` (not `NewFilesystemService` specifically) is the right choke point: every construction path, present and future, passes through it, so registration cannot be bypassed by a future second constructor the way it could be if the call lived only in `NewFilesystemService`. `default_rules`'s value is chosen to document intent honestly and specifically to this capability's actual access model (deny by default; the resource's owner, via Permissions' own bootstrap-owner mechanism, always has access; explicit grants extend access beyond the owner) — consistent with Permissions' own charter §7/`docs/DECISION_LOG.md` "`default_rules` representation" entry, which defers rule-language design to each calling capability and does not itself parse or evaluate this string in the current implementation wave; the actual enforcement remains `CheckPermission`'s existing, entirely unchanged owner/grant logic. No `CheckPermission` action/resource semantics were touched by this fix.

**Testing:** `service_test.go` adds `TestNewService_RegistersBlobPolicyOnConstruction` (asserts `NewService` calls `DefinePolicy` exactly once, with `resourceTypeBlob`/`blobDefaultRules`, via the existing `fakePermissionChecker`, now extended with a recording `DefinePolicy` method) and `TestNewService_PropagatesDefinePolicyFailure` (a failing `DefinePolicy` call surfaces as a construction-time error, not a silently-degraded `Service`). A new `permissions_integration_test.go` adds `realisticPermissionChecker`, a from-scratch, package-local reimplementation of Permissions' actual three-step `CheckPermission` decision (fail closed if unregistered → owner match → active grant → deny) — deliberately *not* a wrapper around the real `internal/permissions.Service`, because doing so (even from a `_test.go` file) trips `scripts/constitution/check-modularity.sh`'s Art. 10 check, which has no test-file exemption for non-shared capability packages (confirmed by actually running the script against a draft version of this test that did import `internal/permissions` — it failed with `ART.10 VIOLATION`, contradicting this fix's dispatch brief, which had assumed test-file imports were exempt; the mechanical check, not the brief, governs). `TestRealisticPermissionChecker_FailsClosedBeforeBlobPolicyRegistered` proves the pre-fix defect directly (an established owner is still denied while `"blob"` is unregistered); `TestNewFilesystemService_RegistersBlobPolicyAgainstRealisticPermissions` proves the fix end to end through the real production constructor (`NewFilesystemService`) and a real gated call (`RetrieveBlob`), confirming both that the owner now succeeds and that a non-owner, non-granted subject is still correctly denied. `go build ./...`, `go test ./...` (all five `services/api` packages, including the pre-existing `internal/permissions` suite, untouched), `gofmt -l ./internal/storage` (clean after this change), and `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` (all six mechanical checks, including `check-modularity.sh`, report `OK`) all pass.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (modularity — the fix is a same-capability construction-time call through this package's own DI interface, no new cross-capability import; the test-approach correction is itself an Art. 10 mechanical-check finding), Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — this fix is what makes a blob's own owner actually able to reach their own data against a real Permissions instance, closing an unintended total-denial defect), Art. 1 (ownership — ownership is meaningless if the owner is unconditionally denied), Art. 5 (the registration failure path fails loudly, as a construction-time error, rather than silently).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer, closing the gap dispatched by the Chief Architect following File Objects' second charter re-gate finding.

---

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects re-gate, round 3: Constitution Warden passes conditionally; two narrow additions made

**Decision:** Constitution Warden's third pass found no factual error in the round-2 design (a first, given the prior two rounds each found one) and passed conditionally on two narrow, non-architectural additions, both now made. (1) §6 point 0 now states explicitly that the dispatched Storage policy-registration fix must go through Storage's existing local `PermissionChecker` DI interface (adding a `DefinePolicy` method to it), not a direct `internal/permissions` import — checked against the actual fix dispatch and confirmed the original instructions already required this, so no re-dispatch was needed. (2) §6 gained a new point 8, stating as a load-bearing invariant (not an incidental interface fact) that `blob_ref` must never be returned through any File Objects RPC response — this is what contains a genuine, traced TOCTOU race between `CreateVersion`'s grant-mirroring snapshot and a concurrent `SetFilePermissions` revoke, where a revoke can fail to reach a version's blob-level grant if that version didn't exist yet when the revoke's `ListVersions` enumeration ran. Routed to Security Steward for explicit sign-off, since Constitution Warden characterized this as a residual access-timing question outside its own remit to close unilaterally.

**Rationale:** Three full rounds on one charter is more guardian cycles than any other capability in this build so far — but each round found a real, distinct, load-bearing defect (missing content-retrieval RPC at all; a composition gap that would have made the new RPCs non-functional; an ownership-bootstrap hazard; a policy-registration gap that turned out to be latent in an already-`stable` capability), not a stylistic nitpick repeated three times. This is exactly what the merge-gate process is for, working as designed under sustained pressure, not a sign the process is too slow.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (the DI-interface requirement on the Storage fix), Art. 15 (the blob_ref-never-exposed invariant is exactly "minimize hidden state" applied honestly — the containment is real, but it must be a stated fact the system depends on, not a silent accident).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, resolving Constitution Warden's third-round conditional pass. Security Steward review requested for the blob_ref invariant/residual race specifically.

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects charter gated; interface frozen

**Decision:** Security Steward's final pass confirmed §6 point 8's rewrite closes all three of its required items (audit-event aggregation bounded to File Objects' own emitted events; Storage errors required to be re-wrapped; Audit's fail-closed cross-subject default reasoned through with a required test), found one small remaining inconsistency (bullet (a) lacked the same explicit test requirement bullets (b)/(c) already had — added), and independently re-verified the dispatched Storage policy-registration fix by reading the code and running `go build`/`go test`/`run-all.sh` directly rather than trusting the report. All three guardians now pass. `docs/capabilities/file-objects.charter.md` §8 updated with the full three-round history for both Constitution Warden and Security Steward. `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` moves File Objects from `gated` to `frozen`; §3's interface is translated into `packages/contracts/proto/ascend/fileobjects/v1/fileobjects.proto` next.

**Rationale:** This charter took the most guardian cycles of any capability in this build (three full rounds each from Constitution Warden and Security Steward) — and every round found a real, load-bearing defect, not a stylistic objection repeated: a missing content-retrieval path entirely; a composition gap that would have made the fix non-functional; an ownership-bootstrap hazard; a policy-registration gap that turned out to be latent in an already-`stable` sibling capability; and finally a concrete data-leak path the containment story hadn't actually closed. Worth recording as the clearest demonstration yet, in this build, of why the charter gate exists before implementation rather than as a final review — every one of these was cheaper to fix as a paragraph of charter text than it would have been as a bug found after a capability-engineer had already built against the flawed version.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 17 (re-asked and re-answered soundly at every round), Art. 5 (the full three-round history is recorded here, not just the final pass, so a future capability hitting the same composition pattern — a capability layering its own Permissions resource on top of another capability's own independent check — has this playbook available rather than having to rediscover it).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, recording the final joint guardian outcome.

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects interface frozen; capability-engineer spawning next

**Decision:** `docs/capabilities/file-objects.charter.md` §3 is translated into `packages/contracts/proto/ascend/fileobjects/v1/fileobjects.proto` and frozen (`buf lint` clean across all seven contracts). Implemented as a normal Go backend service (`services/api/internal/fileobjects`), composing Storage + Permissions + Identity + Audit through their published contracts only. The proto's header comment states explicitly that §6's load-bearing design decisions (dual-layer Permissions resource mirroring, policy-registration ordering, the `blob_ref`-non-exposure invariant) are not obvious from the schema alone and must be read from the charter before implementation begins — this capability's spawn brief will need to carry more of the charter's §6 verbatim than any prior capability's has, given how much of the correctness burden lives in *how* the RPCs are implemented, not just their signatures. `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` moves File Objects from `gated` to `frozen`. **This capability may not merge until Storage's `"blob"`-policy-registration fix (already landed and independently verified, 2026-07-17) is confirmed still in place at implementation time** — no new blocker, just carrying forward the dependency stated in the charter.

**Rationale:** With this freeze, all six Phase 1 foundation capabilities plus Session / Request Authentication have either reached `stable` or, for File Objects, `frozen` with implementation next — completing the originally-chartered Phase 1 set.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (contract is the only interface the engineer builds against — though per the header comment, more of this charter's §6 needs to travel with the spawn brief than usual, since the contract alone underspecifies the implementation), Art. 1 (founder-approved implementation wave continues).

**Made by:** Chief Architect.

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects: DI design, no runtime Identity dependency, in-memory store

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/fileobjects` is implemented as a normal Go backend service (`types.go`, `store.go`, `service.go`, `export.go`, `http.go`, `errors.go`, `idgen.go`), composing Storage, Permissions, and Audit exclusively through three small, capability-owned DI interfaces (`StorageClient`, `PermissionsClient`, `AuditEmitter` — `types.go`), never importing `services/api/internal/{storage,permissions,identity,audit}` directly — the same discipline Session/Request Authentication applied more strictly than `check-modularity.sh` requires (that check exempts `internal/audit`, but this package still only depends on it via a local interface). `AuditEmitter` is injected via a field literally named `audit` so every call site reads `s.audit.Emit(...)`, satisfying `check-audit-events.sh`'s textual match. Although the charter's §3 "Consumes" section lists **Identity** ("owner and history actor references"), this implementation adds **no runtime Go dependency on Identity at all** — `owner`/`requesting_subject`/grant `subject` are treated as opaque identity-reference strings throughout, exactly mirroring Storage's and Permissions' own real implementations (both list Identity as "consumed" in their charters but neither actually resolves or validates identity references at runtime). Persistence for this first implementation wave is an in-memory `Store` (`store.go`) behind a narrow method set, the same precedent Storage/Permissions/Session-Auth established, easy to swap for a real database later without touching `Service`'s public API.

**Rationale:** A charter's "Consumes" list documents which capabilities a consumer's *data model* is conceptually built on, not necessarily which capabilities the implementation must call at runtime — Storage's and Permissions' own already-`stable`, already-guardian-approved implementations already established that "identity references are opaque strings, resolved lazily by whoever needs a display name" is the correct boundary for this build's current wave (no capability has a hard runtime dependency on Identity's resolution API yet). Introducing one here would be new coupling this charter's guardian gates never reviewed. The DI-interface discipline for Storage/Permissions/Audit is what makes this package's own most safety-critical tests (see the CreateVersion ownership-bootstrap entry below) possible to write faithfully without importing another capability's internals into a `_test.go` file, which `check-modularity.sh` treats as a real Art. 10 violation with no test-file exemption (confirmed precedent: Storage's own `permissions_integration_test.go`).

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (modularity — DI interfaces only, no direct imports; and the "no runtime Identity dependency" call is itself a boundary decision, not an oversight), Art. 8 (opaque identity references — no personal-identity attribute is ever resolved or stored by this package).

**Made by:** File Objects capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects: version storage strategy is full copy per version, not delta

**Decision:** Every `CreateVersion` call stores the caller's full, complete content as a brand-new, independent Storage blob (`Service.CreateVersion` → `s.storage.StoreBlob`) — never a delta/diff against a prior version. Charter §7 required this decision to explicitly confirm two properties hold under whichever strategy was chosen: (1) **per-version `CheckPermission` gating is unaffected** — true by construction here, since every version is an independently-addressed, independently blob-level-permission-gated Storage object with no reconstruction step at read time (`GetFileContent`/`ExportFile` call `Storage.RetrieveBlob` once per version, gated exactly like any other blob); (2) **no intermediate delta blob is independently reachable or can be orphaned outside `DeleteFileObject`'s atomic teardown** — trivially true because no intermediate/delta blob exists at all under this strategy; every blob a version's `VersionRecord.BlobRef` points at is a complete, independently retrievable artifact, and `DeleteFileObject`'s teardown loop (`store.go`'s `listVersions` + `storage.DeleteBlob` per version) covers every blob this capability ever created, with nothing else to account for.

**Rationale:** Delta/diff storage would have required either (a) a reconstruction step at `GetFileContent`/`ExportFile` time that walks a chain of prior versions' blobs before returning content — directly complicating the "per-version `CheckPermission` gating is unaffected" property the charter demands be confirmed, since a chain reconstruction would need read access to every ancestor blob, not just the requested version's — or (b) a separate, File-Objects-owned diff/patch storage mechanism entirely outside Storage's own blob abstraction, which charter §4 Art. 10 forbids ("File Objects composes Storage... through their published contracts only; it adds no independent storage... logic of its own"). Full-copy-per-version avoids both problems by construction and keeps every version exactly as independently governable (and independently deletable) as `CreateFileObject`'s own version 1 already was under charter §6's design — no special-cased reconstruction path for GetFileContent to get wrong, and no delta blob that could ever end up orphaned by a partial `DeleteFileObject` failure (there are none to orphan). The storage-efficiency cost of full copies is a legitimate, revisitable future optimization, but not one this implementation wave needed to take on to satisfy the charter.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (modularity — no independent storage mechanism invented), Art. 15 (minimize hidden state — no delta blob exists to become orphaned/hidden), Art. 9 (export — `ExportFile` can retrieve every version's complete content directly, with no reconstruction step that could silently fail or omit an ancestor).

**Made by:** File Objects capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects: grant-cleanup-on-delete revokes both resource namespaces, best-effort

**Decision:** `DeleteFileObject` (`service.go`) does revoke every active grant it can enumerate on both resource namespaces this capability owns: every active grant on `("file_object", file_object_id)` (via `ListGrantsForResource` + `RevokePermission`, grantor always the file's owner) and every active grant on every version's `("blob", blob_ref)`. This cleanup step is **best-effort, not fail-loud** — unlike the content-deletion loop immediately above it (which DOES block marking the file object deleted if any version's `Storage.DeleteBlob` call fails), a failed `RevokePermission` call during grant cleanup is silently ignored (`_ = s.perms.RevokePermission(...)`) and does not prevent `DeleteFileObject` from completing and reporting success.

**Rationale:** Charter §7 explicitly named this as a required, not-charter-blocking decision, and explicitly named the justifying example this implementation adopts: **content deletion already makes any lingering grant record moot**, because by the time grant cleanup runs, every version's blob has already been irreversibly destroyed (physical delete or crypto-shred, Storage's own charter-committed guarantee) — a stale grant record pointing at a `blob_ref` whose content no longer exists poses no residual content-access risk, only a harmless bookkeeping inconsistency in Permissions' own store. Making grant cleanup fail-loud (blocking the delete from completing) would create a strictly worse outcome than skipping it: a caller could correctly destroy all content, yet have `DeleteFileObject` report failure and leave the record un-tombstoned, purely because an unrelated Permissions bookkeeping call failed — exactly backwards from what "the file is genuinely gone" should mean to a user. Attempting the cleanup at all (rather than skipping it entirely) still matters for Permissions' own store hygiene and to prevent `ListGrantsForResource` on a deleted file object from returning stale-looking "active" grants indefinitely — it is done, just not treated as blocking. The content-deletion loop that precedes it remains fail-loud and names the specific failed version(s) in its returned error, per charter §3's "no longer retrievable is a real guarantee" — only the grant-cleanup step is best-effort.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 1 (ownership — content-unreachability is the guarantee that actually matters to the user, and it must not be gate-kept by an unrelated bookkeeping failure), Art. 15 (minimize hidden state — cleanup is still attempted, not silently skipped, even though it isn't blocking), Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — a fail-loud cleanup step here would make deletion *less* reliable, which would itself reduce the user's ownership over "is my data actually gone"), Art. 5 (the content-deletion failure path is fail-loud and names the affected versions explicitly).

**Made by:** File Objects capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects: DeleteFileObject gated by fileobjects.write; no third action invented

**Decision:** `DeleteFileObject` checks `Permissions.CheckPermission(requesting_subject, ActionWrite, "file_object", file_object_id)` — the same action `CreateVersion`/`SetFileMetadata` are gated by — rather than either inventing a third Permissions action (e.g. `fileobjects.delete`) or making an ad hoc local ownership comparison (`requesting_subject == owner`) outside Permissions.

**Rationale:** Charter §6 point 1 states plainly that File Objects defines **exactly two** actions on its `"file_object"` resource, `fileobjects.read` and `fileobjects.write` — inventing a third for this one RPC would silently expand the charter's own stated, guardian-reviewed access-control surface, exactly the kind of scope drift a capability engineer must route back through the Chief Architect for rather than deciding unilaterally. The remaining alternative — a local `requesting_subject == fo.Owner` comparison, bypassing `CheckPermission` entirely — would violate charter §6 point 5 outright ("File Objects never decides allow/deny itself anywhere in this service... every allow/deny comes from a `Permissions.CheckPermission` call"). `fileobjects.write` is the closest-fit, already-defined authority: it already gates every other operation that materially changes the file object's state (new content, changed metadata), and a subject trusted to silently overwrite a file's metadata or add new versions is already trusted with authority proportionate to deleting it. This is logged explicitly, rather than silently assumed, because a future capability-engineer or guardian reviewing this code should be able to see it was a deliberate call within the frozen two-action design, not an accidental gap.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 10 (the frozen contract's own action vocabulary is not expanded unilaterally), Art. 7 (no ad hoc, Permissions-bypassing local authorization check).

**Made by:** File Objects capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects: FileObjectRecord and VersionRecord carry separate Art. 2 / Art. 9 markers

**Decision:** `FileObjectRecord` (`types.go`) is marked `// ascend:file-object` only; `VersionRecord` is marked `// ascend:persisted` only and ships its own `ExportVersionRecord` function plus `version_record_export_test.go`. The two markers are never stacked on the same struct declaration.

**Rationale:** Traced `scripts/constitution/check-file-objects.sh` and `check-export-paths.sh` directly: both scripts compute their target struct declaration as "the line immediately following the marker comment line." Stacking `// ascend:file-object` directly above `// ascend:persisted` directly above `type X struct {` would make the *first* marker's "next line" the *second* marker's comment text, not the struct declaration — silently defeating that check's enforcement (the script would find no `type ... struct` match and skip validation entirely, with no failure surfaced). Reversing the stack order only moves the same problem to the other marker. Rather than rely on a marker ordering that happens to not break anything today but is fragile to a future reordering, `FileObjectRecord` (the Art. 2-obligated struct — identity/owner/history fields) and `VersionRecord` (a genuinely distinct persisted type, independently Art. 9-obligated) are kept as what they actually are: two separate persisted structs, each carrying exactly the one marker that is mechanically meaningful for it. `FileObjectRecord`'s own Art. 9 export obligation is satisfied by `ExportFile`'s bundling (charter §4: "`ExportFile` bundles content, metadata, and history — a user leaving the platform gets a complete, self-contained artifact"), which is the charter's own stated Art. 9 mechanism for the aggregate file object, not a standalone `ExportFileObjectRecord` function.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 2 (files first-class — verified mechanically effective, not just present), Art. 9 (exportability — VersionRecord gets a real, CI-checked export path; FileObjectRecord's is the charter-specified `ExportFile` RPC itself).

**Made by:** File Objects capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-17 — File Objects implementation complete; all charter-mandated tests pass

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/fileobjects` is implemented end to end against the frozen contract (`packages/contracts/proto/ascend/fileobjects/v1/fileobjects.proto`) and charter §6's full dual-layer-mirroring design, exactly as specified across all three prior guardian re-gate rounds — no simplification. Verified the Storage prerequisite (`services/api/internal/storage/service.go`'s `NewService` calling `checker.DefinePolicy(resourceTypeBlob, blobDefaultRules)` at construction) is genuinely in place before writing any code, per this capability's spawn brief. `Service.mu` (a single `sync.Mutex`) serializes `CreateVersion`'s grant-mirroring critical section against `SetFilePermissions`' grant-mirroring critical section and `DeleteFileObject`'s teardown — closing, within this single-process in-memory implementation, more of the TOCTOU race charter §6 point 8's closing paragraph describes as "real... contained" than the charter strictly requires (a distributed, multi-process future implementation would need a different mechanism — e.g. a per-file-object lock/transaction at the Permissions layer itself — to preserve this property, which is out of scope for this wave). `go build ./...` and `go test ./...` (whole `services/api` module, all six packages) pass; `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` (all six mechanical checks) reports `OK`. Test suite includes, per this capability's spawn brief: full happy-path coverage for all ten RPCs; `TestCreateVersion_OwnerGrantedFirst_NonOwnerCollaboratorNeverBecomesBlobOwner` (the charter's most safety-critical invariant, proven against `memPermissions`, a from-scratch reimplementation of Permissions' real bootstrap-owner/active-grant semantics — not a simple allow/deny fake, following Storage's own `permissions_integration_test.go` precedent exactly, since `check-modularity.sh` has no test-file exemption for importing `internal/permissions` directly); `TestSetFilePermissions_PartialMirrorFailure_FailsLoudly`; the three charter §6 point 8 tests (`TestPoint8a...`/`TestPoint8b...`/`TestPoint8c...` in `blob_ref_leak_test.go`); `TestCheckRead_DeniedEmitsSharedAuditEvent` (the §4 Art. 5 read-denial obligation, which `check-audit-events.sh` cannot mechanically verify); and a permission-denied test for every RPC that performs an authorization check (`CreateFileObject` is the one RPC with no such test, since it bootstraps a brand-new resource with nothing yet to check permission against).

**Rationale:** This charter took three full guardian re-gate rounds specifically because its correctness depends on *how* several RPCs are implemented, not just their signatures — the proto header comment says as much explicitly. Implementing to the letter of the wire contract alone, without the dual-layer mirroring design, the owner-first grant ordering, the fail-loud partial-mirror handling, and the blob_ref-non-exposure discipline, would have silently reintroduced every defect the three guardian rounds already found and closed. Recording this summary entry so a future reader of this package's git history or `DECISION_LOG.md` can see, in one place, that the implementation was checked against the charter's specific findings rather than merely against the `.proto` file.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 17 (this capability strengthens Ownership/Transparency/Capability directly — a file object with real identity, versions, permissions, and history is a genuine primitive other features can compose, not a one-off), Art. 2, Art. 5, Art. 8, Art. 9, Art. 10 (all directly implemented and test-verified per the above).

**Made by:** File Objects capability-engineer.
