### 2026-07-17 — File Objects passes merge gate; capability moves to stable; Phase 1 complete

**Decision:** Both Constitution Warden and Security Steward passed the implementation with no required changes, each independently re-verifying every requirement negotiated across the charter's three re-gate rounds directly against the code — not the implementation report. Notably: Security Steward confirmed the ownership-bootstrap-hazard fix using a from-scratch reimplementation of Permissions' real bootstrap semantics (not a trivial allow/deny fake), confirmed the `AuditEmitter` DI interface has no `Query` method at all (a structural, not merely tested, guarantee that `GetFileHistory`/`ExportFile` cannot read Permissions'/Storage's own audit trails), and explicitly ruled that best-effort grant-cleanup-on-delete is the correct design — a fail-loud cleanup step would let an unrelated Permissions-store bookkeeping error mask a successful, irreversible content destruction, which would itself undermine Article 1. Constitution Warden confirmed the Art. 2 mechanical check passes by construction (not accident), the single shared Art. 5 denial-audit call site covers all five read RPCs, and both §7 open questions have substantive, non-placeholder decision-log entries. `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` moves File Objects from `frozen` to `stable`.

**This completes the originally-chartered Phase 1 foundation.** All seven capabilities — Cryptography & Keys, Identity, Permissions, Audit / Explainability, Session / Request Authentication, Storage, File Objects — are now `stable`. None are wired into `services/api/main.go`; that remains gated behind the standing rule requiring a fresh Security Steward review for the specific act of wiring, per the 2026-07-16 "Wave 2 complete" entry.

**Rationale:** File Objects took the most guardian cycles of any capability in this build (three charter rounds, one merge-gate round) and is the clearest demonstration in this project's history of why the charter gate exists before implementation: every one of the real defects it caught — a missing content-retrieval path, a composition gap that would have made the fix non-functional, an ownership-bootstrap hazard, a latent policy-registration gap in an already-`stable` sibling, and a concrete data-leak path — was cheaper to find and fix as charter text than it would have been as a production bug. The process cost more cycles on this one capability than any other; it also caught more real, load-bearing defects than any other. That correlation is the point of the process, not a sign it should be shortened.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 17 (re-affirmed at every stage — genuine ownership mechanism, not feature-shaped work), Art. 1 (the grant-cleanup-on-delete reasoning directly protects "is my data actually gone" from being masked by an unrelated failure), Art. 5 (this full history, across every capability in Phase 1, is recorded in this log specifically so the pattern is available to whoever charters the next capability that composes two independently-gated resources).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, recording the final joint guardian outcome and Phase 1's completion.

### 2026-07-17 — Storage follow-ups: ExportAllBlobs/GetStorageLocation gain requesting_subject; concurrency-hardening dispatched

**Decision:** `packages/contracts/proto/ascend/storage/v1/storage.proto` amended (additive, `buf lint` clean, non-breaking field additions): `GetStorageLocationRequest` and `ExportAllBlobsRequest` both gain `requesting_subject`, closing the two frozen-contract gaps Security Steward flagged at Storage's implementation merge gate. Gating logic decided now, at the contract-amendment stage, rather than left to the capability engineer to invent: `GetStorageLocation` is gated on the same `CheckPermission` action as `RetrieveBlob` (`storage.retrieve_blob`) — no separate, narrower "location" grant, since knowing where a blob lives is a strict subset of what reading its content already implies. `ExportAllBlobs` is **not** per-blob `CheckPermission`-gated at all — it's rejected outright unless `requesting_subject == owner`, since a bulk export of everything an owner has ever stored is a right-to-leave operation (Art. 9), not a shareable read; a collaborator holding a narrower per-blob grant must not be able to use bulk export to obtain blobs beyond what they were individually granted. `docs/capabilities/storage.charter.md` §3/§4/§6 updated to match. Implementation (both gating additions plus the separately-tracked concurrency-hardening of `DeleteBlob`/`MoveBlob` with a `-race`-verified test, matching Session/Request Authentication's nonce-consumption rigor) dispatched to a capability-engineer in one pass, since both touch the same package and neither depends on the other.

**Rationale:** These were the two follow-up items explicitly tracked (not silently dropped) since Storage's original merge gate — closing them now, before Storage is ever considered for network wiring, is exactly the sequencing the merge gate's own notes called for.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 9 (export must remain a genuine right-to-leave operation, not a side channel a collaborator could exploit for over-broad access — hence the owner-only gate, stricter than a per-blob check would be), Art. 7 (location disclosure gated at the same bar as content — no narrower, separately-exploitable grant), Art. 10 (contract amendment made by the Chief Architect, not a capability-engineer self-extension, since these are new wire-contract fields, not an authorization check on an already-present field — per the precedent recorded in the charter template).

**Made by:** Chief Architect.

### 2026-07-17 — Storage: GetStorageLocation/ExportAllBlobs `requesting_subject` gating implemented

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/storage`'s hand-mirrored `GetStorageLocationRequest`/`ExportAllBlobsRequest` (`types.go`) gain `RequestingSubject string`, matching the amended `storage.proto`. `GetStorageLocation` (`service.go`) now requires both `BlobRef` and `RequestingSubject` non-empty, calls `checker.CheckPermission(req.RequestingSubject, ActionRetrieveBlob, resourceTypeBlob, req.BlobRef)` — the exact same action/resource `RetrieveBlob` itself checks — and, on denial, emits a `storage.get_storage_location_denied` audit event (mirroring `RetrieveBlob`/`MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob`'s existing denial-audit pattern) before returning `ErrPermissionDenied`. A successful lookup remains unaudited per call, unchanged from before this fix — the existing rationale (high call volume, matching `CheckPermission`'s own no-per-call-audit precedent) still applies and was not something this fix was asked to revisit. `ExportAllBlobs` (`export.go`) now requires `RequestingSubject` non-empty and rejects outright — `ErrPermissionDenied`, with a `storage.export_all_blobs_denied` audit event (`ruleReference: "not_owner"`) — unless `req.RequestingSubject == req.Owner`; this check never calls `CheckPermission` at all, deliberately (see next paragraph). `http.go`'s `handleGetStorageLocation`/`handleExportAllBlobs` now read `requesting_subject` from the query string alongside the existing fields (this package's HTTP surface remains unmounted from `main.go` per the standing rule, so this is not a live exposure change).

**Testing:** `TestGetStorageLocation_MissingRequestingSubjectRejected`, `TestGetStorageLocation_DeniedForNonGrantedSubject`, `TestGetStorageLocation_AllowedForOwner`, `TestGetStorageLocation_AllowedForGrantedSubject` (`service_test.go`) — the latter three use `realisticPermissionChecker` (`permissions_integration_test.go`, extended with a new `grant(subject, action, resourceType, resourceID)` test helper), not the blanket allow/deny `fakePermissionChecker`, specifically because they need to distinguish "owner", "granted subject", and "neither" for the same `CheckPermission` shape, which a blanket allow/deny fake cannot express — this is the same reasoning that produced `realisticPermissionChecker` for the original blob-policy-registration fix. `TestExportAllBlobs_MissingRequestingSubjectRejected`, `TestExportAllBlobs_AllowedForExactOwner`, and the load-bearing `TestExportAllBlobs_DeniedForNonOwnerEvenWithFullGrantCoverage` (`storage_export_test.go`) — the last of these deliberately wires an allow-everything `fakePermissionChecker` (so a request that mistakenly delegated to `CheckPermission` would incorrectly succeed), stores several blobs under `identity:alice`, and asserts `ExportAllBlobs{Owner: "identity:alice", RequestingSubject: "identity:bob"}` is still denied — additionally asserting `CheckPermission` was called zero times, proving `ExportAllBlobs` never consults per-blob grants at all, not merely that it currently denies non-owners. Every pre-existing call site of these two request types across `service_test.go`/`storage_export_test.go` was updated to supply a matching `RequestingSubject` so existing behavior-under-test is unchanged.

**Rationale:** This is a straightforward implementation of gating logic the Chief Architect had already fully decided at the contract-amendment stage (previous entry) — no new design judgment was exercised here beyond normal engineering (test structure, audit event naming consistent with this package's existing `<verb>_denied`/`ruleReference` conventions). The `ExportAllBlobs`-never-calls-`CheckPermission` assertion in `TestExportAllBlobs_DeniedForNonOwnerEvenWithFullGrantCoverage` is included because "denied for a non-owner" alone would not distinguish a correct owner-only design from a merely-more-restrictive `CheckPermission`-based one that happened to deny in this specific test's setup — the task's own instructions were explicit that full per-blob grant coverage must not be a substitute for ownership, and the strongest way to prove that is to show the grant-checking code path is never even reached.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 9 (export remains owner-only, not merely narrower — proven, not just asserted), Art. 7 (location disclosure gated at the same bar as content, denial audited like every other gated RPC in this package), Art. 5 (every denial — location and export alike — emits an audit event, consistent with `RetrieveBlob`/`MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob`'s existing pattern).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-17 — Storage: concurrency-harden DeleteBlob/MoveBlob (per-blob_ref operation lock)

**Decision:** `Store` (`store.go`) gains `opLocks sync.Map` (keyed by `blob_ref`, values `*sync.Mutex`) and a `lockBlob(blobRef string) func()` method (`sync.Map.LoadOrStore` to get-or-create the mutex, `Lock()`, returns `Unlock`). `DeleteBlob` and `MoveBlob` (`service.go`) each acquire this lock — `unlock := s.store.lockBlob(req.BlobRef); defer unlock()` — immediately after the permission check and before the first `s.store.get(req.BlobRef)` call, holding it across the entire remaining check-then-act sequence (read record, decide, mutate backend(s)/wrapping key, write record, emit audit). This closes the gap Security Steward flagged at Storage's original merge gate: the existing `Store.mu sync.RWMutex` only ever made each individual map read/write atomic in isolation, not the multi-step sequence built from several such calls — two concurrent `DeleteBlob` calls for the same `blob_ref` could previously both observe `Deleted == false` before either wrote the tombstone back, and both would then run the deletion mechanism and report success. Different `blob_ref`s never block each other (the lock is per-key, not a single service-wide mutex), so this does not become a whole-service bottleneck as blob count grows. `RetrieveBlob`, `StoreBlob`, and `GetStorageLocation` were deliberately left unchanged — none of them has a check-then-act sequence whose interleaving could produce an inconsistent result (`RetrieveBlob`/`GetStorageLocation` are pure reads after their gate; `StoreBlob` only ever creates a brand-new, unique `blob_ref`, so no other call can race it for the same key).

**Behavior chosen for concurrent duplicate MoveBlob requests:** "both calls safely serialize and both report success" (not "one succeeds, one fails"). Whichever call acquires the lock first performs the real relocation; the second, once unblocked, re-reads the now-updated record and — via `MoveBlob`'s pre-existing "already at this policy" no-op branch (unrelated to this fix, already in the merged code) — reports success with a real, audited no-op rather than an error. This was chosen because a caller that legitimately retried an ambiguous prior request (the realistic source of "concurrent duplicate requests" in production — e.g. a network timeout followed by a client-side retry) should not need to distinguish "my call performed the move" from "an identical concurrent call already did" to know its data safely ended up at the requested policy; failing the second call would incorrectly signal that the retry itself failed, when the caller's actual goal (the blob is now at `new_policy_id`) was met. `DeleteBlob`, by contrast, correctly returns `ErrBlobAlreadyDeleted` (not success) for the losing call — this is intentional and pre-existing (not changed by this fix): a delete's very premise is "this had not already happened," so unlike a move's idempotent target-state check, a second delete attempt reporting success would misrepresent that the caller's own call was what removed the data.

**Testing (no `-race` available — `gcc` re-confirmed absent in this environment):** `service_concurrency_test.go`, following Security Steward's own precedent for Session/Request Authentication's nonce-consumption proof (mutation-testing a deliberately broken variant in an isolated copy, since `-race` cannot run here), adds two tests, each running `concurrencyTestIterations = 200` independent trials with `raceGOMAXPROCS` forcing `GOMAXPROCS >= 4`:
- `TestDeleteBlob_ConcurrentDuplicateRequests_ExactlyOneSucceeds`: 16 concurrent goroutines call `DeleteBlob` for the same freshly-stored `blob_ref`, released via a closed channel to maximize simultaneous start. Asserts, every iteration: exactly 1 `nil` result and exactly 15 `ErrBlobAlreadyDeleted` results (never more than one success); the fake backend's `Delete` was invoked exactly once (a new `deleteCalls`/`storeCalls` counter pair was added to `fakeBackend`, `fakes_test.go`, specifically so a double-invocation is provable rather than inferred from final map state, which for an idempotent in-memory fake would stay correct even under a real double-call race a production backend might not tolerate); exactly one `storage.delete_blob` audit event and exactly 15 `storage.delete_blob_failed`/`already_deleted` events; and the final `BlobRecord` is a single consistent physical-delete tombstone.
- `TestMoveBlob_ConcurrentDuplicateRequests_SafelySerialized`: 16 concurrent goroutines all call `MoveBlob` for the same `blob_ref` targeting the same `new_policy_id`. Asserts, every iteration: all 16 calls return `nil` (per the "safely serialized, both/all succeed" behavior above); no dual copy and no lost copy across the two backends; the new backend's `Store` and the old backend's `Delete` were each invoked exactly once (proving only one call took the real-move branch, the rest genuinely took the no-op branch rather than each performing — and each being counted for — its own relocation); content is unchanged and still retrievable; and exactly 1 real-move audit event plus exactly 15 no-op audit events.

Both tests pass 100% across 10 repeated full runs against the real, fixed code (2,000 trials each, `count=10`). Both worker counts (starting from 2, raised through 8, settled on 16) and the run-count-10 sweep were chosen empirically, documented in the tests' own doc comments: at 2 concurrent callers, the unguarded `DeleteBlob` check-then-act window (a few in-memory map operations, no I/O) was found to be narrow enough that a deliberately-broken (lock-removed) variant sometimes did NOT exhibit the race within 200 iterations, which would have made the test an unreliable bug-catcher; at 8 workers the broken variant's catch rate was still only about 9 of 10 full 200-iteration runs; at 16 workers (120 pairwise races per iteration instead of 1) the broken variant failed in 9-10 of 10 full runs for `DeleteBlob` and 10 of 10 for `MoveBlob`, while the fixed code passed all 2,000 trials at the same worker count in every run — this is the evidence basis for keeping 16 workers as the committed value, not an arbitrary round number.

**Mutation-testing procedure (matching Security Steward's own precedent exactly, performed manually, not committed to the repo):** the entire `internal/storage` package (all `.go` files, including the exact final test files above) was copied into an isolated scratchpad directory (`<scratchpad>/storage_mutation/services/api/`, alongside a copy of `go.mod`/`go.sum` so the same cached module dependencies resolve) — never the real repository files. In that isolated copy only, the two `unlock := s.store.lockBlob(req.BlobRef); defer unlock()` lines in `DeleteBlob`/`MoveBlob` were deleted (replaced with a comment noting the deliberate removal), reproducing the exact pre-fix code shape. `go test ./internal/storage/... -run 'Concurrent' -count=10` against that broken copy failed reliably (9-10 of 10 runs per test, as above); the identical command against the real, unmodified repository passed 10 of 10 runs (2,000/2,000 trials) for both tests. The scratchpad copy was never used to run `go build`/`go test` against the real module, and no scratchpad file was ever copied back into the repository except the already-finalized `service_concurrency_test.go`/`fakes_test.go`, which were written directly in the real repo first and only mirrored into the scratchpad copy for the mutation-test comparison.

**Rationale:** This closes the concurrency-hardening item Security Steward flagged as required "before this capability is wired to any real network path" (charter §6) — required for that future wiring step, addressed now per the Chief Architect's dispatch alongside the two gating fixes above, since all three touch the same package. The per-`blob_ref` (rather than whole-service) lock granularity was chosen specifically so this fix does not trade a correctness bug for a scalability regression: a single service-wide mutex would have been simpler to reason about but would serialize `DeleteBlob`/`MoveBlob` calls for entirely unrelated blobs, which the charter's threat model does not require and which would become a real bottleneck as this capability's blob count grows past a first implementation wave. `RetrieveBlob`/`StoreBlob`/`GetStorageLocation` were left unlocked after explicit analysis (not merely because the task didn't name them) — extending the lock to operations with no check-then-act race would be unnecessary serialization with no correctness benefit, which this capability-engineer judged out of scope for a fix whose brief was specifically `DeleteBlob`/`MoveBlob`.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — a concurrency bug that could double-delete, double-audit, or corrupt a blob's deletion state would itself be a real Art. 1/15 hazard, not just a code-quality issue), Art. 1 (deletion must mean exactly what it claims — never fewer, never more, than one real deletion per genuine delete), Art. 5 (the concurrent-duplicate-request behavior for both `DeleteBlob` and `MoveBlob`, including which one reports success versus failure for the losing call, is documented here and in code comments, not left as an accidental emergent property), Art. 15 (the lock's existence, scope, and granularity are documented in `store.go`/`service.go`, not a hidden implementation detail).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.

### 2026-07-17 — Storage follow-ups pass scoped guardian confirmation; both tracked items closed

**Decision:** Security Steward's scoped confirmatory review passed both fixes with no required changes, independently verifying rather than trusting each claim: `GetStorageLocation`'s gating traced directly in code (same `CheckPermission` action `RetrieveBlob` uses); `ExportAllBlobs`'s owner-only rejection confirmed to make zero `CheckPermission` calls, with the "denied even with full per-blob grant coverage" test proven to actually exercise that path (asserting the permission checker's call log is empty, not just that the result is a denial); the concurrency fix's mutation-testing proof independently re-run from scratch (10/10 failures against a broken variant, 10/10 passes against the real fix — stronger than the originally claimed 9-10/10). Security Steward also rendered explicit judgment on two design choices, affirming both as correct rather than merely acceptable: owner-only bulk export with no delegation path at all (grant-based delegation on a right-to-leave primitive would itself be a privilege-escalation vector), and `MoveBlob`'s choice to report success to both callers in a race rather than error one of them (both callers' desired end state is genuinely achieved; the no-op case remains discoverable via audit metadata, which is what Art. 5 actually requires). `docs/capabilities/storage.charter.md` updated with the confirmatory guardian row. Both items tracked since Storage's original 2026-07-17 merge gate are now fully resolved — no open guardian findings remain against Storage's current scope.

**Rationale:** This closes the last two open items from Phase 1's build. Every capability in the original charter set, plus the follow-up Session/Request Authentication capability it required, is now `stable` with zero outstanding guardian findings.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 9, Art. 7, Art. 1, Art. 5 (per the specific findings above).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, recording the final guardian confirmation.

### 2026-08-16 — Identity wiring design: real composition, not a rubber-stamped mount

**Decision:** Founder requested wiring Identity end-to-end. Before writing any `main.go` code, traced the actual Go request shapes and found the concrete reason the standing "no wiring without a fresh Security Steward gate" rule exists: `RevokeDeviceRequest`, `ListDevicesRequest`, and `ExportIdentityRequest` (`services/api/internal/identity/types.go`) carry **no caller-identity field at all** — mounted as-is, any caller could revoke any device, list any identity's devices, or export any identity's full record. `CreateIdentity`, `BindDevice`, and `ResolveIdentity` are correctly open by design (bootstrapping an identity that doesn't exist yet, cryptographic signature-based authorization already enforced inside `BindDevice`, and an intentionally public lookup Session/Request Authentication's own charter explicitly relies on) and must stay reachable without a session.

**Scope for this wiring pass:** Identity + Audit + Session / Request Authentication, real (non-fake) instances wired together for the first time via dependency injection at a composition root in `main.go`. Permissions is **not** wired in this pass — `audit.NewService`'s `PermissionChecker` parameter is passed `nil`, which the package's own constructor already defaults to a fail-closed `denyAllChecker{}` (verified in `audit/service.go`, a deliberate, already-tested safe default, not a new gap). This means cross-subject `Audit.Query`/`Explain` will deny everyone but the querying subject's own trail until Permissions is wired in a later pass — narrower than full capability, but not unsafe, and explicitly logged rather than silently deferred.

**Auth design:** rather than gate Identity's entire route surface behind a session (which would break `CreateIdentity` — no session can exist before an identity does — and would wrongly gate `ResolveIdentity`, which must stay public), a small, generic middleware (`requireCallerMatchesIdentity`, built in `main.go` from the real `sessionauth.Service`) validates the caller's bearer session token and rejects unless the validated session's `identity_ref` matches the `{identityRef}` path parameter — applied only to `RevokeDevice`, `ListDevices`, `ExportIdentity`. This requires a small, precise change to `identity.Mount`'s signature (accepting the middleware as a parameter, applied via chi's `r.With(...)` to exactly those three routes) — dispatched to Identity's capability-engineer rather than done unilaterally, since `http.go` is Identity's own package. Identity's package gains no new dependency on Session Auth; it only accepts an opaque `func(http.Handler) http.Handler`, preserving Art. 10 modularity exactly the way every other cross-capability DI seam in this build already works, extended from Go interfaces to HTTP middleware.

**Rationale:** This is the first time any capability in this build is composed with *real* siblings instead of fakes/mocks, and the first time any capability is actually reachable over a network — both are exactly the moments this project's own standing rules were written to slow down for. The gap found here (three routes with literally no caller-identity field) was always going to surface the moment someone tried to wire Identity for real; better now, deliberately, than as a live incident later.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — an unauthenticated revoke/list/export path is a real ownership risk, not hypothetical, the moment it's reachable), Art. 10 (modularity — the middleware seam keeps Identity ignorant of Session Auth's existence, same discipline as every other DI boundary in this build), Art. 1 (founder-directed, first real wiring milestone).

**Made by:** Chief Architect. Scoped fix dispatched to Identity's capability-engineer; full wiring act requires a fresh Security Steward gate before being considered complete, per the standing rule.

---

### 2026-08-16 — Identity wiring, addendum: Audit stays internal-only, not mounted on the network this pass

**Decision:** While preparing `main.go`, found that Audit's own HTTP `Emit` handler (`services/api/internal/audit/http.go`) authenticates the caller via a self-asserted `X-Ascend-Actor` request header — already documented in that file's own comments as "a minimal 'who is calling this endpoint at all' guard," not real authentication. Mounting `/v1/audit` publicly in this pass would let any caller inject a fabricated audit event attributed to any actor, which is precisely the class of gap this wiring effort exists to close, not reintroduce elsewhere. **Scope narrowed accordingly: Audit is wired in this pass only as an internal Go dependency** (via the real `*audit.Service` satisfying `identity.AuditEmitter` directly through the existing type alias, and a small adapter satisfying `sessionauth.AuditEmitter`) — `audit.Mount` is not called from `main.go` this pass. Session/Request Authentication's own HTTP surface is mounted in full: its POST routes (`GetSessionChallenge`, `IssueSession`) are necessarily reachable pre-session by design (equivalent to a login endpoint), and its other routes are already self-scoping by a validated bearer token per that capability's own charter-approved design — no comparable gap found there.

**Rationale:** Consistent with the same discipline applied to Identity itself in this same wiring pass — a capability's implementation being `stable` and guardian-approved is not the same claim as "safe to expose on a real network path unmodified," and each capability's actual HTTP-layer authentication must be checked, not assumed, at the moment it's actually wired.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (an unauthenticated audit-injection path would let anyone forge the platform's own explainability record — arguably worse than a missing check on ordinary data, since it corrupts the mechanism meant to catch corruption), Art. 5 (the audit trail's own integrity is a precondition for Art. 5 meaning anything).

**Made by:** Chief Architect.

---

### 2026-08-16 — Identity: `Mount` accepts caller-supplied middleware, scoped to RevokeDevice/ListDevices/ExportIdentity only

**Decision:** `identity.Mount`'s signature changed from `func(svc *Service) http.Handler` to `func(svc *Service, requireCallerMatchesIdentity func(http.Handler) http.Handler) http.Handler` (`services/api/internal/identity/http.go`). The new parameter is applied via chi's `r.With(requireCallerMatchesIdentity)` to exactly three route registrations — `DELETE /{identityRef}/devices/{deviceId}` (RevokeDevice), `GET /{identityRef}/devices` (ListDevices), `GET /{identityRef}/export` (ExportIdentity) — the three RPCs whose request types (`RevokeDeviceRequest`, `ListDevicesRequest`, `ExportIdentityRequest`, `types.go`) carry no caller-identity field and therefore have no authorization mechanism of their own once reachable over HTTP. `CreateIdentity`, `BindDevice`, and `ResolveIdentity` are left unwrapped, exactly as specified: bootstrapping (no session can exist before the identity does), signature-based `authorization_proof` verification already enforced inside `BindDevice` itself, and `ResolveIdentity`'s intentionally-public design (Session/Request Authentication's own charter relies on being able to resolve a public identity pre-session), respectively. No handler body changed — this is purely the signature change plus three `r.With(...)` registrations. The package gains no new import and no new dependency on `sessionauth` or on any concept of a session; it only invokes an opaque `func(http.Handler) http.Handler` supplied by the caller, per dispatch from the Chief Architect's 2026-08-16 "Identity wiring design" entry above.

**Testing:** `http_test.go` — the pre-existing `TestMount_CreateResolveBindHTTP` now passes a new `passThroughMiddleware` (calls `next.ServeHTTP` unconditionally) into `Mount`, confirming no existing behavior changed. Two new tests use a second test double, `alwaysForbiddenMiddleware` (never calls the wrapped handler, always writes 403): `TestMount_MiddlewareGatesOnlySensitiveRoutes` creates a real identity directly via `svc.CreateIdentity` (bypassing HTTP, since `CreateIdentity` is deliberately unwrapped) and then asserts `GET /{identityRef}/devices`, `GET /{identityRef}/export`, and `DELETE /{identityRef}/devices/{deviceId}` all return 403 — proving the middleware is actually wired into those three routes, not merely accepted as a parameter and ignored. `TestMount_MiddlewareDoesNotAffectUnwrappedRoutes` passes the *same* always-403 middleware into `Mount` and asserts `POST /` (CreateIdentity), `GET /{identityRef}` (ResolveIdentity), and `POST /{identityRef}/devices` (BindDevice) all still succeed (200) — proving the wrapper is genuinely scoped to three routes via `r.With(...)`, not accidentally applied router-wide via `r.Use(...)`, which would have been the natural mistake here.

**Verification:** `go build ./...` and `go test ./...` clean across the whole `services/api` module, including `main.go`/`wiring.go` (already staged by the Chief Architect with a call site — `r.Mount("/v1/identity", identity.Mount(identitySvc, requireCallerMatchesIdentity(sessionAuthSvc, "identityRef")))` — matching this signature exactly, confirming no drift between the dispatched spec and the real integration point). `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` passes all six mechanical checks (Art. 2, 5, 5-TS, 8, 9, 10) with no changes required elsewhere.

**Rationale:** Closes the concrete gap identified in the wiring design entry above: three RPCs with literally no caller-identity field, which would have let any HTTP caller revoke any device, list any identity's devices, or export any identity's full record the moment this capability went live on a real network path. Restricting the fix to a signature change plus `r.With(...)` (rather than, say, adding an actor field to the three request types and checking it inside the handlers) keeps Identity's package free of any session/authentication concept — consistent with Art. 10 and with every other cross-capability DI seam already used in this build (Go interfaces for internal calls, now extended to HTTP middleware for the network boundary).

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — closes an unauthenticated revoke/list/export path before this capability is ever reachable), Art. 10 (modularity — Identity remains fully ignorant of Session/Request Authentication's existence or implementation, depending only on an opaque function signature), Art. 5 (RevokeDevice/ListDevices/ExportIdentity's existing audit emission, unchanged by this fix, remains the explainability record for these operations — this fix only adds the authorization check that must exist alongside it).

**Made by:** Identity capability-engineer.

### 2026-08-16 — Identity network wiring: merge gate findings closed

**Decision:** Both Security Steward and Constitution Warden independently ran the live server themselves (not just read code) and found no auth-bypass, no cross-identity leak, and no audit-forgery path — but both also found the same real defect: `main.go`'s own doc comment claimed an integration test (`main_test.go`) existed and passed, when it didn't. Constitution Warden blocked on this specifically (zero automated coverage of the actual security-critical logic this wiring pass exists to add); Security Steward flagged it as a required, non-blocking follow-up alongside several infrastructure gaps. All are now closed: `services/api/main_test.go` added, driving the real (non-fake) composition root via `httptest` with genuine Ed25519-signed session flows — covering every scenario both guardians hand-verified live (no token, garbage token, valid session against the caller's own identity, valid session against another identity, the three unwrapped routes reachable with no session, `/v1/audit`/`/v1/permissions` unreachable, a forged `BindDevice` signature rejected). `main.go` gained explicit `http.Server` timeouts (`ReadTimeout`/`ReadHeaderTimeout`/`WriteTimeout`/`IdleTimeout`) and a 1 MiB request body cap (`http.MaxBytesReader`, live-tested pre-fix accepting an unbounded 5MB body) — both real Slowloris/resource-exhaustion gaps on a now-network-reachable service, not hypothetical. `wiring.go` gained a note that `internal/audit`'s package-level `Default()`/`SetDefault()` mechanism is unused — every wired capability uses constructor DI instead — so it isn't mistaken for "the way" when the next capability is wired.

**Tracked, not fixed in this pass** (both guardians agreed these don't block this merge, but must be tracked before broader network exposure): rate limiting for `CreateIdentity`/`GetSessionChallenge` (identity- and challenge-flooding are currently unmitigated), and a documented TLS-termination/CORS stance (currently plaintext `ListenAndServe`, safe only under an assumed reverse-proxy that isn't written down anywhere).

**Rationale:** This is the first composition-root wiring pass in the codebase, and both guardians explicitly called it out as the precedent for every capability wired next (Storage, File Objects, Permissions) — worth getting the "how do we verify a wiring pass" answer right now (a real integration test against the real composition root, not a one-off manual probe) rather than let a weaker standard become the template.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (a security-critical code path with no automated regression coverage is an explainability gap in the tooling sense, same category as the mechanical-check bugs found earlier in this build), Art. 7 (the infra hardening gaps are real ownership risks on a now-reachable service, not code-quality nitpicks).

**Made by:** Chief Architect, resolving both guardians' merge-gate findings.

---

### 2026-08-16 — Permissions and Audit wiring design

**Decision:** Founder requested completing the network wiring for Permissions and Audit / Explainability, following Identity's pattern. Traced both packages' actual HTTP layers before touching `main.go`, per the same discipline as the Identity pass, and found real gaps in both — one of them the most severe found in this build so far.

**Audit** (`services/api/internal/audit/http.go`): every route already, deliberately, documents its own gap — caller identity comes from a self-asserted `X-Ascend-Actor` header with a comment explicitly flagging it as "a placeholder, not a real auth mechanism." Fix: a new middleware validates the caller's bearer session token against the real Session/Request Authentication service and **overwrites** the `X-Ascend-Actor` header with the verified identity before the request reaches Audit's existing handlers — Audit's package needs no changes for `Query`/`Explain`/`ExportAuditTrail`, since they already only trust whatever's in that header; the fix is entirely in the composition-root middleware, not Audit's own code. **One exception needs an actual code change**: `Emit`'s request body carries its own `actor` field, independent of the caller header, because the charter's original design anticipated "a backend service emitting on behalf of a user." That pattern is only safe for privileged in-process Go callers (which don't go through this HTTP path at all — they use constructor-injected DI, unaffected by this wiring). For the network-facing endpoint, allowing an arbitrary authenticated caller to `Emit` an event attributed to *any* actor they name would let a compromised or merely misbehaving mobile client forge audit trail entries for other users. Fix (dispatched to Audit's capability-engineer): `handleEmit` must reject unless `req.Actor == <verified caller identity>` — self-attribution only over the network.

**Permissions** (`services/api/internal/permissions/http.go`): `GrantPermission`/`RevokePermission`'s `grantor` field is a plain, unverified request-body value — as written, this is the most severe gap found in this build: anyone could grant or revoke *any* permission by simply naming themselves as an arbitrary grantor in the JSON body, with no signature, session, or identity check of any kind standing in the way. `CheckPermission`'s `subject` and `ListGrantsForSubject`'s `subject` have the same shape of gap, one severity level down (an information-disclosure/enumeration risk — "does X have access to Y" — rather than a direct privilege-escalation one). `ExportPermissions` has the by-now-familiar identity-export pattern already fixed the same way for Identity/Session Auth/Storage. Fix (dispatched to Permissions' capability-engineer): `GrantPermission`/`RevokePermission` reject unless `grantor == <verified caller identity>`; `CheckPermission`/`ListGrantsForSubject` reject unless `subject == <verified caller identity>`; `ExportPermissions` reject unless `identity_ref == <verified caller identity>`.

**Two routes excluded from network exposure this pass, tracked as follow-ups, not silently dropped:**
- `DefinePolicy` — this is a capability's own bootstrapping call to register its resource type's default policy at construction time (the same pattern Identity/Storage/File Objects already use internally). No end user has a legitimate reason to call it directly; excluding it from the network mount entirely rather than inventing a caller-identity model for an operation that was never meant to have external callers.
- `ListGrantsForResource` — has **no caller-identity field in the frozen contract at all** (`ListGrantsForResourceRequest{Resource ResourceRef}`), the same class of gap Storage's `ExportAllBlobs`/`GetStorageLocation` had, requiring an actual wire-contract amendment (not just an HTTP-layer fix) to close properly — and unlike Storage's fix, the right authorization rule here needs a real design decision (does listing a resource's grants require already holding a grant on it? Being its owner? Something else?) that deserves its own pass, not a rushed answer folded into this one. Tracked as an open task; not mounted this pass.

**The other real milestone this wiring pass reaches**: once Permissions' real `*permissions.Service` is constructed and injected as Audit's `PermissionChecker` (replacing the `nil`/fail-closed default from the Identity wiring pass), this is the first time the three-way Identity/Permissions/Audit opaque-reference/lazy-resolution design (`docs/DECISION_LOG.md`, 2026-07-06, "Foundation contracts composition") is exercised for real, end to end, rather than proven only through each capability's own isolated tests against fakes.

**Rationale:** Same reasoning as the Identity pass: each of these capabilities being individually `stable` and merge-gated was never the same claim as "safe to expose on the network as-is" — their own HTTP layers were written honestly (Audit's placeholder is explicitly commented; Permissions' gap was simply never exercised before now, since nothing called it over HTTP until this pass). Catching this now, before mounting, is the same discipline, applied a second time, not a new standard invented for this pass.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (an unauthenticated grant/revoke path is the most direct privilege-escalation risk found in this entire build — this is not a hypothetical), Art. 5 (Audit's own integrity depends on Emit not being forgeable by an arbitrary network caller), Art. 10 (DefinePolicy staying an internal-only, capability-to-capability operation is a modularity boundary, not an oversight), Art. 1 (self-only enforcement across every affected route — a caller only ever acts as themselves).

**Made by:** Chief Architect. Fixes dispatched to both capabilities' engineers; full wiring act requires a fresh Security Steward gate before being considered complete, per the standing rule.

---

### 2026-08-16 — Audit: `handleEmit` requires body `actor` to match verified caller identity

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/audit/http.go`'s `handleEmit` now reads `caller := r.Header.Get(callerHeader)` (unchanged, still the sole "is anyone authenticated at all" guard) and, after decoding the request body, additionally rejects with `403 ErrPermissionDenied` unless `req.Actor == caller`. No change to `svc.Emit`'s signature or logic, and no new import or dependency — the check is a single string comparison added to the existing HTTP-layer guard, matching the existing convention (`ErrPermissionDenied` is the same sentinel `Query`/`Explain`/`ExportAuditTrail` already use for authorization failures, mapped to 403 by the existing `statusForError` switch). `Query`, `Explain`, and `ExportAuditTrail` are untouched — per the Chief Architect's wiring-design dispatch, those three need no package change at all, since composition-root middleware (owned by the Chief Architect, not this package) will overwrite `X-Ascend-Actor` with a session-verified identity before requests reach these handlers, and all three already only trust that header. Also updated `Mount`'s doc comment, which previously described Emit's body `actor` as fully independent of the caller header — that description was accurate for the in-process-only phase but became stale, and misleading, the moment this endpoint became network-reachable.

**Testing:** `http_test.go` gains `TestHTTP_EmitActorMismatchRejected` (caller header `identity:bob`, body `actor: "identity:alice"` → expects 403, then queries as `identity:alice` and asserts zero events were recorded — proving the rejection happens before any state mutation) and `TestHTTP_EmitActorMatchesCallerSucceeds` (caller header and body `actor` both `identity:alice` → expects 201 with a non-empty `event_id`, i.e. unchanged behavior for the legitimate self-attribution case). The pre-existing `TestHTTP_EmitThenQueryThenExplainThenExport`, `TestHTTP_MissingCallerHeaderRejected`, and `TestHTTP_CrossSubjectQueryDeniedByDefault` were not modified and continue to pass unchanged, since none of them touch `Query`/`Explain`/`Export` in a way this fix affects, and the one existing emit call in the happy-path test already used a matching header/body actor.

**Verification:** `go build ./...` and `go test ./...` clean across the whole `services/api` module. `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` passes all six mechanical checks (Art. 2, 5, 5-TS, 8, 9, 10) with no changes required elsewhere.

**Rationale:** Closes the concrete gap the Chief Architect's wiring-design entry identified: the body's self-asserted `actor` field was designed for privileged in-process Go callers acting on a user's behalf, a pattern that is unsafe the instant the same request shape is reachable by an arbitrary authenticated network caller who could otherwise name any identity and forge audit trail entries attributed to someone else. Keeping the fix entirely inside `http.go` (never touching `service.go`) preserves the boundary the rest of this package's HTTP layer already holds — auth/authorization concerns live at the HTTP edge, `Service` stays ignorant of callers versus subjects.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (the audit trail's own trustworthiness — the thing this capability exists to provide — depends on entries not being forgeable by an unprivileged network caller; an audit log an attacker can freely attribute to other users explains nothing), Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership: without this check, a compromised or misbehaving client could attribute actions to a user who never took them, directly undermining that user's ownership of their own record), Art. 10 (the fix stays entirely within Audit's own HTTP layer, touching no other capability's code and adding no new dependency).

**Made by:** Audit capability-engineer.

---

### 2026-08-16 — Permissions: HTTP-layer caller-identity enforcement, and `Mount` gated by composition-root middleware

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/permissions/http.go` gains a `callerHeader = "X-Ascend-Actor"` convention and a `requireCaller` helper, exactly matching `services/api/internal/audit/http.go`'s existing shape: reads the verified caller identity the composition-root session-auth middleware (Chief-Architect-owned, `main.go`/`wiring.go`) leaves in that header, and rejects with `401 ErrMissingCaller` (new sentinel, `errors.go`, this package's first — it previously had none, only inline `errors.New`) if the header is empty. On top of that, four handlers now additionally reject with `403 ErrCallerMismatch` (also new) unless the caller's verified identity matches the specific request field naming who they claim to be: `handleGrantPermission`/`handleRevokePermission` check `req.Grantor == caller` (after decoding the body, before calling `svc.GrantPermission`/`svc.RevokePermission`), `handleCheckPermission` checks `req.Subject == caller`, `handleListGrantsForSubject` checks the `subject` query param against `caller`, and `handleExportPermissions` checks the `identity_ref` query param against `caller`. No change to `service.go` at all — `authorizeGrant`/`authorizeRevoke`/`CheckPermission`'s own escalation-rule logic is untouched; this is purely an additive HTTP-layer guard against a caller impersonating a different identity in the request itself, sitting in front of that existing logic. `handleDefinePolicy` and `handleListGrantsForResource` are untouched (no auth logic added to either), per the Chief Architect's explicit instruction not to address either in this pass.

**Mid-task amendment, folded into the same pass:** the Chief Architect additionally requested `Mount`'s signature change to `func Mount(r chi.Router, svc *Service, requireVerifiedCaller func(http.Handler) http.Handler)`, matching `services/api/internal/identity/http.go`'s established shape for the same problem — `requireVerifiedCaller` is applied via `r.With(requireVerifiedCaller)` to exactly the five routes with a per-handler caller check (`check`, `grants` POST, `revoke`, `grants/subject` GET, `export`). `/policies` (DefinePolicy) and `/grants/resource` (ListGrantsForResource) are now **not registered by `Mount` at all** — removed from the route table entirely rather than left mounted-but-unauthenticated, since neither has a per-handler caller check and leaving them reachable would have defeated the point of excluding them. Their handler functions (`handleDefinePolicy`, `handleListGrantsForResource`) are left in the file, unused by `Mount`, for a future pass that re-introduces them behind their own contract-appropriate guard (`DefinePolicy` stays callable as a direct Go method by internal bootstrapping callers, e.g. Storage's own policy registration; `ListGrantsForResource` needs its own wire-contract amendment before it can have a caller check at all, tracked separately). The per-handler `requireCaller`/mismatch checks described above stay in place even with the middleware also applied — deliberate defense-in-depth, not redundant, in case the middleware is ever accidentally skipped for a route.

**Testing:** new `http_test.go` (this package had none before). A `noopVerifiedCaller` passthrough stands in for the real composition-root middleware in every test, so these tests exercise this package's own `requireCaller`/mismatch checks specifically, independent of whatever real middleware is eventually wired — mirroring how `audit/http_test.go`'s tests set `callerHeader` directly rather than depend on real session auth. Two tests per fixed handler (mismatch → 403, match → same success as before): `TestHTTP_GrantPermission_CallerMismatchRejected`/`CallerMatchSucceeds`, `TestHTTP_RevokePermission_CallerMismatchRejected`/`CallerMatchSucceeds` (the mismatch case additionally re-checks via `svc.CheckPermission` that the grant was never actually revoked, proving the rejection happens before `Service.RevokePermission` runs at all), `TestHTTP_CheckPermission_CallerMismatchRejected`/`CallerMatchSucceeds`, `TestHTTP_ListGrantsForSubject_CallerMismatchRejected`/`CallerMatchSucceeds`, `TestHTTP_ExportPermissions_CallerMismatchRejected`/`CallerMatchSucceeds`. One additional table-driven `TestHTTP_MissingCallerHeaderRejected` covers all five gated routes with no header at all, asserting 401 (not 403) — the distinct "middleware never ran"/"never authenticated" failure mode. No existing test file was modified; `service_test.go`/`permissions_export_test.go` untouched.

**Verification:** `go build ./...` and `go test ./...` clean across the whole `services/api` module. `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` passes all six mechanical checks (Art. 2, 5, 5-TS, 8, 9, 10) with no changes required elsewhere.

**Rationale:** Closes the most severe gap identified in this build's wiring pass (Chief Architect's 2026-08-16 "Permissions and Audit wiring design" entry above): as written pre-fix, any network caller could grant or revoke *any* permission by simply naming themselves as an arbitrary `grantor` in the JSON body, and could enumerate any other identity's permissions/grants/export data by naming them as `subject`/`identity_ref`. Fixing this at the HTTP layer (not `service.go`) keeps the same boundary Audit's equivalent fix and Identity's `Mount`-middleware fix both established: `Service` stays ignorant of network callers versus the domain concepts (grantor/subject/owner) it already reasons about; authorization-against-the-caller is strictly an HTTP-edge concern. Removing `/policies` and `/grants/resource` from `Mount` entirely (rather than leaving them mounted with no check) follows the same "don't leave a nominally-excluded route silently reachable" discipline the amendment specifically called out.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (closes a direct privilege-escalation path — the most severe class of gap found in this build, not hypothetical), Art. 1 (self-only enforcement: a network caller now only ever grants/revokes/checks/lists/exports as themselves), Art. 10 (the fix stays entirely within Permissions' own HTTP layer and its own opaque middleware-function seam, touching no other capability's code and adding no new dependency — same pattern as Identity's and Audit's equivalent fixes), Art. 5 (the audit events `GrantPermission`/`RevokePermission`/`ExportPermissions` already emit remain meaningful only if the caller attributed in them is actually who performed the action, which this fix now guarantees at the network boundary).

**Made by:** Permissions capability-engineer.

## 2026-08-16 — Permissions and Audit network wiring: composition, route-prefix consistency fix, and verification

**Decision:** Completed the composition-root wiring begun in the "Permissions and Audit wiring design" entry above. `services/api/main.go`'s `newRouter()` now constructs Audit and Permissions as real (non-fake) instances and mounts both on the network, alongside Identity and Session/Request Authentication:

- **Circular construction dependency resolved for real.** `audit.NewService` needs a `PermissionChecker`; `permissions.NewService` needs an `AuditEmitter`. Broken via `lateBoundPermissionChecker` (`wiring.go`): a pointer-based adapter constructed empty (`&lateBoundPermissionChecker{}`), passed into `audit.NewService`, then patched (`checker.svc = permsSvc`) immediately after `permissions.NewService` runs — before any request can reach either service. This is the first time the charter-time "opaque reference, lazy resolution" design (2026-07-06, "Foundation contracts composition") has been exercised in real running code rather than as a conceptual placeholder.
- **Audit** mounted via `r.Group` wrapping `audit.Mount(r, auditSvc)` with `requireVerifiedCaller` (its `Mount` signature takes no middleware parameter, unlike Identity/Permissions, so the composition root scopes the middleware externally via `chi`'s `Group`).
- **Permissions** mounted via `permissions.Mount(r, permsSvc, requireVerifiedCaller(sessionAuthSvc))`, exposing 5 of 7 routes as decided in the design entry above (`DefinePolicy`/`ListGrantsForResource` remain excluded).
- **Route-prefix consistency fix (found during wiring, fixed directly — not a security change):** Permissions' `Mount` hardcoded its own routes under bare `/permissions`, inconsistent with Audit's `/v1/audit` and Identity's externally-supplied `/v1/identity` convention. Changed to `/v1/permissions` in `internal/permissions/http.go` (route registration + doc comment) and updated all path literals in `internal/permissions/http_test.go` to match — mechanical rename only, no behavior change, verified by that package's own test suite passing unchanged in substance.
- **`main_test.go` updated:** `TestAuditAndPermissions_NotMountedOnNetwork` (now factually false) replaced with `TestAuditRoutes_GatedAndReachable`, `TestPermissionsRoutes_GatedAndReachable`, and `TestPermissionsExcludedRoutes_NotMounted` — real `httptest`-driven coverage of: no-token rejection, actor/grantor/subject mismatch rejection, genuine self-action success (Emit, Query, Export, Grant, Check, ListGrantsForSubject, Export, Revoke), and a forged `X-Ascend-Actor` header being deleted-then-overwritten by `requireVerifiedCaller` rather than trusted.
- **Full verification, matching the standard set by the Identity pass — actually run, not just described:** `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...`, `go test ./...` (all packages pass), `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` (all six mechanical checks pass), and a live manual walkthrough against the real `go run .` binary (Ed25519-signed identity + session, then Audit Emit/Query/Export and Permissions Grant/Check/List/Export/Revoke, both success and rejection paths, plus the forged-header attempt and both excluded routes) — all behaved exactly as designed.
- **Operational note:** during the live walkthrough, a stale `api.exe` from an earlier manual `go run .` in this same session was found still bound to :8080, silently serving 404s for the new routes while a fresh `go run .` failed to bind and exited. Killed the stale process before re-verifying. Not a code defect, but worth remembering: `go run .`'s failure-to-bind is not obviously fatal-looking in a background shell — always confirm the *current* binary is what's actually listening before trusting a live probe.

**Follow-up gap surfaced (not a defect in this pass, pre-existing Permissions behavior, newly visible now that Permissions is network-reachable):** `CheckPermission` fails closed for any `resource_type` with no registered policy (charter §6, "default-policy gaps: fail closed, always" — see `service.go`'s `hasPolicy` check). Because `DefinePolicy` is excluded from network exposure this pass, a resource type only ever touched via network `GrantPermission` calls (which does not require a registered policy — only `authorizeGrant`'s ownership/delegation rule) can be granted successfully yet still read `Allowed: false` from `CheckPermission` until some in-process Go caller registers that resource type's policy. Confirmed live during manual verification. Tracked as a follow-up alongside the existing `ListGrantsForResource` wire-contract gap — likely resolved by whichever capability owns a given resource type calling `DefinePolicy` in-process at its own startup/bootstrap (e.g. Storage, File Objects), not by exposing `DefinePolicy` on the network.

**Rationale:** Same as the original design entry — completes the three-way Identity/Permissions/Audit composition for real, closes the grantor/subject/actor forgery gaps identified there, and holds this pass to the same verification bar (real integration tests + live manual walkthrough, not code review) established by the Identity wiring pass.

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session).

---

## 2026-08-16 — Permissions and Audit network wiring: merge gate — both guardians PASS

**Decision:** Ran the standing mandatory fresh Security Steward gate (plus Constitution Warden, per the practice established on the Identity pass) on the composition-root act above. Both independently rebuilt (`go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`), independently ran `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`, and independently started the real `go run .` binary and adversarially probed it themselves (Constitution Warden wrote and deleted its own throwaway Go client; Security Steward built a separate one) — neither took the decision log's prose as sufficient, matching the standard this pass explicitly set out to hold itself to.

**Constitution Warden: PASS.** Article 17 asked first and answered yes — this closes a live, non-hypothetical privilege-escalation and audit-forgery gap, a direct ownership increase, not a hedge. Verified self-service grant/check/list/export/revoke live; forged grantor and forged `X-Ascend-Actor` header both correctly 403'd live; `DefinePolicy`/`ListGrantsForResource`/the old bare `/permissions` prefix all confirmed dead (404); audit hash-chain (`prev_hash`) verified intact across identity/session/permissions/audit events in a real export; confirmed neither `audit/http.go` nor `permissions/http.go` imports `sessionauth` (Art. 10 held). Two non-blocking nits for future follow-up: (1) Audit's `ExportAuditTrail` defaults `identity_ref` to the caller when omitted; Permissions' `ExportPermissions` doesn't (403s instead) — an Art. 16 consistency gap between two capabilities wired in the same pass; (2) Audit's actor-mismatch error string ("permission denied for cross-subject access") is slightly misleading for what's actually an `Emit` forgery rejection, not a `Query`/`Explain` cross-subject denial — cosmetic, Experience Guardian's territory.

**Security Steward: PASS.** Verified the `lateBoundPermissionChecker` has no invocation window before `checker.svc` is patched — the patch happens synchronously before `newRouter()` returns, before any request-handling goroutine exists, so this is a structural (Go memory-model, happens-before-via-goroutine-creation) guarantee, not a lucky-timing one, holding even without `-race` available in this sandbox. Live-probed the sharpest forgery attempt directly: a real bearer token plus a literal forged `X-Ascend-Actor` header plus a matching forged body `actor` field, combined — still 403, proving the delete-then-set overwrite genuinely holds under a coordinated attempt, not just each vector tested in isolation. Confirmed via grep that `permissions.Mount` is called exactly once and the two excluded handlers are registered nowhere. Re-read `service.go` line by line and confirmed the `CheckPermission` fail-closed gap has no code path that produces a false **allow** — strictly fail-closed, never fail-open — though noted its blast radius is currently wider than "network-only resource types" might suggest, since neither Storage nor File Objects are wired into `main.go` yet to call `DefinePolicy` in-process either, so the gap currently applies to every resource type in the running server. Grepped the whole repo for stray bare `/permissions` references post-rename — found none (one unrelated `/v1/fileobjects/.../permissions` sub-route is a different, pre-existing path). One test-coverage finding, since fixed (see below): `TestPermissionsRoutes_GatedAndReachable`'s self-grant-then-check subtest asserted only the HTTP status (200), never the response body's `Allowed` value — meaning it would keep passing unchanged even though `Allowed` is actually `false` today, silently masking the known gap's visibility in CI.

**Fix applied post-gate (both guardians marked this non-blocking, but it was cheap and directly improves the honesty-of-test-coverage standard this whole build has been holding itself to):** `main_test.go`'s `SelfGrant_Bootstraps_Then_Check_List_Revoke_Export` subtest now explicitly decodes and asserts `Allowed == false` on that self-grant-then-check flow, with a comment explaining why (the known fail-closed gap) and stating that this assertion is expected to flip to `true`, and must be updated, once `DefinePolicy` is wired for that resource type — turning "the gap is invisible in CI" into "the gap's eventual fix is a required, verifiable test update." Rebuilt/retested clean (`go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`) after the change.

**Outcome:** Both guardians signed off with no blocking findings. Permissions and Audit / Explainability are confirmed network-live per this pass's verification standard — real build/vet/test, real mechanical constitution checks, real live adversarial probing by two independent parties, not review of prose. The Art. 16 export-default inconsistency and the `ErrPermissionDenied` message-clarity nit are tracked as open, non-blocking follow-ups; the `ListGrantsForResource` wire-contract gap and the `CheckPermission` fail-closed-for-unregistered-types gap (both already tracked in `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md`) remain open, now with an explicit note that the latter's blast radius is "every unregistered resource type," not a narrow edge case, until Storage/File Objects are themselves wired into `main.go`.

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session), incorporating Constitution Warden's and Security Steward's independent gate findings.

### 2026-08-16 — Storage and File Objects wiring design

**Decision:** Wire Storage and File Objects onto the network, closing out the last two `Mount`-unwired backend capabilities in `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md`. Both are already `stable`/frozen; both already fail closed on missing caller checks would leave a real gap, since every mutating and read RPC in both capabilities carries a plain, unauthenticated `requesting_subject`/`owner` field in the request body (or, for two Storage GET routes, a query string) — exactly the same shape of gap Permissions/Audit had before their own wiring pass. The fix is the same fix, applied twice more: an HTTP-layer caller-identity check, additive to and never a replacement for each Service's own `CheckPermission`-based authorization, following `internal/permissions/http.go`'s now-established pattern (`callerHeader = "X-Ascend-Actor"`, a verified-caller check per handler, `ErrMissingCaller`/`ErrCallerMismatch` sentinels) rather than inventing a new one.

**Split of work, hub-and-spoke:**
- Two capability-engineers (Storage, File Objects) each add HTTP-layer caller-identity enforcement to their own `http.go`, mirroring the Permissions pattern, entirely within their own package — no `Mount` signature change (matching Audit's precedent: middleware is applied externally, via `r.Group`, at the composition root, not threaded through `Mount`'s parameters).
- Storage: `handleStoreBlob`/`handleExportAllBlobs` check `req.Owner == caller`; `handleRetrieveBlob`/`handleMoveBlob`/`handleDeleteBlob`/`handleGetStorageLocation` check `req.RequestingSubject == caller`. The two query-string-sourced fields (`GetStorageLocation`'s `requesting_subject`, `ExportAllBlobs`'s `owner`) default to `caller` when the query param is omitted, deliberately following Audit's `ExportAuditTrail` default-to-self precedent rather than Permissions' stricter no-default-omitted-is-a-mismatch precedent — this is a deliberate choice to NOT replicate the Art. 16 inconsistency the Permissions/Audit merge gate already flagged as an open, tracked nit between those two capabilities. `handleListStoragePolicies` requires a verified caller present (401 if missing) but has no identity field to compare against — it carries no owner/subject concept.
- File Objects: all ten RPCs check `req.RequestingSubject == caller`; `CreateFileObject` additionally checks `req.Owner == caller` (a network caller may only create a file object owned by themselves — mirrors Storage's own `StoreBlob` decision exactly, and is consistent with `CreateFileObject`'s existing bootstrap-grant logic, which already assumes `Owner` is a real, acting identity).
- Each capability-engineer owns its own `errors.go` additions (`ErrMissingCaller`, `ErrCallerMismatch`) and its own test coverage (`http_test.go` — net new for File Objects, which had none before this pass) mirroring `permissions/http_test.go`'s per-handler mismatch/match/missing-header structure.
- The Chief Architect (this session) then does the composition-root half: constructing real `*storage.Service` (via `NewFilesystemService`) and `*fileobjects.Service` instances in `wiring.go`/`main.go`, wiring File Objects' `StorageClient`/`PermissionsClient`/`AuditEmitter` DI seams to real adapters over the already-constructed `storage.Service`/`permissions.Service`/`audit.Service`, and mounting both under `r.Group(requireVerifiedCaller(...))` exactly like Audit.

**Resolves, as a side effect:** the `CheckPermission` fail-closed-for-unregistered-resource-type gap tracked in `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` since the Permissions/Audit wiring pass — both `storage.NewService` and `fileobjects.NewService` already call `checker.DefinePolicy`/`perms.DefinePolicy` for `"blob"`/`"file_object"` at construction (pre-existing code, confirmed by reading `service.go` in both packages); that in-process registration only actually runs once these constructors are called from the composition root, which this pass does for the first time. No code change needed to close it — it was already correct, just unreached.

**Rationale:** Same as the Permissions/Audit precedent this mirrors — an HTTP-layer identity check, sitting in front of each `Service`'s existing, unchanged authorization logic, closes a real (not hypothetical) forgery/impersonation gap before either capability becomes network-reachable, rather than after.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 17 (both capabilities become real, reachable user capability for the first time — direct ownership increase), Art. 7 (closes a caller-impersonation gap before network exposure, not after), Art. 10 (fix stays within each capability's own HTTP layer; `Mount` signatures unchanged; composition-root DI seams are the only cross-capability code, and only in `wiring.go`, which already holds that responsibility for every other capability), Art. 5 (existing audit events from both capabilities are only meaningful if the attributed actor is actually who performed the action).

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session).

### 2026-08-16 — File Objects: HTTP-layer caller-identity enforcement

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/fileobjects/http.go` gains a `callerHeader = "X-Ascend-Actor"` convention and a `requireCaller` helper, exactly matching `services/api/internal/permissions/http.go`'s existing shape (which itself mirrors `services/api/internal/audit/http.go`): reads the verified caller identity the composition-root session-auth middleware (Chief-Architect-owned, `main.go`/`wiring.go`) leaves in that header, and rejects with `401 ErrMissingCaller` (new sentinel, `errors.go`, this package's first — it previously had none for caller-identity purposes) if the header is empty. On top of that, all ten handlers now additionally reject with `403 ErrCallerMismatch` (also new) unless `req.RequestingSubject == caller` (checked after decoding the body, before calling the corresponding `Service` method); `handleCreateFileObject` additionally requires `req.Owner == caller` — a network caller may only create a file object owned by themselves, mirroring Storage's own `StoreBlob` decision exactly, per the Chief Architect's "Storage and File Objects wiring design" dispatch above. `Mount`'s signature is unchanged (no middleware parameter, unlike Permissions') — per that same dispatch, composition-root session-auth middleware will be applied externally via `r.Group`, matching Audit's precedent, not threaded through `Mount`'s parameters. No change to `service.go`, `store.go`, `export.go`, or any type in `types.go` — `Service`'s own `CheckPermission`-based authorization logic is untouched; this is purely an additive HTTP-layer guard against a caller impersonating a different identity in the request itself, sitting in front of that existing logic.

**Testing:** `http_test.go` is net new for this package (it had no HTTP-layer tests before this pass). Mirrors `permissions/http_test.go`'s structure: a `newHTTPTestServer` that calls `Mount(r, svc)` with no middleware parameter (since this `Mount` never gained one) and a `doJSON` helper that sets `callerHeader` directly on each request, exactly like `audit/http_test.go`'s tests do — not a `noopVerifiedCaller` parameter, which belongs to Permissions' different `Mount` shape only. Covers all ten routes: a `requesting_subject`-mismatch 403 test and a matching-caller success test each (`CreateFileObject` gets a third test for the `owner`-mismatch case specifically), plus one table-driven `TestHTTP_MissingCallerHeaderRejected` asserting 401 across all ten routes. Reuses the package's existing real fakes (`newTestService(t)`, `fakeStorageClient`, `memPermissions`, `fakeAuditEmitter` from `fakes_test.go`) and the existing `owner`/`collaborator`/`stranger` constants and `createTestFileObject` helper from `service_test.go` — no new fakes invented. `TestHTTP_DeleteFileObject_CallerMismatchRejected` additionally asserts the file object was NOT actually deleted (content still retrievable, history still resolvable) after the rejected request, proving the mismatch check runs before `Service.DeleteFileObject` is ever reached, matching the equivalent proof pattern in `permissions/http_test.go`'s revoke-mismatch test.

**Verification:** `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...`, and `go test ./...` all clean from `services/api`; `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` passes all six mechanical checks (Art. 5, Art. 5 TS, Art. 8, Art. 9, Art. 2, Art. 10).

**Rationale:** Closes the same class of gap Permissions/Audit/Storage's equivalent fixes closed, for the last unwired backend capability: as written pre-fix, any network caller reaching this HTTP surface could read, write, export, or delete any file object by simply naming a different `requesting_subject` in the JSON body, and could create a file object attributed to an arbitrary `owner`. Fixing this at the HTTP layer (not `service.go`) keeps the same boundary every prior equivalent fix in this build established: `Service` stays ignorant of network callers versus the domain concepts (`requesting_subject`, `owner`) it already reasons about; authorization-against-the-caller is strictly an HTTP-edge concern, additive to and never a replacement for `Service`'s own `CheckPermission`-based authorization.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (closes a direct impersonation/privilege-escalation path — a network caller can no longer act as, or create content owned by, an identity other than themselves), Art. 1 (self-only enforcement at the network edge: a caller now only ever reads/writes/exports/deletes as themselves, and only ever creates file objects they themselves own), Art. 10 (the fix stays entirely within File Objects' own HTTP layer and reads only the same `callerHeader` convention every other capability's HTTP layer already reads — no new import, no new dependency, `Mount`'s signature unchanged), Art. 5 (this package's existing audit events — `create_file_object`, `create_version`, `set_file_permissions`, `set_file_metadata`, `delete_file_object`, `access_denied` — remain meaningful only if the attributed actor is actually who performed the action, which this fix now guarantees at the network boundary), Art. 2 (File Objects' first-class-file guarantees — identity, owner, history — are only real guarantees once the HTTP surface that reaches them can't be impersonated).

**Made by:** File Objects capability-engineer.

### 2026-08-16 — Storage: HTTP-layer caller-identity enforcement

**Decision:** `services/api/internal/storage/http.go` gains a `callerHeader = "X-Ascend-Actor"` convention and a `requireCaller` helper, exactly matching `services/api/internal/permissions/http.go`'s existing shape (which itself mirrors `services/api/internal/audit/http.go`): reads the verified caller identity the composition-root session-auth middleware (Chief-Architect-owned, `main.go`/`wiring.go`) leaves in that header, and rejects with `401 ErrMissingCaller` (new sentinel, `errors.go`, this package's first for caller-identity purposes — it previously only had domain sentinels) if the header is empty. On top of that, six handlers now additionally reject with `403 ErrCallerMismatch` (also new) unless the caller's verified identity matches the specific request field naming who they claim to be: `handleStoreBlob` checks `req.Owner == caller` (after decoding the body, before calling `svc.StoreBlob`); `handleRetrieveBlob`/`handleMoveBlob`/`handleDeleteBlob` check `req.RequestingSubject == caller`; `handleGetStorageLocation` checks the `requesting_subject` query param against `caller`, defaulting it to `caller` when omitted; `handleExportAllBlobs` checks the `owner` query param against `caller`, likewise defaulting to `caller` when omitted. Both defaults deliberately mirror Audit's `ExportAuditTrail` default-to-self precedent rather than Permissions' stricter no-default precedent, per the Chief Architect's explicit instruction in the "Storage and File Objects wiring design" dispatch above (a deliberate choice not to replicate the Art. 16 inconsistency that dispatch flagged as an open, tracked nit between Audit and Permissions). `handleListStoragePolicies` requires a verified caller present (401 if missing, via `requireCaller`) but performs no identity comparison — it has no owner/subject field to check against, matching the dispatch's explicit instruction. `Mount`'s signature is unchanged (no middleware parameter, unlike Permissions') — per the same dispatch, composition-root session-auth middleware will be applied externally via `r.Group`, matching Audit's precedent, not threaded through `Mount`'s parameters. No change to `service.go`, `store.go`, `wrap.go`, `backend.go`, `default.go`, or any type in `types.go` — `Service`'s own `CheckPermission`-based authorization logic (and `ExportAllBlobs`'s existing `RequestingSubject == Owner` check) is untouched; this is purely an additive HTTP-layer guard against a caller impersonating a different identity in the request itself, sitting in front of that existing logic.

**Testing:** `http_test.go` extended (it already had two `Mount` smoke tests, both updated to set `callerHeader` so they continue to pass unchanged in substance). Mirrors `permissions/http_test.go`'s structure: a `doRequest` helper that sets `callerHeader` directly on each request when non-empty, exactly like `audit/http_test.go`'s and `permissions/http_test.go`'s tests do — no `noopVerifiedCaller` parameter, since this `Mount` never gained one. Covers all seven routes: a mismatch → 403 test and a matching-caller → same-success-as-before test for the six routes with an identity field (`StoreBlob`, `RetrieveBlob`, `MoveBlob`, `DeleteBlob`, `GetStorageLocation`, `ExportAllBlobs`; `GetStorageLocation` and `ExportAllBlobs` each additionally get an omitted-query-param-defaults-to-caller test proving the deliberate default holds), a caller-present-succeeds test for `ListStoragePolicies` (no mismatch case, since it has no identity field), and one table-driven `TestHTTP_MissingCallerHeaderRejected` covering all seven routes with no header at all, asserting 401. Reuses the package's existing real fakes (`newFakeBackend`, `newFakePermissionChecker`, `newFakeAuditEmitter`, `NewService`) via a new `newTestServer(t)` helper that bootstraps one stored blob for the read/move/delete/location/export tests to operate on — no new fakes invented.

**Verification:** `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...`, and `go test ./... -count=1` all clean from `services/api` (run twice, including once with caching disabled, after an unrelated transient filesystem flake in `TestNewFilesystemService_EndToEnd` — a pre-existing real-disk test unrelated to this change — was confirmed non-reproducible by rerunning in isolation and then as part of the full suite again). `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` passes all six mechanical checks (Art. 5, Art. 5 TS, Art. 8, Art. 9, Art. 2, Art. 10).

**Rationale:** Closes the same class of gap Permissions/Audit/File Objects' equivalent fixes closed, for the fifth of five backend capabilities to get this pass: as written pre-fix, any network caller reaching this HTTP surface could store a blob attributed to an arbitrary `owner`, or read/move/delete/locate/export any blob by simply naming a different `requesting_subject`/`owner` in the JSON body or query string. Fixing this at the HTTP layer (not `service.go`) keeps the same boundary every prior equivalent fix in this build established: `Service` stays ignorant of network callers versus the domain concepts (`owner`, `requesting_subject`) it already reasons about; authorization-against-the-caller is strictly an HTTP-edge concern, additive to and never a replacement for `Service`'s own `CheckPermission`-based authorization and `ExportAllBlobs`'s own ownership check.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (closes a direct impersonation/privilege-escalation path — a network caller can no longer store, act on, or bulk-export blobs as an identity other than themselves), Art. 1 (self-only enforcement at the network edge: a caller now only ever stores/retrieves/moves/deletes/locates/exports as themselves), Art. 10 (the fix stays entirely within Storage's own HTTP layer and reads only the same `callerHeader` convention every other capability's HTTP layer already reads — no new import, no new dependency, `Mount`'s signature unchanged), Art. 5 (this package's existing audit events remain meaningful only if the attributed actor is actually who performed the action, which this fix now guarantees at the network boundary), Art. 9 (`ExportAllBlobs` is this capability's right-to-leave path; this fix ensures it can only ever be invoked, at the network edge, by the owner it exports on behalf of).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.
