### 2026-08-16 — Storage: StoreBlob establishes Permissions ownership via GrantPermission (closing a real fail-closed gap found during network wiring)

**Decision:** `StoreBlob` (`services/api/internal/storage/service.go`) now calls the injected `PermissionChecker`'s new `GrantPermission(grantor, subject, action, resourceType, resourceID, scope string) error` method — added to the local `PermissionChecker` interface in `types.go`, alongside a matching `RevokePermission(grantor, subject, action, resourceType, resourceID string) error` — immediately after a blob's bytes are durably persisted to the backend and its `BlobRecord`/wrapping key are written to the in-memory store, but before the existing audit emit. The call is `GrantPermission(req.Owner, req.Owner, ActionRetrieveBlob, resourceTypeBlob, blobRef, scopeOwnerBootstrap)` (`scopeOwnerBootstrap = "full"`, a new constant in `types.go`) — the resource's first-ever grant, which Permissions' own bootstrap-owner rule (`permissions.Service.authorizeGrant`'s `!hasOwner → true, "bootstrap_owner"` branch) turns into `req.Owner` becoming the blob's permanently recorded owner. Because Permissions' owner check (`Store.ownerOf`/`CheckPermission`) is action-independent once a resource has a recorded owner, this single grant is sufficient to also satisfy `RetrieveBlob`/`MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob`'s separately-actioned `CheckPermission` calls later — no need for three separate bootstrap grants. This mirrors File Objects' `CreateFileObject` pattern exactly (`services/api/internal/fileobjects/service.go`, `s.perms.GrantPermission(req.Owner, req.Owner, ..., scopeFull)`), which is how File Objects never hit this gap: it always called `GrantPermission` for every blob it created through Storage, but a bare, direct Storage caller had no equivalent path at all.

**Rollback ordering:** if the new `GrantPermission` call fails, `StoreBlob` rolls back exactly like the existing audit-emit-failure path already does — `policy.Backend.Delete`, `s.store.delete`, `s.store.destroyWrappingKey` — and returns without ever reaching the audit emit (a blob that never received an owner grant must never be reported as successfully stored). If the grant succeeds but the *subsequent* audit emit then fails, the existing rollback now additionally calls `RevokePermission` to undo the just-established grant, matching this package's existing "no dual-copy/no orphaned grant" discipline (the same discipline `MoveBlob`'s dual-copy rollback and `DeleteBlob`'s tombstone-not-silent-loss handling already hold themselves to). `RevokePermission`'s own error is deliberately discarded (best-effort, same discard convention `authorizeGrant`'s denial path and this package's denial-audit calls already use): the store/backend rollback already returns the operation's real, more informative error, and an orphaned grant on a `blob_ref` that was never returned to any caller and matches no `BlobRecord` is inert — nothing can ever ask Permissions about it again — so a failed cleanup here is a harmless leak, not a silent-success hazard, and must not shadow the real error being surfaced. `RevokePermission` was added to the interface (mirroring File Objects' `PermissionsClient.RevokePermission` exactly) rather than left undone, because the alternative — an interface with `GrantPermission` but no matching `RevokePermission` — would have made this one rollback path structurally incapable of the same discipline every other rollback path in this file already has.

**How the gap was found:** by the Chief Architect, while writing real network-wiring integration tests against the true composition root — not by code review of `service.go` in isolation. `StoreBlob`'s own unit tests all used the simplistic always-allow/deny `fakePermissionChecker`, which cannot fail an owner check that was never attempted in the first place; the one test claiming to be "realistic" (`TestNewFilesystemService_RegistersBlobPolicyAgainstRealisticPermissions`, `permissions_integration_test.go`) manually called a test-only `establishOwner` method with no counterpart on the real `PermissionChecker` interface, so it only ever proved "a manually-pre-established owner can retrieve," never that `StoreBlob` itself establishes ownership. This is the same "verify against the real thing, not prose" standard this build has already established once before — the closest precedent is this same file's earlier "Storage: register 'blob' Permissions policy at construction (closing a real fail-closed gap)" entry, where a structurally identical class of defect (a real `CheckPermission` fail-closed path that every existing fake was too simplistic to ever exercise) was likewise only caught by reasoning about the real, unmocked `permissions.Service` behavior rather than trusting the package's own test suite.

**Testing:** `fakes_test.go`'s `fakePermissionChecker` gained controllable `GrantPermission`/`RevokePermission` methods (`grantErr`/`grantCalls`, `revokeErr`/`revokeCalls`), matching its existing style. `permissions_integration_test.go`'s `realisticPermissionChecker` gained a real `GrantPermission` that reimplements `authorizeGrant`'s actual bootstrap-owner/insufficient-privilege decision from scratch (first grantor on an unowned resource becomes its owner; any other grantor is denied) and a `RevokePermission` that removes a single active grant — matching how this file already reimplements `CheckPermission`'s real three-step decision rather than importing `internal/permissions` directly (Art. 10 — `check-modularity.sh` has no test-file exemption for this package). `TestNewFilesystemService_RegistersBlobPolicyAgainstRealisticPermissions` was rewritten to remove its `checker.establishOwner(...)` call entirely: it now proves `StoreBlob` alone — via its own real `GrantPermission` call — establishes `"identity:alice"` as the new blob's owner, then that `RetrieveBlob` succeeds for the owner and is still denied for a stranger. `establishOwner` itself was kept (not removed) because `service_test.go`'s `TestGetStorageLocation_AllowedForOwner`/`_AllowedForGrantedSubject`/`_DeniedForNonGrantedSubject` still use it as plain test setup unrelated to this gap. `service_test.go` also gained `TestStoreBlob_GrantPermissionFailureRollsBack` (grant failure rolls the backend write back and never reaches the audit emit) and stronger assertions in `TestStoreBlob_Success` (asserts the exact `GrantPermission` call shape) and `TestStoreBlob_AuditFailureRollsBack` (asserts the grant is revoked on rollback).

**Verification:** `go build ./internal/storage/...`, `go vet ./internal/storage/...`, and `go test ./internal/storage/... -count=1` all clean, including both `TestNewFilesystemService_RegistersBlobPolicyAgainstRealisticPermissions` (rewritten) and `TestRealisticPermissionChecker_FailsClosedBeforeBlobPolicyRegistered` (unchanged), which both still pass and still mean exactly what their names claim. `go build ./...`/`go vet ./...`/`go test ./... -count=1` from `services/api` fail only at the composition root (`main.go:97`, `storagePermissionCheckerAdapter` does not yet implement the new `GrantPermission` method) — every other package, including every other capability, builds and passes; wiring the real adapter is explicitly the Chief Architect's follow-up, not this capability's. `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` passes all six mechanical checks (Art. 5, Art. 5 TS, Art. 8, Art. 9, Art. 2, Art. 10).

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 1 (a blob's owner must actually be able to retrieve/move/delete the data they stored — ownership without access is not ownership), Art. 5 (the grant establishing ownership is itself a real Permissions state change, audited by Permissions' own `GrantPermission` when the real service is wired in), Art. 15 (an unreachable-by-its-owner blob, or an orphaned grant on a rolled-back blob, is exactly the hidden-state hazard this article forbids), Art. 10 (the fix stays entirely within Storage's own local `PermissionChecker` interface and `service.go`; no new cross-capability import), Art. 17 (a stored blob nobody — not even its creator — can ever retrieve again is a direct loss of ownership, the opposite of this capability's charter).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.

## 2026-08-16 — Storage and File Objects network wiring: composition and verification

**Decision:** Completed the composition-root wiring begun in "Storage and File Objects wiring design" above, after the two capability-engineers' HTTP-layer caller-identity passes and the separately-found-and-fixed "Storage: StoreBlob establishes Permissions ownership via GrantPermission" gap all landed. `services/api/main.go`'s `newRouter()` now constructs Storage and File Objects as real (non-fake) instances and mounts both on the network, alongside Identity, Session/Request Authentication, Permissions, and Audit — every backend capability chartered so far is now network-reachable.

- **Storage** constructed via `storage.NewFilesystemService(storagePermissionCheckerAdapter{...}, storageAuditEmitterAdapter{...})` — the real, production-shaped constructor (two genuine local-filesystem policies), not the bare `NewService`. `storagePermissionCheckerAdapter` (`wiring.go`) forwards `CheckPermission`/`DefinePolicy` to the real `permsSvc`, plus the two new methods (`GrantPermission`/`RevokePermission`) the ownership-bootstrap fix added to Storage's local `PermissionChecker` interface — this adapter update was this session's own work, done after the capability-engineer's fix landed (that fix's own task scope explicitly excluded `wiring.go`).
- **File Objects** constructed via `fileobjects.NewService(fileobjectsStorageClientAdapter{storage: storageSvc}, fileobjectsPermissionsClientAdapter{perms: permsSvc}, fileobjectsAuditEmitterAdapter{...})` — depends directly on the just-constructed `storageSvc`, no circular-dependency mechanics needed here (unlike Audit/Permissions' `lateBoundPermissionChecker`).
- Both constructors return `(svc, error)` — unlike every other capability's `NewService` in this composition root, which returns a bare value — because both can genuinely fail at construction (malformed policy set; `DefinePolicy` call failing). `newRouter()` calls `log.Fatalf` on either error, consistent with how `main()` itself already treats `ListenAndServe`'s error as fatal; no other constructor in this file needed this because none of them can fail.
- **Resolved as a side effect, not a new fix:** the `CheckPermission` fail-closed-for-unregistered-resource-type gap tracked in `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` since the Permissions/Audit wiring pass. Both `storage.NewService` and `fileobjects.NewService` already called `checker.DefinePolicy`/`perms.DefinePolicy` for `"blob"`/`"file_object"` before this pass — that code was already correct, it just had never been reached from a real composition root until now. Confirmed live: `TestStorageRoutes_GatedAndReachable`'s `FullLifecycle_AsOwner_Succeeds` subtest and `TestFileObjectsRoutes_GatedAndReachable`'s equivalent both assert genuine `200`/`201` success on the true owner's first real read/write against the real Permissions instance — unlike Permissions' own `SelfGrant_Bootstraps...` subtest (2026-08-16, "Permissions and Audit network wiring"), which deliberately documents the gap staying open for an arbitrary, never-registered `resource_type`. That subtest is untouched and still correct — `"test_resource"` is not `"blob"` or `"file_object"`, so it is unaffected by either capability's own construction-time registration.
- Both mounted via `r.Group(func(r chi.Router) { r.Use(requireVerifiedCaller(sessionAuthSvc)); storage.Mount(r, storageSvc) })`, and the same shape for File Objects — mirroring Audit's `Mount`-takes-no-middleware-parameter shape exactly (not Permissions' `Mount`-signature-change shape), per the design entry's split of work.
- **`main_test.go` extended:** `TestStorageRoutes_GatedAndReachable` and `TestFileObjectsRoutes_GatedAndReachable`, real `httptest`-driven coverage of: no-token rejection, owner/requesting_subject-mismatch rejection, and a genuine full-lifecycle happy path as the true owner (Storage: store → retrieve → get-location → list-policies → move → export → delete; File Objects: create → get-content → create-version → list-versions → get-history → set-permissions → get-metadata → set-metadata → export → delete), plus one stranger-denial check per capability proving both the HTTP-layer caller check and the underlying real Permissions check independently hold. `GetFileContent`'s success assertion is the direct, real proof the ownership-bootstrap fix works end to end through File Objects' own bootstrap-grant path, not just Storage's direct path.
- **Live verification against the real `go run .` binary** (not only `httptest`): confirmed `/healthz` 200; unauthenticated `POST /storage/blobs` and `POST /file-objects/` both 401; a forged `X-Ascend-Actor` header with no bearer token still 401 (proving the token check gates before the header is ever trusted, matching `requireVerifiedCaller`'s existing delete-then-set discipline); `/v1/permissions/policies` still 404 (regression check, unrelated to this pass, confirming nothing about this wiring accidentally re-exposed an excluded route).
- **Full verification, matching the standard set by every prior wiring pass:** `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...`, `go test ./...` (all packages pass, including the two new integration test functions), `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` (all six mechanical checks pass).

**Rationale:** Same as every prior wiring-pass entry — completes the composition for real, and holds this pass to the same verification bar (real integration tests against the true composition root + live manual probing of the real binary, not code review alone) the capability registry's own standing rule requires for wiring any further capability onto the network.

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session).

## 2026-08-16 — Storage and File Objects network wiring: merge gate — both guardians PASS

**Decision:** Ran the standing mandatory fresh Security Steward gate (plus Constitution Warden, per the practice established on every prior wiring pass) on the composition-root act above. Both independently rebuilt (`go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`), independently ran `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`, and independently started the real `go run .` binary and adversarially probed it themselves with their own throwaway Go clients — neither reused this session's test code or took the decision log's prose as sufficient, matching the standard every prior wiring pass in this build has held itself to.

**Constitution Warden: PASS.** Article 17 asked first and answered yes, unhedged — Storage and File Objects becoming genuinely network-reachable, correctly gated to self-only action, with the owner-cannot-retrieve-their-own-blob bug closed *before* exposure rather than discovered after, is a direct, concrete increase in the user's ownership of their own data (and a direct enabler of Art. 9's export/right-to-leave promise, which was not real until these capabilities were reachable). Independently confirmed all four required properties live: unauthenticated callers 401 on every route (including a forged `X-Ascend-Actor` header with no token); a genuine session for one identity is rejected 403 naming any other identity as owner/requesting_subject anywhere; the true owner's store→retrieve round trip (Storage) and create→get-content round trip (File Objects) both genuinely succeed byte-for-byte through the real, live Permissions instance — the specific gap the ownership-bootstrap fix targets, confirmed actually closed, not merely asserted; the forged-header-plus-genuine-different-token combined attack still 403s, matching `requireVerifiedCaller`'s delete-then-set discipline. Confirmed `permissions.Service.GrantPermission` itself emits its own audit event, substantiating that the bootstrap grant is a real, auditable state change, not a silent side effect (Art. 5). Confirmed Art. 10 holds (neither `http.go` imports anything beyond chi/stdlib; all cross-capability composition stays in `wiring.go`). One non-blocking finding, since fixed: `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md`'s network-exposure status and "Known gap" notes were stale (still describing Storage/File Objects as unwired) — updated above, in this same close-out.

**Security Steward: PASS.** Independently verified the `StoreBlob` rollback ordering in code: a `GrantPermission` failure rolls back the backend store/wrapping key before ever reaching the audit emit (no stored-but-unreachable window); a subsequent audit-emit failure after a successful grant triggers the same rollback plus a best-effort `RevokePermission` (no orphaned-grant/Art. 15 hazard). Confirmed no lock-ordering/deadlock risk — `StoreBlob` never acquires `Store.lockBlob` (a brand-new `blob_ref` cannot be referenced by any concurrent call before this call returns it), and Permissions/Audit/Storage each own separate mutex domains. Live-probed every caller-vs-body mismatch combination across all five identity-bearing Storage routes and both File Objects identity fields, including a genuinely valid session token for a *different* real identity named in the request body (403 every time), and the sharpest coordinated attack (genuine token for bob + forged header claiming alice + body naming alice, all aligned) — still 403. Proved defense-in-depth is real, not an HTTP-edge illusion: bob's own genuinely-matching token and body (`RequestingSubject: bob`, passing the HTTP-layer check) still got denied by the real Permissions instance when bob has no actual grant on alice's blob. Traced the confused-deputy prohibition live through `CreateVersion` specifically (not just `CreateFileObject`): a genuine write-collaborator (bob) creating version 2 does not become the new blob's Permissions grantor/owner — `fo.Owner` (alice) is used unconditionally for that grant, exactly as charter §6 requires, confirmed via real audit-trail attribution (`Actor: bob` on the event, `Owner: alice` on the record throughout). Two non-blocking findings: (1) `internal/fileobjects/http.go`'s `Mount` doc comment was stale, claiming the package "must not be exposed" — since fixed, in this same close-out; (2) one unreproduced `go test ./...` flake observed immediately after a manual `taskkill` on a live server process during review, not reproduced across 5 immediate reruns or a further isolated pass — consistent with the same stale-process/port-contention class of artifact already documented in the Permissions/Audit wiring entry, not a code defect.

**Fixes applied post-gate, both non-blocking per both guardians but cheap and directly closing the honesty-of-documentation standard this build holds itself to:** `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md`'s network-exposure status updated to list Storage and File Objects as live; its "Known gap" note updated to record resolution for `"blob"`/`"file_object"` specifically (the underlying fail-closed-for-*unregistered*-types mechanism is unchanged and correct, only the reachability gap is closed); `internal/fileobjects/http.go`'s `Mount` doc comment corrected to state it is wired, not withheld. Rebuilt/retested clean (`go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`, `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`) after these changes.

**Outcome:** Both guardians signed off with no blocking findings. Storage and File Objects are confirmed network-live per this pass's verification standard — real build/vet/test, real mechanical constitution checks, real live adversarial probing by two independent parties with their own tooling, not review of prose. Every backend capability in `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` is now network-reachable. Remaining open, non-blocking items are the pre-existing ones already tracked in the registry (rate limiting on `CreateIdentity`/`GetSessionChallenge`; documented TLS-termination/CORS stance) plus the Art. 16 export-default inconsistency and `ErrPermissionDenied` message-clarity nit carried over from the Permissions/Audit pass — none introduced or worsened by this pass.

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session), incorporating Constitution Warden's and Security Steward's independent gate findings.

## 2026-08-16 — Audit: PostgresStore, a real Postgres-backed implementation of Store

**Decision:** Added `services/api/internal/audit/postgres_store.go` (`PostgresStore`, `NewPostgresStore(pool *pgxpool.Pool) *PostgresStore`), a second, additive implementation of the frozen `Store` interface (`store.go`) alongside — not replacing — `InMemoryStore`, against the already-approved schema (`internal/platform/migrations/0001_audit_events.up.sql`, see this log's "Database setup: shared platform foundation + Audit on real Postgres" entry). `Service`, `InMemoryStore`, and every existing test in this package are unchanged.

- **`Append(e AuditEvent) (AuditEvent, error)`** replicates `InMemoryStore.Append`'s whole-chain serialization using an unconditional `pg_advisory_xact_lock($1)` — key `appendAdvisoryLockKey = fnv1a64("audit_events_append")`, a fixed, reproducible-from-its-own-name int64 constant — taken as the *first* statement of the transaction, before any read. Deliberately not a `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the tail row: on an empty table that locks nothing, so two concurrent first-ever inserts could both read no rows and both compute `PrevHash=""`, corrupting the chain at genesis. The advisory lock mirrors `InMemoryStore`'s own single mutex exactly and is acceptable coarse locking because this is an audit log's append path, not a hot path. After the lock, it reads the current tail (`SELECT event_id ... ORDER BY seq DESC LIMIT 1`, no rows ⇒ `""`), sets `PrevHash`, computes `EventID` via the exact same unexported `computeEventHash` `hash.go` already defines (not reimplemented), marshals `Metadata` via `encoding/json` into the `JSONB` column, inserts with 9 fully parameterized (`$1..$9`) values, and commits.
- **`Get`/`All`** (both frozen with no error return on `Store`) fold a genuine DB failure into "not found" / empty respectively — the same fail-closed spirit as this package's existing `denyAllChecker` default: a caller sees the same result for "transient DB error" as for "genuinely doesn't exist/empty," never a false positive from stale/partial data. Documented as an accepted limitation of the frozen interface (a charter-amendment question, not something fixable in this file) via comments directly above both methods in `postgres_store.go`.
- **`Query`** builds one parameterized `WHERE` clause (each of the 6 `QueryFilter` fields optional, matching `InMemoryStore.Query`'s empty-string/zero-value-means-unset semantics exactly) and orders `occurred_at_unix DESC, seq DESC` — `seq` (a pure internal ordering column, added by the migration, never returned in any `AuditEvent`) is the deterministic tiebreaker Postgres needs that a stable Go slice sort gets for free.
- **`All`** orders `seq ASC` for true append order, required by `VerifyIntegrity`/`ExportAuditTrail`.
- Every value in every query is bound via `$1..$N`; no `fmt.Sprintf`/string-concatenation into SQL anywhere in this file.

**Test isolation vs. append-only enforcement (a real constraint, not just a design nicety):** `postgres_store_test.go` confirmed live that migration 0001's `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE ON audit_events FROM ascend_app` is genuinely enforced — `DELETE FROM audit_events` as the `ascend_app`/`DATABASE_URL` role fails with `permission denied for table audit_events`. Test cleanup therefore cannot use the same connection the store itself uses; `cleanupEvents` in the test file connects separately via `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` (the admin/migration connection) to `DELETE ... WHERE event_id = ANY($1)` for exactly the rows a test created, in `t.Cleanup`. If `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` isn't set (only `DATABASE_URL` is required to run these tests at all), cleanup is skipped with a logged note rather than failing the test — every test scopes its own reads through a per-test random `uniqueRunID`-tagged actor/action, so correctness never depends on the table being empty, only on cleanup being a hygiene nicety when the fuller local `.env` is present (which `.env.example` already provides both variables for).

**Concurrency test:** `TestPostgresStore_ConcurrentAppends_ChainStaysConsistent` fires 20 goroutines calling `Append` concurrently (distinct actor per goroutine, one shared distinguishing action tag), then asserts: no error from any call; no duplicate `EventID` among the 20 results; `VerifyIntegrity(store.All())` passes over the *whole* table (meaningful, not vacuous, because every row in this append-only table was necessarily written via `PostgresStore.Append` itself); and exactly 20 rows carry the run's distinguishing action via `Query`, with no duplicates and no foreign IDs mixed in. Deliberately scoped the "exactly N" assertion to a `Query` on the run's own tag rather than a literal `len(store.All())` — asserting a bare global row count would make the test fragile against any earlier suite run whose own cleanup couldn't reach `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL`, given the table can never be forcibly reset by the app role by design.

**Verification:** `go build ./internal/...` and `go vet ./internal/...` clean (repo's top-level `go build ./...`/`go vet ./...` on `services/api` fail only in `main.go`/`wiring.go`, pre-existing, unrelated, in-progress composition-root work explicitly out of this task's scope — confirmed via `git status`/`git diff` that `go.mod`/`go.sum`/`main.go`/`internal/platform` were already modified before this work started). `go test ./internal/audit/... -count=1` with `DATABASE_URL` unset: all 4 new tests report `--- SKIP` with the documented message; all pre-existing tests in the package pass unchanged. Ran live against a real local Postgres: `docker compose up -d postgres` (already running), applied `0001_audit_events.up.sql` directly via `psql` (not `platform.RunMigrations` — a throwaway `cmd/tmpmigrate` attempt hit what appears to be a pre-existing Windows file-URL bug in `platform.toFileURL`/`golang-migrate`'s file source unrelated to this task and out of this capability's scope to fix; `psql` is an explicitly-permitted alternative per this task's own verification instructions), then `ALTER ROLE ascend_app WITH LOGIN PASSWORD ...` to match `.env`'s `ASCEND_APP_DB_PASSWORD`. With both `DATABASE_URL` and `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` set: all 4 Postgres tests plus all 18 pre-existing tests pass; confirmed the table is empty (`SELECT count(*)`) after a full run, proving cleanup works; reran the concurrency test 3 consecutive times, all green, confirming re-runnability with no cross-run pollution; independently confirmed live that `ascend_app` genuinely cannot `DELETE FROM audit_events` (`permission denied`), validating the cleanup design above is not just theoretical. `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`: 5 of 6 checks pass; the one failure (`ART.8 VIOLATION: services/api/internal/platform has no DATA_MANIFEST.md`) is entirely within `internal/platform` (the Chief Architect's own in-progress, untracked work per `git status`), not `internal/audit` — this capability's own `DATA_MANIFEST.md` is present and untouched.

**Deviation from spec:** none in the implementation itself. One deviation in verification method only: used `psql` to apply the migration directly instead of `platform.RunMigrations`, for the Windows-path reason noted above — flagged for the Chief Architect's awareness in case it's a real, separate bug worth a follow-up, since it's in `internal/platform`, not this capability.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (this is the durable backing store behind Audit's Service, which is the substrate every other capability's mutating operation emits an explainable event into), Art. 9 (this table is what `ExportAuditTrail`/`ExportAuditEvent` ultimately read from once wired to real Postgres — unchanged export logic, now durable), Art. 15 (the hash-chain/advisory-lock design is specifically what prevents hidden, silently-reordered, or duplicated state under real concurrency — the risk profile that doesn't exist for a single-process `InMemoryStore`), Art. 10 (this file stays entirely within `internal/audit`, importing only `internal/platform`'s pgx pool type and pgx itself — both already-exempted/third-party — no new cross-capability import).

**Made by:** Audit / Explainability capability-engineer.

### 2026-08-17 — Database setup: shared platform foundation + Audit on real Postgres — design

**Decision:** `services/api` has had zero database infrastructure since inception — every capability persists to an in-memory, mutex-guarded map, and Storage's blob bytes live on local disk. `go.mod` had no Postgres/Redis/S3 driver. This was never an oversight: five separate decision-log entries (Permissions, Identity, Audit, Storage, File Objects — all 2026-07-16/17) each independently deferred real persistence, explicitly because no repo-wide decision on how capabilities share a database connection/migration story existed yet. This entry is that decision.

Confirmed with the founder before design: **Postgres is the durable source of truth** for everything that needs to survive a restart (today: Audit; over time: Identity, Permissions, Storage metadata, File Objects, Sessions). **Redis is scoped to purely ephemeral state with no durability/export requirement** — challenge nonces and future rate-limiting counters. Session tokens stay out of Redis and go to Postgres in a later pass specifically because `ExportSessions` must show expired-but-not-yet-revoked sessions, which a Redis TTL would evict and lose.

**Scope of this pass:** build the shared foundation (a new `services/api/internal/platform` package: Postgres pool, Redis client, S3-compatible client, migration runner) plus a local Docker Compose stack (Postgres, Redis, MinIO), then migrate exactly one capability end to end onto real Postgres as a proven, guardian-gated pattern — **Audit**, chosen because it already has a clean `Store` **interface** (not a concrete struct, unlike Permissions/Storage/File Objects) and its own `Store` doc comment already explicitly anticipated "a future Postgres-backed implementation (append-only table, DB-level REVOKE of UPDATE/DELETE grants)." The other five capabilities stay on their current implementations — this pass proves the pattern, it does not migrate everything. No cloud credentials, no real deployment — local Docker Compose only; real DigitalOcean Managed Postgres/Redis/Spaces provisioning stays the founder's own step.

**Design choices made, each with a documented default rather than left open:**
- **Driver/tooling:** pgx v5 + pgxpool (Postgres), go-redis v9 (Redis), AWS SDK for Go v2 with a custom endpoint (S3-compatible — works unchanged against MinIO locally and DigitalOcean Spaces later), golang-migrate (SQL-file migrations). No ORM anywhere — matches this repo's existing hand-written-Go style (e.g. Audit's own hand-rolled canonical-JSON hashing).
- **`internal/platform` as the package name/location** — already a pre-blessed exemption in `scripts/constitution/check-modularity.sh`'s Art. 10 mechanical check (`shared_exempt="platform audit"`), confirming this was the anticipated shared-infra location before this pass ever ran.
- **Migrations run automatically at server startup**, not a separate CLI — single-service/single-instance scale, `golang-migrate`'s Postgres driver takes an advisory lock during migration so even an accidental concurrent local run is safe. A real multi-replica production rollout should switch to a pre-deploy migration step instead; explicitly deferred, not solved here.
- **Two separate Postgres connection strings**, not one: `MigrationsDatabaseURL` (elevated admin role, used only at startup to run migrations/`CREATE ROLE`/`REVOKE`) and `DatabaseURL` (the least-privilege `ascend_app` role every capability's runtime connection actually uses). `ascend_app`'s password is set via a Go-level `ALTER ROLE ... PASSWORD` step (`platform.ProvisionAppRolePassword`) sourced only from `ASCEND_APP_DB_PASSWORD`, never embedded in a committed `.sql` migration file.
- **No in-memory fallback for Audit once wired** — composition root requires a real Postgres connection, `log.Fatalf`s otherwise, matching this codebase's existing fail-fast-on-construction-error precedent (Storage/File Objects). An env-gated fallback would let a misconfigured deployment "work" while silently discarding every audit event forever.
- **Testing:** `DATABASE_URL`-env-var-skip-gated, not a build tag — no build tags exist anywhere in this repo, and a skip is visible/self-documenting in `go test` output.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 17 (asked first — audit history surviving a restart is a direct, concrete ownership increase, not hedged), Art. 1 (a capability's data must actually remain reachable to the user who owns it, not merely claimed durable), Art. 7 (least-privilege DB role, secrets only from environment variables, never committed), Art. 10 (the shared foundation stays capability-agnostic; `internal/platform` exports only generic clients, never capability-specific SQL), Art. 16 (mirrors, not reinvents, the verification/ownership-split discipline established by every prior wiring pass).

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session), with the founder's explicit sign-off on Sessions-storage placement and pass scope (see plan approval, same date).

## 2026-08-17 — Database setup: composition, live verification, and a testing-artifact false alarm

**Decision:** Completed the composition-root half of the design above. `services/api/main.go`'s `newRouter()` now takes an already-constructed `*platform.Platform` (built once, either by `main()` or by `main_test.go`'s new `TestMain`, never per-call — reconstructing it per `httptest.Server` would re-run migrations and leak connection pools across this file's ~15 test functions). `main()` constructs `Platform` via `platform.ConfigFromEnv()` + `platform.New(ctx, cfg)` before starting the HTTP server, `log.Fatalf`ing on any error and `defer`ing `Close()`. Audit's store swapped from `audit.NewInMemoryStore()` to `audit.NewPostgresStore(plat.DB)` — the only change to Audit's own wiring; `audit.NewService(auditStore, checker)` is untouched. Redis and S3 clients are constructed and health-checked at startup but consumed by nothing yet this pass (documented in `wiring.go`/`main.go` so this doesn't read as dead code later).

**Real bug found and fixed during implementation, unrelated to any capability's logic — a golang-migrate/Windows file-URL incompatibility:** `RunMigrations`' original `"file:///" + absPath` URL form parses (per `source/file/file.go`'s own `parseURL`, read directly to confirm) to a path with a leading slash before the Windows drive letter (`/C:/Users/...`), which `os.DirFS` rejects (confirmed live: `open .: The filename, directory name, or volume label syntax is incorrect.`). Fixed by using the *opaque* URL form (`"file:" + absPath`, no `//`) for Windows drive-letter paths specifically — `net/url.Parse` then populates `u.Opaque` directly with no leading-slash artifact, which the driver uses verbatim. Non-Windows absolute paths (already starting with `/`) are unaffected, unchanged code path. Verified by successfully running real migrations end to end afterward — this was blocking every live verification below until fixed.

**Mechanical check gap found and fixed:** `internal/platform` is a real new directory under `services/api/internal/`, so `check-data-manifests.sh` correctly flagged it as missing a `DATA_MANIFEST.md` (Art. 8) — the check has no "infra package" exemption, by design (an infra package claiming no data is exactly the kind of claim that should be checked, not assumed). Added one stating plainly that this package holds no capability-specific data of its own and pointing to Audit's own manifest for the data its schema actually holds — deliberately using a `## Scope` heading rather than `## Fields`, since the check's per-bullet `Purpose:` rule is keyed on a literal `## Fields` prefix and a `## Fields` heading here would misleadingly imply fields this package doesn't own.

**Live verification performed (not just `go test`, matching every prior wiring pass's standard):**
- `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...`, `go test ./...` — clean both with `DATABASE_URL` set (real Postgres/Redis/MinIO via `docker compose up -d`) and unset (clean skip, one message, exit 0) — both paths required and both proven, not just the happy one.
- `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` — all six checks pass.
- **The `REVOKE` actually holds, checked directly, not trusted from the migration file's intent:** connected via `psql` as `ascend_app` inside the running Postgres container — `SELECT` and `INSERT` succeed (matching the `GRANT`), `UPDATE`/`DELETE` both fail with `permission denied for table audit_events`.
- **Live end-to-end round trip against the real `go run .` binary** (not `httptest`): a throwaway Go client drove the full real flow — `CreateIdentity` → `GetSessionChallenge` → `IssueSession` (real Ed25519 signing) → `Emit` → `Query` → `Explain` → `ExportAuditTrail` — all against the real, live Postgres instance, with correct hash-chain linkage across Identity's, Session/Request Authentication's, and this event's own audit emissions (three different capabilities' events chained together correctly in one table).
- **The headline claim — data survives a restart — proven twice, at two different layers:** first via `docker compose restart postgres` + direct `psql` re-query of a manually-inserted row (exact row present, byte-for-byte, after restart); then, more rigorously, via the live HTTP API itself — restarted Postgres while `go run .` kept running, then re-fetched (via `GET /v1/audit/events/{id}/explain`, using the same still-valid session token from before the restart) the exact event created before the restart. Both the application's connection pool and the session/audit data itself survived, through the real network-facing API, not just the database layer in isolation.

**A false alarm during verification, worth recording so it isn't mistaken for a real bug later:** partway through manual verification, `SELECT count(*) FROM audit_events` unexpectedly returned 0 immediately after a `docker compose restart postgres`, despite `pg_total_relation_size` still showing ~168kB of (not-yet-vacuumed) data — briefly looking exactly like a durability failure. Root cause, traced via Postgres logs (clean shutdown/restart checkpoints, "skipping initialization" confirming the data directory was reused, not reinitialized) and via timing: the Audit capability-engineer's own background verification (a separate, concurrently-running task) was exercising the same shared local `docker compose` Postgres instance at the same time via its own `go test` runs, and that suite's own precisely-scoped, event-ID-targeted cleanup (`cleanupEvents`, `internal/audit/postgres_store_test.go`) coincided with this session's manual checks in a way that produced a momentarily-empty table — not a restart-durability defect. Re-run cleanly, with no concurrent process touching the database, the restart-survival proof above (both the `psql` and live-API forms) held without exception. Recorded here as a testing-environment caveat (concurrent test/verification runs against one shared local dev database can interleave confusingly) rather than a product finding — not something either the platform code or Audit's own implementation needs to change.

**Rationale:** Same as every prior wiring-pass entry — completes the composition for real, and holds this pass to the same verification bar (real integration tests against the true composition root + live manual probing of the real binary, not code review alone) this codebase has held every network-wiring pass to.

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session).

## 2026-08-17 — Database setup: Security Steward block and fix

**Decision:** Ran the standing merge gate on the composition/verification pass above — Constitution Warden and Security Steward in parallel, both independently rebuilding, testing, running the mechanical checks, and live-adversarially-probing the real binary with their own throwaway clients (neither reused this session's scratchpad).

**Constitution Warden: PASS.** Article 17 asked first and answered yes, unhedged — audit history genuinely surviving a restart is a direct ownership increase, independently reproven with the guardian's own binary and probe client (full `Emit`→`Query`→`Explain`→`Export` round trip, restart-survival via re-fetching a pre-restart event by ID with a still-valid pre-restart session token). Full Art. 1/5/7/8/9/10/15/16 review, no blocking findings. Two non-blocking: `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` needed updating (done, see below); an observed instance of concurrent local verification runs interfering with each other on `:8080`, recommending a convention for the next guardian/engineer.

**Security Steward: 🚫 BLOCKED, then ✅ PASS after a charter/documentation fix — not a code revert.** Every mechanical control was independently verified sound and held under direct live attack: `ascend_app`'s `SELECT`/`INSERT`-only privilege (confirmed via `pg_roles`: not superuser, not createrole, not createdb, not bypassrls) with `UPDATE`/`DELETE`/`TRUNCATE`/`DROP TABLE`/`ALTER TABLE` all rejected; 100% parameterized queries in `postgres_store.go`, live-tested with SQL-injection-shaped payloads through real `/v1/audit/events` filters and `Emit` body fields (all treated as inert literal data, table intact afterward); `ProvisionAppRolePassword`'s deliberately-non-parameterized `ALTER ROLE` literal-escaping attacked directly with an adversarial password (`x'; DROP TABLE audit_events; CREATE ROLE pwned SUPERUSER LOGIN PASSWORD 'x'; --`) — no error, no table loss, no rogue role created, the entire string usable verbatim as the literal password afterward, proving the escaping genuinely contains the payload; the migration-admin connection confirmed transient (used only inside `platform.New`, never held in `Platform` or reachable from request-handling code); no secret committed (`.env` untracked, `.env.example`/`docker-compose.yml` placeholders only).

**The block:** Audit's charter (§6, frozen 2026-07-06) makes an explicit, unqualified promise — audit events "must not be silently editable or deletable — **including by platform operators**." Live-tested for the first time this claim became testable at all: connected as `ascend_admin` (the migration-time role — the exact credential a "platform operator" running migrations authenticates as) and ran a plain `DELETE FROM audit_events WHERE ...`. It succeeded silently. No error, no chain-repair. The consequence was then independently confirmed: `VerifyIntegrity` fails on the resulting broken `prev_hash` linkage, exactly as it should for genuine tampering. Root cause is structural, not a fixable oversight in this migration: Postgres table ownership carries an implicit, un-revokable right to `DELETE`/`ALTER`/`DROP`/re-grant any privilege — `REVOKE` has no power over a table's own owner, and `ascend_admin` owns `audit_events` because it ran the `CREATE TABLE`. This was never claimed solved by this pass's own prose (the decision log entries above only ever describe the `REVOKE` as targeting `ascend_app`/`PUBLIC`) — but the charter's frozen text made the stronger claim, and nothing before this merge gate disclosed, narrowed, or tracked the gap between the two. A reader of the charter alone would reasonably believe platform-operator tampering was already prevented. It was not, and (per Security Steward's report) required no adversarial intent to trigger — the capability's own test-cleanup helper (`postgres_store_test.go`'s `cleanupEvents`, using the documented `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` path) is exactly the kind of routine operation that can do it.

**Fix applied, matching Security Steward's three required changes exactly, none of which touch the Postgres/platform implementation itself:**
1. **Charter corrected**, not silently rewritten: `docs/capabilities/audit-explainability.charter.md` §6 gains a dated amendment stating precisely what's enforced today (`ascend_app`/network-caller level) versus what remains an open trust boundary (`ascend_admin`, the table owner) — the original language is left in place as the still-standing design intent, not deleted, with the amendment explicit that it should not be read as already delivered. A new row was added to the charter's own Guardian gate results table recording the block-then-fix cycle, alongside (not replacing) the original 2026-07-06 gate record.
2. **Gap tracked** in `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md`, matching the exact treatment already given to the `CheckPermission` fail-closed gap and the rate-limiting/TLS gaps — a named, findable, open item, not a fact buried only in this log.
3. **Follow-up path scoped, not built:** decoupling the schema-owning role from the migration-runner role (so no login-capable credential owns the table), and/or periodic external anchoring of the hash-chain tip to storage the DB admin doesn't control, so tampering by any DB-level actor becomes detectable even where prevention isn't structurally possible within Postgres's own privilege model. Recorded as future work, deliberately not attempted in this pass — Security Steward's own report explicitly endorsed this scoping as sufficient to unblock, distinct from "solved."
4. **Overclaiming code comments corrected** alongside the charter: `internal/platform/migrations/0001_audit_events.up.sql`'s `REVOKE` comment and `internal/audit/postgres_store_test.go`'s `cleanupEvents` doc comment (which, ironically, had cited the false "including platform operators" claim as justification for using the very admin connection that defeats it) both now state the true scope and — in `cleanupEvents`'s case — explicitly flag that its own `DELETE` calls are a live demonstration of the gap, not just a description of it.

**Re-verification after the fix:** `go build ./... && go vet ./...` clean; `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` — all six checks pass (the charter/comment/registry changes are prose-only, no Go/SQL behavior changed, so no regression risk, but re-run regardless per this build's own standing discipline of never trusting "should still pass" without checking).

**Rationale:** This is the same "disclose gaps, don't overclaim" discipline this codebase has applied repeatedly (the `CheckPermission` fail-closed gap, the Art. 16 export-default inconsistency, the `ListGrantsForResource` wire-contract gap) — a Security Steward block over a written claim exceeding a delivered guarantee is exactly the kind of finding this build's guardian gates exist to catch, and the fix is to make the writing honest and the gap trackable, not to pretend the gate never found anything or to attempt a rushed, under-scoped structural fix under pressure. Postgres's ownership model makes "REVOKE from the operator role" a fundamentally unavailable option — the real fix is a genuine design change (role/ownership decoupling or external anchoring), correctly deferred to its own future, dedicated pass rather than bolted on here.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (an audit trail whose "tamper-evident" claim doesn't match what's actually enforced undermines the explainability guarantee this whole capability exists for — precision in the claim IS the fix, until the structural fix lands), Art. 7 (security must never reduce ownership — shipping an overclaimed guarantee would have been the platform quietly asserting more protection than the user actually has, the opposite of Art. 7's intent), Art. 14 (trust through transparency — the charter amendment and registry entry are exactly "earned through disclosure," not "requested" by asserting a stronger claim than delivered), Art. 17 (the fix itself — precise disclosure plus a scoped follow-up — is what actually increases ownership here; a silently-overclaiming charter would not have).

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session), incorporating Constitution Warden's and Security Steward's independent gate findings.

---

### 2026-08-17 — Migrating remaining capabilities onto Postgres: batch plan

**Decision:** Audit proved the pattern (shared `internal/platform` foundation, real Postgres, guardian-gated). The remaining five capabilities — Identity, Permissions, Storage (metadata only; blob bytes stay on the local-filesystem `Backend` for now), File Objects, Session/Request Authentication — now follow it. Batched in three passes, mirroring this codebase's own established precedent of pairing related capabilities under one merge gate (the "Permissions and Audit" and "Storage and File Objects" network-wiring passes both did this):

- **Batch A (this pass): Identity + Permissions.** Identity's `Store` is already a real interface (`Create`/`Get`/`Replace`, three methods) — the same shape Audit had, least invasive. Permissions' `Store` is currently a concrete struct, not an interface, passed by pointer into `NewService(store *Store, audit AuditEmitter)` — this pass introduces a `Store` interface (same 11 unexported method signatures the concrete type already has, renamed `InMemoryStore`), the same interface-introduction move Storage and File Objects will also need in Batch B.
- **Batch B (next): Storage (metadata) + File Objects.** Both currently build their store internally inside `NewService`/`NewFilesystemService`, no DI seam at all — larger change (constructor signature updates, all call sites).
- **Batch C (last): Session/Request Authentication.** Architecturally distinct — two separate stores (nonces, sessions), split across two backends per the original "Database setup" design entry (nonces → Redis, pure ephemeral, no durability/export need; sessions → Postgres, `ExportSessions` must show expired-but-unrevoked sessions, which Redis TTL would evict). Saved for last deliberately — most design surface, best done once the interface-introduction pattern is proven twice more (Storage, File Objects) beyond Audit.

**Shared conventions for every batch, decided once here rather than re-litigated per capability:**
- **One runtime role for everything, `ascend_app`** (already provisioned by migration 0001) — not a new role per capability. Least privilege is enforced per-*table* via each migration's own `GRANT`s, not by proliferating database roles; this keeps one connection string/credential for the whole application (Art. 12/13 — simple by default) while still giving each table exactly the privileges its own access pattern needs (e.g. an append-only history/log table gets `SELECT, INSERT` only, with an explicit `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE`, mirroring `audit_events`'s own discipline; a table genuine business logic deletes from, e.g. Permissions' active-grants table, gets `DELETE` too).
- **Migration numbering is centrally assigned, not first-come-first-served**, to keep parallel capability-engineer dispatches collision-free: `0002` = Identity, `0003` = Permissions, `0004`/`0005` reserved for Storage/File Objects (Batch B), `0006`+ for Session/Request Authentication (Batch C).
- **Interface-introduction pattern** (Permissions this batch; Storage/File Objects next): rename the existing concrete store type to `InMemoryStore` (matching Audit's/Identity's naming), extract its current method set into a `Store` interface with identical (unexported is fine — everything stays in-package) signatures, change the constructor parameter from `*ConcreteType` to the new `Store` interface type. This is the smallest possible diff that unlocks a second implementation — `Service` itself never changes.
- **`PostgresStore` always lives inside the capability's own package**, never in `internal/platform` — mirrors Audit's precedent exactly (`internal/platform` stays capability-agnostic, exporting only generic clients).
- **Verification bar**: same as every prior pass — real `go build`/`vet`/`test` (both with and without `DATABASE_URL` set), `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`, live probing against the real `go run .` binary, restart-survival proof, one consolidated guardian merge gate per batch (not per capability) — matching the established pairing precedent.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 1 (every capability's data should genuinely survive a restart, not just Audit's), Art. 16 (batch size, role strategy, and interface-introduction shape are all deliberately copied from precedent rather than reinvented per capability), Art. 10 (interface-introduction keeps every `Service` unchanged; no new cross-capability import).

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session).

### 2026-08-17 — Identity: PostgresStore, a real Postgres-backed implementation of Store

**Decision:** Added `services/api/internal/identity/postgres_store.go` (`PostgresStore`, `NewPostgresStore(pool *pgxpool.Pool) *PostgresStore`), a second, additive implementation of the frozen `Store` interface (`store.go`: `Create(record IdentityRecord) error`, `Get(identityRef string) (IdentityRecord, error)`, `Replace(record IdentityRecord) error`) alongside — not replacing — `InMemoryStore`, against the schema `internal/platform/migrations/0002_identities.up.sql`/`.down.sql` (migration number centrally assigned to Identity — see this log's "Database setup: composition, live verification, and a testing-artifact false alarm" entry, Batch A). `Service`, `InMemoryStore`, `IdentityRecord`, `Device`, and every existing test in this package are unchanged.

- **Schema:** `identities (identity_ref TEXT PRIMARY KEY, display_name TEXT, public_key BYTEA, created_at_unix BIGINT, epoch BIGINT DEFAULT 0, devices JSONB DEFAULT '[]')`. Every column maps 1:1 onto a documented field in `DATA_MANIFEST.md` (Art. 8). Devices are packed as a single JSONB array, not a separate relational table: `Replace` overwrites the entire record atomically in the current design, and nothing anywhere queries "list all devices across identities" as a cross-cutting operation, so JSONB preserves `InMemoryStore`'s whole-record-replace semantics exactly with the least complexity (Art. 12/13) — the same reasoning `audit_events.metadata` already established for a Go value living in a JSONB column. No indexes beyond the primary key: every lookup this `Store` interface exposes is by `identity_ref` alone.
- **Role/grants:** the runtime role `ascend_app` already exists (created idempotently by migration 0001) — this migration does not create a new role, only `GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE ON identities TO ascend_app`. No `DELETE` grant: `Store` has no delete method — `RevokeDevice` (service.go) calls `Replace` with a shrunk `Devices` list, never removes a row. Unlike `audit_events`, Identity's charter has no append-only/tamper-evidence clause, so `UPDATE` is granted plainly with no accompanying `REVOKE`.
- **`Create(record IdentityRecord) error`**: parameterized `INSERT`, `Devices` marshaled via plain `encoding/json` (direct `json.Marshal(record.Devices)` — `Device`'s existing camelCase JSON tags and `encoding/json`'s automatic base64 encoding of `[]byte` already produce a correct, round-trippable shape; no separate DTO type needed, confirmed by the round-trip test). Duplicate `identity_ref` is detected via the real Postgres unique-constraint violation on the primary key (`pgconn.PgError.Code == "23505"`, checked with `errors.As`), not a pre-check `SELECT` (which would race against a concurrent `Create` of the same ref), and mapped to `ErrInvalidArgument` — the exact sentinel `InMemoryStore.Create` returns for the same condition. Any other insert failure is a genuine, wrapped error (`fmt.Errorf("identity: postgres create: ...: %w", err)`), not folded into a sentinel — unlike Audit's `Get`/`All`, this capability's `Store` methods all have real error returns to use, so genuine DB failures (a dropped connection, etc.) are propagated honestly rather than disguised as "not found" or "invalid argument."
- **`Get(identityRef string) (IdentityRecord, error)`**: parameterized `SELECT`; `pgx.ErrNoRows` maps to `ErrIdentityNotFound` (the exact sentinel `InMemoryStore.Get` returns); any other scan/query failure is a genuine wrapped error.
- **`Replace(record IdentityRecord) error`**: parameterized `UPDATE ... WHERE identity_ref = $1`, checked via `RowsAffected()` rather than a separate existence check first (avoids a check-then-act race against a concurrent `Replace` of the same ref) — zero rows affected maps to `ErrIdentityNotFound`, matching `InMemoryStore.Replace` exactly.
- Every value in every query is bound via `$1..$N`; no `fmt.Sprintf`/string-concatenation into SQL anywhere in this file.

**Test isolation:** `identities` carries no `DELETE` grant for `ascend_app` by design (same reasoning as above), so `postgres_store_test.go`'s `cleanupIdentities` — mirroring Audit's `cleanupEvents` pattern exactly — connects separately via `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` (the admin connection) to delete exactly the rows a test created, in `t.Cleanup`. If `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` isn't set, cleanup is skipped with a logged note rather than failing the test; every test scopes its own reads through a per-test random `uniqueRunID`-tagged `identity_ref`, so correctness never depends on the table being empty. Five test cases: `Create`→`Get` round trip (every field, including byte-for-byte `PublicKey` and a two-device `Devices` slice, asserted individually — not a bare `reflect.DeepEqual`, since JSON round-tripping through a DB column is exactly the kind of place a subtle field-ordering or byte-encoding bug would hide); duplicate `Create` returns `ErrInvalidArgument`; `Get` on a nonexistent ref returns `ErrIdentityNotFound`; `Replace` updates an existing record and correctly overwrites `Devices` with a wholly different set (one carried-over device, one dropped, one new — proving whole-column overwrite, not a merge); `Replace` on a nonexistent ref returns `ErrIdentityNotFound`.

**Verification:** `go build ./...` and `go vet ./...` (from `services/api`) clean. `go test ./internal/identity/... -count=1` with `DATABASE_URL` unset: all 5 new Postgres tests report `--- SKIP` with the documented message; all pre-existing tests in the package (identity, http, sign, service, export, etc.) pass unchanged. Docker was available (`docker compose up -d` — Postgres/Redis/MinIO already running from the parallel Database-setup composition pass); applied `0002_identities.up.sql` directly via `psql` piped over stdin rather than `platform.RunMigrations` — a throwaway `go test`-based call to `RunMigrations` against the same local Postgres hung past a 2-minute timeout for reasons not diagnosed further (out of this capability's scope — `internal/platform` is explicitly off-limits for this task, and `ascend_app`'s password was already provisioned from a prior successful run, confirmed live via `pg_authid`), so fell back to the explicitly-permitted `psql` alternative, same as Audit's own capability-engineer did when blocked by a related but distinct Windows file-URL issue. Manually advanced `schema_migrations.version` to 2 to keep the migration ledger consistent with what was actually applied. With both `DATABASE_URL` and `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` set: all 5 new Postgres tests pass live against real Postgres; full repo `go test ./...` from `services/api` shows every internal package passing (`internal/audit`, `internal/fileobjects`, `internal/identity`, `internal/permissions`, `internal/sessionauth`, `internal/storage`) — the only failure is the top-level `github.com/ascend/services/api` package itself (`main.go:103: undefined: permissions.NewStore`), confirmed via `git status` to be pre-existing, in-progress, parallel composition-root work already modified before this task started, not caused by anything in this change (this task's own instructions explicitly place `main.go`/`wiring.go` out of scope). Confirmed the `identities` table is empty (`SELECT count(*)`) after the full run, proving test cleanup works. `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`: all 6 checks pass (Art. 5 audit events, Art. 5 audit events TS, Art. 8 data manifests, Art. 9 exportability, Art. 2 file objects, Art. 10 modularity).

**Deviation from spec:** none in the implementation. One deviation in verification method only, disclosed above: used `psql` to apply the migration directly instead of `platform.RunMigrations`, after the latter hung — flagged for the Chief Architect's awareness as a possible real issue in `internal/platform` worth investigating separately, since diagnosing it further would mean touching a package this task explicitly places off-limits.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 1/9 (this table is what backs every one of Identity's export/resolve/list paths once wired to real Postgres — unchanged export logic, now durable across restarts), Art. 8 (every persisted column maps 1:1 onto `DATA_MANIFEST.md`; no new field introduced), Art. 12/13 (a single JSONB `devices` column, not a new relational table, chosen specifically because it is the least additional complexity that still preserves exact existing semantics), Art. 10 (this file stays entirely within `internal/identity`, importing only `internal/platform`'s pool type and pgx itself — both already-exempted/third-party — no new cross-capability import).

**Made by:** Identity capability-engineer.

### 2026-08-17 — Permissions: Store interface introduced, PostgresStore added

**Decision:** Two parts, both against `services/api/internal/permissions/`, matching Batch A's interface-introduction pattern (see this log's "Migrating remaining capabilities onto Postgres: batch plan" entry).

**Part 1 — `Store` interface introduced.** `store.go`'s concrete `Store` struct is renamed `InMemoryStore` (`NewStore()` → `NewInMemoryStore()`); a new `type Store interface` is defined with the exact same 11 method signatures `InMemoryStore` already had (`activeGrant`, `ownerOf`, `putGrant`, `removeGrant`, `appendHistory`, `setPolicy`, `hasPolicy`, `grantsForResource`, `grantsForSubject`, `historyForIdentity`, `activeGrantsForIdentity`) — unexported, staying in-package, same shape as `InMemoryStore`'s pre-existing method set. `Service.store` (`service.go`) changes from `*Store` to `Store`; `NewService(store *Store, ...)` becomes `NewService(store Store, ...)`. No other line in `service.go`/`export.go` changed — every `s.store.xxx(...)` call site is textually identical, because the interface's method names and signatures are identical to what `*Store` already exposed. The one in-package test call site (`service_test.go`) was updated to `NewInMemoryStore()`; `main.go`'s `permissions.NewStore()` call site was deliberately left untouched — that composition-root update is explicitly the Chief Architect's own separate, parallel work, per this task's brief.

**Part 2 — `PostgresStore` added**, a second, additive implementation of `Store` (`postgres_store.go`, `NewPostgresStore(pool *pgxpool.Pool) *PostgresStore`), against migration `0003_permissions` (`internal/platform/migrations/0003_permissions.{up,down}.sql`, migration number centrally assigned to Permissions per the batch plan): four tables — `permissions_grants` (PK `(subject, action, resource_type, resource_id)`, mirroring `InMemoryStore`'s `active` map exactly, keyed on the real composite columns rather than `ResourceRef.key()`'s synthetic string concatenation, which only ever existed to make a Go map lookup work), `permissions_owners` (PK `(resource_type, resource_id)`), `permissions_policies` (PK `resource_type`), `permissions_history` (`BIGSERIAL` PK, append-only). `ascend_app` (already provisioned by migration 0001) gets `SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE` on `permissions_grants`/`permissions_owners` (real business-logic tables Service actually deletes from — `RevokePermission` calls `removeGrant`), `SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE` on `permissions_policies` (`DefinePolicy` redefines in place, never deletes), and `SELECT/INSERT` only on `permissions_history` with an explicit `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE` — the same append-only discipline `audit_events` (migration 0001) established, carrying the identical, already-disclosed scope caveat from the "Database setup: Security Steward block and fix" entry (the `REVOKE` binds `ascend_app`/`PUBLIC`, not the migration-admin role that owns the table).

**Owner-bootstrap-once semantics, replicated exactly:** `InMemoryStore.putGrant` records a resource's owner once, on its first-ever grant, via `if _, hasOwner := s.owners[...]; !hasOwner { s.owners[...] = g.Grantor }` — and `removeGrant` never touches the owners map, so ownership outlives the grant that established it even after that grant is revoked. `PostgresStore.putGrant` replicates this in a single transaction: an `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (subject, action, resource_type, resource_id) DO UPDATE` upserts the grant row (matching `InMemoryStore`'s "latest grant is authoritative" re-grant-overwrites behavior), followed by `INSERT INTO permissions_owners ... ON CONFLICT (resource_type, resource_id) DO NOTHING` — a no-op if an owner row already exists, regardless of whether the grant that created it is still active. Both writes commit atomically so no concurrent reader can observe one without the other. Live-tested directly (`TestPostgresStore_OwnerBootstrapOnce`): grant → owner recorded; revoke that same grant → owner unchanged; a *different* grantor grants a new action on the same resource → original owner still unchanged.

**Error-handling design decision — the load-bearing one for this whole file, documented in full at the top of `postgres_store.go` (summarized here):** Part 1's constraint that `Service`'s call sites stay textually and behaviorally identical fixes `Store`'s 11 method signatures to `InMemoryStore`'s existing error-return-free shape — adding error returns here would be exactly the kind of quiet interface drift a capability engineer must not introduce without stepping back through the Chief Architect for a fresh gate, even though this interface (unlike Audit's) was introduced in this very task. So the real design question was narrower: how does a genuine Postgres failure surface with no error-return channel to use? Two different policies, applied consistently within each group:
- **The seven methods with a meaningful zero-ish return** fold a genuine query failure into the same shape as a legitimate miss/false/empty result — Audit's own `Get`/`All` precedent (`internal/audit/postgres_store.go`'s "defensive-error-handling" notes) — **except `ownerOf`**, which needed a *different* fold specifically: `("", false)` on a genuine error would be indistinguishable from "this resource has never been granted before," and `Service.authorizeGrant` reads exactly that shape as license to let the requesting grantor bootstrap themselves in as owner (`"bootstrap_owner"`) — silently manufacturing a privilege-escalation opportunity out of a database hiccup, categorically worse than a stale read. So a genuine (non-`ErrNoRows`) failure in `ownerOf` returns `("", true)` instead: `ok=true` asserts "yes, owned", while `owner=""` is a value no real owner can ever hold (every `Grantor` that can become an owner is required non-empty by `GrantPermission`'s own validation before `putGrant` is ever called), so every downstream owner-equality check fails to match — the safe, deny-shaped direction — without ever reaching the bootstrap branch.
- **The four void-return mutation methods** (`putGrant`, `removeGrant`, `appendHistory`, `setPolicy`) have no return value at all to fold anything into. Silently no-oping on a genuine write failure here would let `Service` proceed to append a history record and emit a `permissions.grant`/`permissions.revoke`/`permissions.define_policy` audit event reporting success for a mutation that never reached the database — exactly the "silent success hazard Art. 5 forbids" `service.go`'s own comments already call out repeatedly for the audit-emit step, one layer lower. These four `panic` on a genuine failure instead, via a shared `storeFailure(op, err)` helper. A panic inside an HTTP handler goroutine is recovered per-request by Go's own `net/http` server (logs the stack trace, closes that one connection, never writes a 200-with-lies response) — a hard failure visible in logs, not a soft one, and no other in-flight request is affected. This is the disciplined extension of this package's existing "fail loudly, never silently report success" philosophy to the one layer where Part 1's fixed interface signatures leave no other channel to do so.

**Schema notes:** every query parameterized (`$1..$N`), no string-built SQL anywhere in `postgres_store.go`. `historyForIdentity`/`activeGrantsForIdentity` match `InMemoryStore`'s exact `subject == identityRef || grantor == identityRef` predicate (`WHERE subject = $1 OR grantor = $1`). `historyForIdentity` orders `ORDER BY id ASC` to mirror `InMemoryStore`'s natural append-order slice iteration; `activeGrantsForIdentity`/`grantsForResource`/`grantsForSubject` carry no explicit order, matching `InMemoryStore`'s own unordered Go-map iteration for the equivalent methods (there is nothing to preserve).

**Test isolation:** `permissions_grants`/`permissions_owners` carry real `DELETE` grants for `ascend_app`, so `postgres_store_test.go` cleans those up via the store's own `DATABASE_URL` connection; `permissions_history` (and, best-effort, `permissions_policies`, which also has no `DELETE` grant) are cleaned via `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL`, mirroring Audit's `cleanupEvents` pattern exactly, including its "skip with a logged note if unset, tests stay correct via unique run-id tags regardless" disclosure. Seven test cases cover: owner bootstrap-once (grant/revoke/re-grant-by-someone-else); `activeGrant` hit/miss plus re-grant-overwrites-in-place; `hasPolicy`/`setPolicy` before/after/redefine (plus a direct row-count assertion proving redefine upserts rather than duplicating); `grantsForResource`/`grantsForSubject` filtering; `historyForIdentity`/`activeGrantsForIdentity` matching as either subject or grantor; and a direct, live proof that `appendHistory` writes a queryable row while a `DELETE` attempted through the store's own app-role connection is rejected with Postgres `SQLSTATE 42501` (insufficient privilege) — confirming the migration's `REVOKE` holds against the exact credential every production request uses, mirroring Audit's own live `REVOKE`-verification discipline.

**Verification:** `go build ./internal/permissions/...` and `go vet ./internal/permissions/...` clean. `go test ./internal/permissions/... -count=1` with `DATABASE_URL` unset: all 7 new Postgres tests report `--- SKIP` with the documented message; every pre-existing test in the package (service, http, export) passes unchanged — confirming the interface introduction caused zero regressions. Docker was available and already running (`ascend-postgres-1`, shared with the parallel Identity/Database-setup work); migration `0003_permissions` was applied via `platform.RunMigrations` itself (not a `psql` fallback) — invoked through a temporary, git-ignored `cmd/scratch-migrate` throwaway binary inside the module (needed only because `internal/` packages aren't importable from outside `services/api`'s own module tree; removed immediately after use, never committed) — which completed successfully on the first try, confirming the Windows file-URL fix already landed in `internal/platform/migrate.go` (`toFileURL`'s opaque-form `"file:C:/..."` construction) works as intended; no fallback needed. `\dt`/`\z permissions_history` confirmed all four tables exist and `ascend_app`'s privileges on `permissions_history` are exactly `ar` (SELECT, INSERT) — no `w`/`d`. With both `DATABASE_URL` and `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` set: all 7 new Postgres tests pass live (including the live `SQLSTATE 42501` REVOKE proof), and the full non-root suite (`internal/audit`, `internal/fileobjects`, `internal/identity`, `internal/permissions`, `internal/sessionauth`, `internal/storage`) passes — the only failure anywhere is the pre-existing, out-of-scope `github.com/ascend/services/api` root package itself (`main.go:103: undefined: permissions.NewStore`), confirmed via `git status` to already exist before this task started, and explicitly the Chief Architect's own parallel work per this task's brief. `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`: all 6 checks pass.

**Deviation from spec:** none. The `cmd/scratch-migrate` throwaway used to invoke `platform.RunMigrations` directly (rather than `psql`) was created and deleted entirely within this task's own verification step, never committed, and touches no file this task's constraints place off-limits (`internal/platform/` itself was read-only, never edited).

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (the panic-on-write-failure design keeps every persisted mutation loudly, not silently, tied to its own success/failure — no audit or history record can ever claim success for a grant/revoke/policy-change that didn't actually land), Art. 7 (`ownerOf`'s `("", true)` fold specifically closes a privilege-escalation path a database hiccup could otherwise have opened — "security must never reduce ownership" cuts both ways: an outage must never *grant* more access than intended either), Art. 8 (every column maps 1:1 onto `DATA_MANIFEST.md`'s documented fields; `Policy`'s manifest-documented exclusion from Art. 8 collection obligations carries through unchanged to `permissions_policies`), Art. 9 (`permissions_history`/`permissions_grants` are exactly what `ExportPermissions` reads, now durable across restarts), Art. 10 (this file stays entirely within `internal/permissions`, importing only `internal/platform`'s pool type and pgx itself — no new cross-capability import), Art. 16 (interface-introduction shape, migration numbering, role/grant strategy, and Audit-precedent error-handling for read paths are all deliberately copied from the batch plan rather than reinvented).

**Made by:** Permissions capability-engineer.

## 2026-08-17 — Batch A (Identity + Permissions) network composition and live verification

**Decision:** Wired both capability-engineers' work into the composition root. `services/api/main.go`'s `newRouter()`: `identity.NewInMemoryStore()` → `identity.NewPostgresStore(plat.DB)` (no other change — `identity.NewService`'s call site untouched, since `Store` was already an interface); `permissions.NewStore()` → `permissions.NewPostgresStore(plat.DB)` (Permissions' own rename of its constructor from `NewStore` to `NewInMemoryStore` meant this call site had to change regardless of which implementation was chosen — picked the real one). No in-memory fallback for either, matching Audit's own precedent and this codebase's standing fail-fast-on-construction-error discipline.

**A hang, diagnosed, not a bug:** Identity's capability-engineer reported `platform.RunMigrations` hanging past a 2-minute timeout during their own verification and worked around it with `psql`, flagging it as a possible real issue in `internal/platform`. Permissions' capability-engineer, working concurrently against the same shared local Postgres, ran `platform.RunMigrations` directly (via a throwaway `cmd/scratch-migrate` binary) and it succeeded immediately. Root cause: `golang-migrate`'s Postgres driver takes a session-level Postgres advisory lock for the duration of a migration run — with both capability-engineers' tasks dispatched in parallel and each independently attempting to run migrations against the one shared `docker compose` Postgres instance around the same time, one call held that lock while the other waited, indistinguishable from a hang within a 2-minute window. Confirmed by elimination (no code difference between the two attempts; the second, later, uncontended attempt succeeded immediately) rather than by direct reproduction — recorded here, not silently dropped, since it's now the *fourth* documented instance this session of concurrent local-dev-stack contention producing a false alarm (see the two "Database setup" entries and Constitution Warden's/Security Steward's own merge-gate reports for the other three). `platform.RunMigrations` itself required no code change.

**Live verification performed (same standard as every prior pass):**
- `go build ./... && go vet ./...` — clean immediately, no iteration needed (both capability-engineers' own pre-integration verification already caught what would otherwise have been composition-root-discovered errors).
- `go test ./...` — clean both with `DATABASE_URL` set (real stack) and unset (clean skip).
- `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` — all six checks pass.
- **Live round trip against the real `go run .` binary**, a throwaway Go client: `CreateIdentity` → `GetSessionChallenge` → `IssueSession` → `GrantPermission`/`CheckPermission`/`ListGrantsForSubject`/`ExportPermissions`, all against real Postgres. Two incidental findings while probing, both correctly diagnosed as test-harness artifacts, not product bugs, and left as extra confirmation rather than re-run to hide them: (1) `CheckPermission` on a resource type this probe never registered via `DefinePolicy` correctly returned `Allowed: false` — the same long-tracked fail-closed-for-unregistered-type behavior, not new; (2) a second run of the same probe (fresh identity, but a **fixed, reused** `resource_type`/`resource_id` literal) correctly had its self-grant *denied* (`insufficient_privilege`) rather than bootstrap-owner-succeeding, because the first run's process had already durably recorded a different identity as that exact resource's owner in real Postgres — an unplanned but genuine live proof that Permissions' ownership state persists not just across a restart but across entirely separate process invocations. A follow-up `StoreBlob`/`RetrieveBlob` round trip through Storage (still in-memory metadata, unmigrated) against the real Postgres-backed Permissions instance, using the properly-registered `"blob"` resource type, confirmed the clean bootstrap-owner-succeeds/`CheckPermission: true` path works end to end across the capability boundary.
- **Restart-survival, proven via the live API** (not just `psql`): `docker compose restart postgres`, then re-fetched the identity created before the restart via `GET /v1/identity/{ref}` — 200, fields intact.

**Rationale:** Same as every prior wiring/database pass — completes the composition for real, holds this pass to the same verification bar, and records rather than hides the incidental findings (a diagnosed false-alarm hang, two test-harness-caused-but-informative Permissions results) so a future reader doesn't waste time re-discovering the same false alarms.

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session).
