### 2026-08-17 — Storage: Store interface introduced, PostgresStore added

**Decision:** Two parts, both against `services/api/internal/storage/`, matching Permissions' interface-introduction pattern exactly (see this log's "Permissions: Store interface introduced, PostgresStore added" entry, itself following the Batch A plan).

**Part 1 — `Store` interface introduced.** `store.go`'s concrete `Store` struct is renamed `InMemoryStore` (`NewStore()` → `NewInMemoryStore()`); a new `type Store interface` is defined with the exact same 9 method signatures `InMemoryStore` already had (`lockBlob`, `put`, `get`, `delete`, `recordsForOwner`, `putWrappingKey`, `wrappingKey`, `destroyWrappingKey`, `hasWrappingKey`) — unexported, staying in-package. `Service.store` (`service.go`) changes from `*Store` to `Store`. `NewService(policies []Policy, checker PermissionChecker, audit AuditEmitter) (*Service, error)` becomes `NewService(store Store, policies []Policy, checker PermissionChecker, audit AuditEmitter) (*Service, error)` — `store` placed **first**, mirroring `permissions.NewService(store, audit)`'s parameter ordering (confirmed live at its own `main.go` call site: `permissions.NewService(permissionsStore, permissionsAuditEmitterAdapter{...})`), so a reader scanning any capability's constructor signature finds `Store` in the same position every time. `NewFilesystemService(checker PermissionChecker, audit AuditEmitter) (*Service, error)` (`default.go`) becomes `NewFilesystemService(store Store, checker PermissionChecker, audit AuditEmitter) (*Service, error)`, passing `store` straight through to `NewService` unexamined. Every call site in this package's own test files (`service_test.go`, `service_concurrency_test.go`, `http_test.go`, `storage_export_test.go`, `permissions_integration_test.go`, `default_test.go`) was updated to pass `NewInMemoryStore()` explicitly. `main.go`/`wiring.go` were deliberately left untouched — that composition-root update is the Chief Architect's own separate, parallel work, per this task's brief (confirmed via `git status` that the resulting `go build ./...` failure at `main.go:137` is exactly this signature change, plus a second, unrelated pre-existing failure at `main.go:146` from File Objects' own in-flight parallel `NewService` signature change — neither caused by anything else in this change).

**Final signatures (for the Chief Architect's `main.go`/`wiring.go` update):**
```go
func NewService(store Store, policies []Policy, checker PermissionChecker, audit AuditEmitter) (*Service, error)
func NewFilesystemService(store Store, checker PermissionChecker, audit AuditEmitter) (*Service, error)
```

**Part 2 — `PostgresStore` added**, a second, additive implementation of `Store` (`postgres_store.go`, `NewPostgresStore(pool *pgxpool.Pool) *PostgresStore`), against migration `0004_storage` (`internal/platform/migrations/0004_storage.{up,down}.sql`, migration number centrally assigned to Storage): two tables — `storage_blobs` (PK `blob_ref`, one row per `BlobRecord`, indexed on `owner` for `recordsForOwner`/`ExportAllBlobs`) and `storage_wrapping_keys` (PK `blob_ref`, `BYTEA key_bytes`) — mirroring `InMemoryStore`'s own two separate maps exactly, for the same reason documented in `store.go`'s package doc comment: keeping wrapping-key material structurally separate from the Art. 8/9-governed `BlobRecord` data is a deliberate safeguard against a future change accidentally leaking a wrapping key through an export path. Unlike `permissions_history`/`audit_events`, **neither table gets a `REVOKE`** — both legitimate business-logic paths here (`DeleteBlob`'s crypto-shred destroying a wrapping key, `DeleteBlob`'s physical-delete/tombstone path updating a record in place, `StoreBlob`'s rollback path hard-deleting a record) genuinely need `UPDATE`/`DELETE`; this is not an append-only/tamper-evidence table the way `audit_events`/`permissions_history` are.

**The genuinely new design problem — `lockBlob`'s cross-call advisory lock.** `InMemoryStore.lockBlob` hands out one `*sync.Mutex` per `blob_ref` (via a `sync.Map`), held across an entire `Service.MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob` method body — several separate `Store` method calls (`get`, then business logic, then `put`), with no shared transaction connecting them. A transaction-scoped `pg_advisory_xact_lock` (what Audit's `Append` uses) cannot replicate this: it releases automatically at the end of one transaction, and there is no single transaction spanning `MoveBlob`'s/`DeleteBlob`'s several separate `Store` calls. The fix: `PostgresStore.lockBlob` calls `pool.Acquire(ctx)` to check out a **dedicated `*pgxpool.Conn`** from the pool, runs `SELECT pg_advisory_lock($1)` on it (`$1` derived from `blobRef` via `fnv1a64` — the identical algorithm Audit's own `postgres_store.go` uses for its fixed lock key, copied rather than imported cross-package per Art. 10), and returns a closure that runs `SELECT pg_advisory_unlock($1)` on that **exact same connection**, then calls `conn.Release()`. The connection is never returned to the pool between lock and unlock — a plain `pool.Exec` in between would hand back a different, essentially random connection, and `pg_advisory_lock`'s session-level variant is tied to the specific backend session that took it, so locking and unlocking on different connections would silently fail to serialize anything (either erroring on unlock, or worse, leaving the lock held by the original connection until it eventually closes). A failure to acquire the connection or take the lock panics rather than returning a no-op unlock — proceeding as though the lock were held when it wasn't would silently reintroduce the exact double-delete/lost-update race this exists to prevent.

**Proving it actually serializes, not just that lock/unlock don't error:** `TestPostgresStore_LockBlob_SerializesConcurrentGetThenPutSequences` runs N goroutines each executing the identical `lockBlob → get → increment a counter field → put → unlock` sequence against the same `blob_ref` — a classic read-modify-write race detector. If the lock genuinely serializes, the final counter equals exactly N (every increment observed by the next reader); if two connections' lock/unlock pairs were not tied to the same session, goroutines would race past each other and undercount with very high probability. This passed live against real Postgres. A companion test, `TestPostgresStore_LockBlob_DifferentBlobRefsDoNotBlockEachOther`, proves the other half of the contract (`InMemoryStore.lockBlob`'s own doc comment: "different blob_refs never block each other") — two goroutines lock two different `blob_ref`s concurrently and neither blocks on the other, caught via a timeout channel rather than hanging forever if this regressed to one global lock.

**A real pool-exhaustion hazard found while writing the test, not silently worked around:** `pg_advisory_lock` blocks server-side until the lock is free, holding its checked-out connection for the *entire time a caller is waiting*, not just while it holds the lock. The serialization test's first draft used 12 concurrent workers against `platform.maxPoolConns = 10` and hung for the full test timeout (~99s, confirmed via a background run) before a connection was administratively terminated — root cause: enough goroutines piled up blocked *inside* `SELECT pg_advisory_lock` that every pool connection was consumed by blocked waiters, leaving none free for the eventual lock-holder's own subsequent `get()`/`put()` calls, which need a connection from the *same* pool — a genuine self-inflicted deadlock, not a correctness bug in the lock's serialization semantics. Fixed the test by keeping `workers` well below `maxPoolConns` (6, not 12) rather than architecting around the underlying limitation (e.g. a second, dedicated connection pool just for locking) — the latter would be a real architecture change beyond this task's scope, and Storage's actual traffic pattern (a handful of concurrent operations per `blob_ref`, not pool-exhausting volumes) makes this a low-probability risk in production. Documented prominently in both `postgres_store.go`'s `lockBlob` doc comment and `postgres_store_test.go`'s own worker-count comment, flagged here as a follow-up worth revisiting if hot-`blob_ref` MoveBlob/DeleteBlob throughput ever grows materially — not silently dropped.

**Error-handling design decision**, following Permissions' precedent exactly (full reasoning at the top of `postgres_store.go`): Part 1's constraint that `Service`'s call sites stay textually and behaviorally identical fixes `Store`'s 9 method signatures to `InMemoryStore`'s existing error-return-free shape. Split the same way Permissions' was:
- **The five read-shaped methods** (`get`, `recordsForOwner`, `wrappingKey`, `hasWrappingKey`, plus `destroyWrappingKey`'s implicit existence check) fold a genuine query failure into the same shape as a legitimate miss — `get`: DB error → `(BlobRecord{}, false)`, same as "no such `blob_ref`," which every caller already treats as `ErrBlobNotFound`, never an implicit "proceed as if it exists." `recordsForOwner`: DB error → `nil`, matching Audit's/Permissions' identical precedent for a read-only export path (`ExportAllBlobs`) with no allow/deny decision riding on it.
- **The four write methods** (`put`, `delete`, `putWrappingKey`, and `destroyWrappingKey`'s actual `DELETE`) have either no return value or (for `destroyWrappingKey`) a `bool` whose true/false is part of its own pre-existing, frozen contract ("Returns false if no key was present to destroy") — not a spare error channel. Silently no-oping on a genuine write failure would let `StoreBlob`/`MoveBlob`/`DeleteBlob` proceed to emit a `storage.store_blob`/`storage.move_blob`/`storage.delete_blob` audit event reporting success for a mutation that never reached the database — the same "silent success hazard Art. 5 forbids" `service.go`'s own comments call out repeatedly for the audit-emit step. These panic on a genuine failure via a shared `storeFailure(op, err)` helper (same name and shape as Permissions' own, kept package-local rather than shared, since Art. 10 forbids either package importing the other's internals just for a helper). `destroyWrappingKey` specifically does **not** fold a genuine DB failure into `false`: that would be indistinguishable from "already destroyed," and `DeleteBlob`'s crypto-shred path treats that as a hard failure condition it must not silently swallow into a false "shred succeeded."

**Schema notes:** every query parameterized (`$1..$N`), no string-built SQL anywhere in `postgres_store.go`. `put`/`putWrappingKey` use `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (blob_ref) DO UPDATE`, matching `InMemoryStore.put`'s/`putWrappingKey`'s unconditional overwrite-on-write semantics exactly (proven directly by `TestPostgresStore_PutGetRoundTrip`'s explicit row-count assertion after an overwrite). `destroyWrappingKey` checks `tag.RowsAffected() > 0` rather than a preceding `SELECT`, avoiding a read-then-write race within the method itself.

**Verification:** `go build ./internal/storage/...` and `go vet ./internal/storage/...` clean. `go test ./internal/storage/... -count=1` with `DATABASE_URL` unset: all 8 new Postgres tests report `--- SKIP` with the documented message; every pre-existing test in the package (service, concurrency, http, export, permissions-integration, default) passes unchanged — confirming the interface introduction caused zero regressions to the existing in-memory-backed suite, including the two pre-existing concurrency-hardening proofs (`TestDeleteBlob_ConcurrentDuplicateRequests_ExactlyOneSucceeds`, `TestMoveBlob_ConcurrentDuplicateRequests_SafelySerialized`). Docker was available and already running (`ascend-postgres-1`, shared with parallel work); migration `0004_storage` was applied via `platform.RunMigrations` through a temporary, git-ignored `cmd/scratch-migrate` throwaway binary (created and deleted entirely within this task's own verification step, never committed — needed only because `internal/` packages aren't importable from outside `services/api`'s own module tree), which completed successfully. With both `DATABASE_URL` and `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` set: all 8 new Postgres tests pass live, including the corrected (workers=6) lock-serialization proof; full package suite passes with `DATABASE_URL` set. `go build ./...` from `services/api` fails only at `main.go:137` (this task's own signature change, expected and explicitly out of scope per this task's brief — the Chief Architect updates `main.go` separately) and `main.go:146` (File Objects' own unrelated, parallel `NewService` signature change) — confirmed via `git status`/`git diff` neither is caused by anything in this change. `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`: all 6 checks pass (Art. 5 audit events, Art. 5 audit events TS, Art. 8 data manifests, Art. 9 exportability, Art. 2 file objects, Art. 10 modularity).

**Deviation from spec:** none in the implementation. The pool-sizing hazard found while writing the lock-serialization test (see above) is disclosed as a documented, accepted limitation of the connection-holding advisory-lock pattern this task specified, rather than a silent workaround — no architecture change (e.g. a second dedicated pool for locking) was made, since that would exceed this task's scope; flagged for future revisiting instead.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (the panic-on-write-failure design keeps every persisted mutation loudly, not silently, tied to its own success/failure — no `storage.store_blob`/`storage.move_blob`/`storage.delete_blob` audit event can ever claim success for a write that didn't actually land), Art. 7/6 (the `lockBlob` cross-call serialization is what makes `DeleteBlob`'s crypto-shred/physical-delete guarantee and `MoveBlob`'s no-dual-copy guarantee hold under real concurrent Postgres access, not just in-memory — a security/integrity property this migration must not silently regress), Art. 8 (every `storage_blobs` column maps 1:1 onto `DATA_MANIFEST.md`'s documented fields; `storage_wrapping_keys` stays explicitly outside the manifest and outside every export path, matching the manifest's own "Not collected / not a data field" note), Art. 9 (`storage_blobs`/`recordsForOwner` is exactly what `ExportAllBlobs` reads, now durable across restarts), Art. 10 (this file stays entirely within `internal/storage`, importing only `internal/platform`'s pool type and pgx itself — no new cross-capability import; `fnv1a64` copied rather than imported from `internal/audit`), Art. 15 (the pool-exhaustion hazard is disclosed rather than hidden — an inspectable, documented limitation, not a silent one), Art. 16 (interface-introduction shape, migration numbering, role/grant strategy, and error-handling policy are all deliberately copied from Permissions' precedent rather than reinvented).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.

### 2026-08-17 — File Objects: Store interface introduced, PostgresStore added

**Decision:** Two parts, both against `services/api/internal/fileobjects/`, matching Permissions' identical interface-introduction pattern (see this log's "Permissions: Store interface introduced, PostgresStore added" entry) and Batch A's batch plan.

**Part 1 — `Store` interface introduced.** `store.go`'s concrete `Store` struct is renamed `InMemoryStore` (`newStore()` → `newInMemoryStore()`, keeping this package's pre-existing unexported-constructor convention — unlike Storage's `NewStore`/Identity's `NewInMemoryStore`, this package's constructor was never exported); a new `type Store interface` is defined with the exact same 8 method signatures `InMemoryStore` already had (`putFileObject`, `getFileObject`, `deleteFileObjectRecord`, `addVersion`, `listVersions`, `getVersion`, `addEvent`, `eventsForFileObject`) — unexported, staying in-package. `Service.store` (`service.go`) changes from `*Store` to `Store`. `NewService` also gains a new `store Store` parameter, placed FIRST — `NewService(store Store, storage StorageClient, perms PermissionsClient, audit AuditEmitter) (*Service, error)` — mirroring Permissions' own `NewService(store Store, audit AuditEmitter)` parameter ordering exactly rather than inventing a new convention, and consistent with this package's own pre-existing nil-check discipline (storage/perms/audit were already nil-checked at construction; store now gets the same treatment). No other line in `service.go` changed — every `s.store.xxx(...)` call site is textually identical, because the interface's method names and signatures are identical to what `*Store` already exposed. The two in-package test call sites (`fakes_test.go`'s `newTestService` helper, used by every test in `http_test.go` and `service_test.go`'s own remaining direct call in `TestNewService_PropagatesDefinePolicyFailure`) were updated to pass `newInMemoryStore()` explicitly. `main.go`'s `fileobjects.NewService(...)` call site was deliberately left untouched — that composition-root update is the Chief Architect's own separate, parallel work, per this task's brief; confirmed via `git status` that `main.go` was already modified before this task started.

**Part 2 — `PostgresStore` added**, a second, additive implementation of `Store` (`postgres_store.go`, `NewPostgresStore(pool *pgxpool.Pool) *PostgresStore`), against migration `0005_fileobjects` (`internal/platform/migrations/0005_fileobjects.{up,down}.sql`, migration number `0005` — `0004` had already been claimed by Storage's own parallel, in-flight work by the time this task ran): three tables — `fileobjects_records` (PK `file_object_id`, mirroring `InMemoryStore.fileObjects`), `fileobjects_versions` (PK `version_ref`, plus a `BIGSERIAL seq` column and `(file_object_id, seq)` index to reproduce `InMemoryStore.versions`' creation-order slice-append semantics), `fileobjects_events` (PK `event_id`, same `seq`/index pattern for `InMemoryStore.events`' emission order). `tags` (`fileobjects_records`) and `metadata` (`fileobjects_events`) are `JSONB` columns, marshaled/unmarshaled via `encoding/json` — `Tags []string` and `Metadata map[string]string` respectively. `ascend_app` (already provisioned by migration 0001) gets full `SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE` on all three tables, unlike `audit_events`'/`permissions_history`'s append-only discipline: `deleteFileObjectRecord`'s atomic teardown of a file object + all its versions + all its events (charter §3's content-unreachability guarantee, `DeleteFileObject`'s own designed behavior) is genuine, intended application behavior here, not something to prevent — the spec was explicit on this point and it is followed exactly.

**`deleteFileObjectRecord`'s atomicity** is the one method in this file carrying a real Art. 15 hidden-state hazard: a partial failure (record deleted but versions/events orphaned, or vice versa) would leave exactly the kind of silent inconsistency Art. 15 forbids. `PostgresStore.deleteFileObjectRecord` wraps all three `DELETE`s (events, then versions, then the record — order is irrelevant since there are no foreign-key constraints between these tables, but this order was chosen for readability) in a single transaction, committed only if all three succeed, mirroring `InMemoryStore.deleteFileObjectRecord`'s single mutex-protected critical section covering all three maps. Proved live, not just asserted: `TestPostgresStore_DeleteFileObjectRecordIsAtomicAcrossAllThreeTables` puts a file object with 2 versions and 2 events, deletes it, and then checks all three tables for that `file_object_id` via BOTH this package's own `Store` methods AND a direct `SELECT COUNT(*)` against each of the three tables by name — the row-count check is the stronger proof, since it can't be masked by a bug in the `Store` read methods themselves.

**Error-handling design decision:** `Store`'s 8 methods (introduced in this same change) are fixed to `InMemoryStore`'s pre-existing error-return-free shape, for the identical reason Permissions' own equivalent entry gives: `Service`'s call sites must stay textually and behaviorally identical. Read both precedents (`internal/audit/postgres_store.go`'s Get/All "defensive-error-handling" notes; Permissions' own entry and its `postgres_store.go` doc comment) before landing on this package's split, which turned out simpler than Permissions' — File Objects has no `ownerOf`-style method whose miss-vs-error distinction gates a privilege-escalation bootstrap decision, so no special exception fold was needed anywhere:
- **The four methods with a meaningful zero-ish return** (`getFileObject`, `getVersion` — `(_, bool)`; `listVersions`, `eventsForFileObject` — slice) fold a genuine query failure into the same shape as a legitimate miss/empty result, Audit's own `Get`/`All` precedent exactly. Every caller in `service.go` already treats a miss as `ErrFileObjectNotFound`/`ErrVersionNotFound` or "nothing to show" — never as an implicit allow or implicit success, so this fold is safe in the same "uncertainty must never look like yes" sense Audit's and Permissions' own reasoning establishes.
- **The four void-return mutation methods** (`putFileObject`, `deleteFileObjectRecord`, `addVersion`, `addEvent`) have no return value to fold anything into. Silently no-oping on a genuine write failure would let `Service` proceed to append a `FileEvent`/emit a `fileobjects.*` audit event reporting success for a mutation that never reached the database — exactly the same "silent success hazard Art. 5 forbids" Permissions' own entry documents one layer lower. These four `panic` on a genuine failure via a shared `storeFailure(op, err)` helper, identical in shape and reasoning to Permissions' own `storeFailure` — a panic inside an HTTP handler goroutine is recovered per-request by Go's own `net/http` server (logs the stack trace, closes that one connection, never writes a 200-with-lies response).

**Schema notes:** every query parameterized (`$1..$N`), no string-built SQL anywhere in `postgres_store.go` (the one exception, `postgres_store_test.go`'s direct row-count proof, builds a table name from a fixed, hardcoded three-element literal slice, never from any caller-supplied value). `listVersions`/`eventsForFileObject` order `ORDER BY seq ASC`, matching `InMemoryStore`'s natural append-order slice iteration exactly — `seq` itself is never returned by any `Store` method, purely an internal ordering column, mirroring `audit_events`'/`permissions_history`'s own `seq`/`id` columns.

**Test isolation:** all three tables carry real `DELETE` grants for `ascend_app`, so `postgres_store_test.go` cleans up entirely via the store's own `DATABASE_URL` connection (`cleanupFileObject`) — no `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` admin-connection dance needed, unlike Audit's/Permissions' own append-only tables. Four test cases cover: `putFileObject`/`getFileObject` round trip including `Tags` and `LastHistoryEventID`, plus an upsert-overwrites-in-place check; `addVersion`/`listVersions` creation-order plus `getVersion` hit/miss; `addEvent`/`eventsForFileObject` emission-order plus `Metadata` round-tripping; and `deleteFileObjectRecord`'s atomicity (above).

**Verification:** `go build ./internal/fileobjects/...` and `go vet ./internal/fileobjects/...` clean. `go test ./internal/fileobjects/... -count=1` with `DATABASE_URL` unset: all 4 new Postgres tests report `--- SKIP` with the documented message; every pre-existing test in the package passes unchanged — confirming the interface introduction caused zero regressions. Docker was available and already running (`ascend-postgres-1`, shared with Storage's own parallel, in-flight work); migration `0005_fileobjects` was applied via `platform.RunMigrations` itself, invoked through a temporary, git-ignored `cmd/scratch-migrate` throwaway binary (same technique Permissions' own entry used, needed because `internal/` packages aren't importable from outside `services/api`'s own module tree), which completed successfully on the first try — deleted immediately after use, never committed, confirmed via a post-hoc directory listing showing only the pre-existing `cmd/zz-steward-probe`. With both `DATABASE_URL` and `MIGRATIONS_DATABASE_URL` set: all 4 new Postgres tests pass live, and every pre-existing test in `internal/fileobjects` still passes (58 total test functions, zero failures). `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`: all 6 checks pass. Top-level `go build ./...` fails exactly as this task's brief predicted, on `main.go`'s now-stale `storage.NewFilesystemService`/`fileobjects.NewService` call sites — confirmed via `git status` that `main.go` was already modified (and `internal/storage/` already mid-edit, with untracked `postgres_store.go`/`postgres_store_test.go`) before this task began, i.e. entirely Storage's/the Chief Architect's own parallel, out-of-scope work, not a regression introduced here. A `go test ./internal/storage/...` run independently timed out (60s) during this same verification pass — also confirmed via `git status` to be Storage's own in-flight, uncommitted parallel work, not touched by this task. An `internal/audit` test (`TestPostgresStore_ConcurrentAppends_ChainStaysConsistent`) failed once during this same pass; `internal/audit/postgres_store.go`/`postgres_store_test.go` are themselves untracked (new, in-flight) files per `git status`, confirming Audit's own capability-engineer was concurrently exercising the same shared local Postgres instance during this verification window — the same class of false alarm this log's own "Batch A" entry already documents for concurrent local-dev-stack contention. Neither observation is this task's own regression; both are recorded rather than hidden, per that same entry's precedent.

**Deviation from spec:** none. The `cmd/scratch-migrate` throwaway used to invoke `platform.RunMigrations` directly (rather than `psql`) was created and deleted entirely within this task's own verification step, never committed, and touches no file this task's constraints place off-limits (`internal/platform/` itself was read-only, never edited).

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 2 (`FileObjectRecord`'s identity/owner/history fields are now durable across restarts, not just process-lifetime), Art. 5 (the panic-on-write-failure design keeps every persisted mutation loudly, not silently, tied to its own success/failure — no `FileEvent`/audit record can ever claim success for a create/version/delete that didn't actually land), Art. 8 (every column maps 1:1 onto `DATA_MANIFEST.md`'s documented fields; `blob_ref` remains stored only as an internal cross-reference, never exposed through any `Store` return value beyond the raw column, matching the manifest's existing exclusion), Art. 9 (`fileobjects_records`/`fileobjects_versions`/`fileobjects_events` are exactly what `GetFileHistory`/`ExportFile` read, now durable across restarts), Art. 10 (this file stays entirely within `internal/fileobjects`, importing only `internal/platform`'s pool type and pgx itself — no new cross-capability import), Art. 15 (`deleteFileObjectRecord`'s transaction is the concrete mechanism preventing the orphaned-versions/orphaned-events hidden-state hazard the spec called out explicitly), Art. 16 (interface-introduction shape, migration numbering, `NewService` parameter placement, and Audit-/Permissions-precedent error-handling split are all deliberately copied from prior batch entries rather than reinvented).

**Made by:** File Objects capability-engineer.

## 2026-08-17 — Batch A merge gate closes; Batch B (Storage + File Objects) composition and live verification

**Batch A merge gate — both guardians PASS, no blocking findings.** Constitution Warden independently rebuilt, tested, and live-probed (own throwaway client, own isolated build): full round trip, owner-bootstrap-once semantics proven live end to end (grant → revoke → different grantor denied re-grant), restart-survival through the live API for both Identity and Permissions. Security Steward went further on the security-critical property: live-forced a genuine Postgres privilege failure (temporarily revoking `ascend_app`'s `SELECT` on `permissions_owners` mid-test) and confirmed `ownerOf`'s `("", true)` defensive fold holds under an actual failure, not a simulated one — then restored the grant and confirmed `ascend_app`'s privileges were exactly as before. Also ran a live two-real-identity confused-deputy proof (Alice grants→revokes; Bob, a different real session, is correctly denied re-granting the same resource; `permissions_owners` unchanged by Bob's attempt).

Both guardians surfaced findings, all resolved before this entry:
- **Constitution Warden:** the shared local `audit_events` table was left with a genuine hash-chain gap from the earlier "Security Steward block and fix" pass's live tamper demonstration, and a stray `cmd/zz-steward-probe/` directory was left in the repo tree by a concurrent probe. Both were local-dev-environment artifacts, not code defects — resolved by truncating all tables (see below) and removing the stray directory.
- **Security Steward Finding A (build broken at review time):** caused by Batch B's implementation already landing in the same shared working tree concurrently with Batch A's gate, before Batch B's own composition-root wiring existed — `go build ./...` briefly failed at the repo root. Resolved: this entry's own composition step (below) completes that wiring; `go build ./...` is confirmed clean as of this entry.
- **Security Steward Finding B (real bug, fixed):** `main_test.go`'s `TestPermissionsRoutes_GatedAndReachable/SelfGrant_Bootstraps_...` used a fixed `resource_id` literal (`"res-1"`) — safe under the old in-memory-per-process-lifetime Permissions, but now that Permissions is durable, a fixed resource stays permanently owned by whichever test run first claimed it, making every subsequent run's bootstrap-owner assertion (expects 201) fail (gets 403) against a shared dev database. **Fixed:** `resource_id` now incorporates the test's own freshly-generated `alice.identityRef` (already a random UUID per run), guaranteeing a fresh, never-before-owned resource every time, matching the `uniqueRunID` discipline every `postgres_store_test.go` in this batch already follows.
- **Security Steward Finding C:** same corrupted-`audit_events` observation as Constitution Warden's — resolved by the same truncation.

**Batch B composition:** `services/api/main.go`'s `newRouter()` — `storage.NewFilesystemService(storage.NewPostgresStore(plat.DB), storagePermissionCheckerAdapter{...}, storageAuditEmitterAdapter{...})` and `fileobjects.NewService(fileobjects.NewPostgresStore(plat.DB), fileobjectsStorageClientAdapter{...}, fileobjectsPermissionsClientAdapter{...}, fileobjectsAuditEmitterAdapter{...})` — `store` as each constructor's new first parameter, matching both capability-engineers' reported final signatures exactly. No other change to either capability's wiring.

**Environment reset:** all 11 data tables (`audit_events`, `identities`, `permissions_grants`/`owners`/`history`/`policies`, `storage_blobs`/`storage_wrapping_keys`, `fileobjects_records`/`versions`/`events`) truncated on the shared local Postgres — clears both the disclosed audit hash-chain gap and every batch's accumulated test/verification data, giving Batch B's guardian gate (and any future work) a genuinely clean starting state rather than one carrying forward incidental scars from prior live adversarial testing.

**Live verification performed (same standard as every prior pass), on the clean, reset environment:**
- `go build ./... && go vet ./...` — clean.
- `go test ./...` (repeated twice for idempotency) — clean, including `internal/audit`'s previously-failing integrity tests, now passing again post-reset.
- `go test ./...` with env unset — clean skip.
- `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` — all six checks pass.
- **Live round trip against the real `go run .` binary**: `CreateIdentity` → session → `StoreBlob`/`GetStorageLocation`/`RetrieveBlob` (Storage, metadata now Postgres, bytes still local disk) → `CreateFileObject`/`CreateVersion`/`ListVersions`/`GetFileContent`/`GetFileHistory` (File Objects, fully Postgres) — every step succeeded, version ordering and current-content resolution both correct.
- **Restart-survival, through the live API**: `docker compose restart postgres`, then re-fetched (with the same pre-restart session token) both the blob's storage location and the file object's current content — both 200, both byte-identical to their pre-restart values.

**Rationale:** Same as every prior pass in this series — closes both guardians' findings for Batch A properly (fixing the real test bug, not just noting it), completes Batch B's composition for real, and holds it to the same live-verification bar established since Audit's own first pass.

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session), incorporating Constitution Warden's and Security Steward's independent Batch A gate findings.

## 2026-08-17 — Batch B merge gate: Constitution Warden PASS, Security Steward BLOCKED (lockBlob deadlock)

**Decision:** Ran the standing merge gate on Batch B (Storage + File Objects). Constitution Warden independently rebuilt, tested, and live-probed (own throwaway client): full round trips for both capabilities, restart-survival through the live API, and — the property under the most scrutiny — a live concurrency probe (8 concurrent duplicate `DeleteBlob`/`MoveBlob` calls on one `blob_ref`) confirming `lockBlob` correctly serializes: exactly one real operation, the rest safely rejected/no-op, no double-delete, no dual-copy. **PASS, no blocking findings.**

**Security Steward: 🚫 BLOCKED — a real, live-proven, severe finding, not a false alarm.** Asked to specifically adversarially test `lockBlob`'s connection-holding design, Security Steward fired ~10-20 concurrent duplicate `MoveBlob` requests against ONE blob a single ordinary authenticated user already legitimately owns. Result, confirmed via direct `pg_stat_activity` introspection: every one of `internal/platform`'s 10 pool connections (`maxPoolConns`) got consumed — most blocked on `pg_advisory_lock` waiting for the same key, and the actual lock-holder itself permanently starved of a connection for its own subsequent `get()`/`put()` call, because none remained free. A genuine circular deadlock: `lockBlob` used `context.Background()` throughout (both the connection `Acquire` and the advisory-lock `Exec`), so nothing ever timed out. Live consequences, reproduced twice: 0/20 flood requests ever completed (hung 3m15s until manually killed); an entirely unrelated capability (`POST /v1/identity`) hung too, because the pool is shared across every Postgres-backed capability — this is a whole-server outage, not a Storage-scoped one; the process did not recover on its own.

**Why this blocked rather than just getting disclosed-and-tracked** (the pattern the earlier "Database setup" Audit block established as sufficient for a comparable-looking gap): Security Steward drew the line explicitly — that earlier precedent covered a narrow gap reachable only by a specific *admin* credential no ordinary user holds. This one is reachable by *any single ordinary authenticated user*, against *a resource they already legitimately own*, with a request count matching `maxPoolConns` and no exploit, injection, or privilege escalation required — a cheap, fully-authorized, single-actor denial of service against the whole platform. The registry's own prior disclosure of this exact mechanism (written by Storage's capability-engineer alongside the original `lockBlob` implementation) additionally understated it in three specific ways Security Steward called out: calling a permanent, unrecoverable deadlock a "stall"; framing a whole-server outage as Storage-scoped; and framing "one user, ~10 requests, zero privilege" as "real concurrent multi-user load at unusual scale." Both the severity and the disclosure needed fixing, not just the disclosure.

**Required fix, dispatched immediately:** a bounded timeout on `lockBlob`'s connection-acquire and advisory-lock steps (`context.WithTimeout` in place of `context.Background()`), converting an unrecoverable deadlock into a bounded, recoverable failure — consistent with `lockBlob`'s own existing "fail loudly rather than proceed unsafely" philosophy, extended to cover the wait itself. Security Steward's stated minimum bar to unblock; a fully separate connection pool for lock-holders (closing the cross-capability blast radius entirely, not just bounding it) was noted as the more complete fix but not required to unblock. Dispatched back to Storage's own capability-engineer (already holding full context on `lockBlob`'s design) rather than reassigned, per this org's ownership model. `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md`'s bullet corrected to state the true severity (total/indefinite/whole-server/single-ordinary-user-triggerable) immediately, not held pending the code fix — an inaccurate disclosure should not persist even briefly once its inaccuracy is confirmed.

**Status:** fix in progress as of this entry; see the follow-up entry once it lands and is re-verified live (bounded recovery under flood, unrelated capabilities staying responsive throughout — the specific re-proof Security Steward will expect before this batch can be considered merge-ready).

**Rationale:** Same discipline as the Audit charter-overclaiming block — a guardian gate exists precisely to catch the gap between what a design claims/discloses and what it actually delivers under real adversarial load, and the fix is to close that gap for real (both the code and the words describing it), not to argue the finding away or ship on a technicality.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 7 (security-motivated machinery — the advisory lock exists to protect data integrity — whose actual availability cost wasn't honestly represented is exactly the "security must never reduce ownership" failure mode turned sideways: a user's own legitimate, ordinary use of a capability they own must never be able to take down the platform for every other user), Art. 1 (an unrecoverable whole-server outage is a direct, severe ownership failure — no user can act on any of their data while the process is down), Art. 14 (trust through disclosure — the correction to the registry's own prior wording is itself the Art. 14 act, done immediately rather than deferred to the code fix).

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session), incorporating Constitution Warden's and Security Steward's independent Batch B gate findings.

### 2026-08-17 — Storage: lockBlob bounded-timeout fix (Security Steward block)

**Decision:** Fix dispatched against the finding recorded in this log's immediately preceding entry ("Batch B merge gate: Constitution Warden PASS, Security Steward BLOCKED (lockBlob deadlock)") — the minimum bar Security Steward stated to unblock: bound `PostgresStore.lockBlob`'s `pool.Acquire` and `pg_advisory_lock` calls with a real deadline instead of `context.Background()`, so a failure to obtain the lock within a bounded window fails loudly rather than blocking forever.

**The finding, restated for this entry's own record (see the preceding entry for the full report):** `lockBlob`'s connection-holding advisory-lock design (this log's earlier "Storage: Store interface introduced, PostgresStore added" entry) used `context.Background()` for both the `pool.Acquire` call and the `SELECT pg_advisory_lock` `Exec` call — no deadline anywhere. Security Steward fired 20 concurrent `MoveBlob` requests against one `blob_ref` a single ordinary authenticated user legitimately owned and, via direct `pg_stat_activity` introspection, confirmed every one of `platform.maxPoolConns` (10) connections got consumed — most goroutines blocked inside `pg_advisory_lock` waiting on the same key, and the actual lock holder itself then permanently starved waiting on `pool.Acquire` for its own subsequent `get()`/`put()` call, because zero connections remained free. A genuine circular deadlock with nothing to resolve it: 0/20 requests ever completed (hung past 3 minutes until manually killed, reproduced twice), and — because the pool is shared across every Postgres-backed capability, not Storage-scoped — an entirely unrelated capability (`POST /v1/identity`) hung too. This capability's own prior documentation of the risk (`postgres_store.go`'s "KNOWN POOL-SIZING CAVEAT" comment and this log's own earlier entry) materially understated what was actually happening: it called a permanent, unrecoverable deadlock a "stall"; framed a whole-server outage as Storage-scoped; and framed "one ordinary user, ~10-20 requests, zero privilege escalation" as "real concurrent multi-user load at unusual scale." Both the severity and the prior disclosure needed correcting, not just the code.

**Fix applied, exactly matching Security Steward's stated minimum bar:** `postgres_store.go`'s `lockBlob` now wraps the connection-acquire-and-lock-take sequence in a single `context.WithTimeout(context.Background(), lockAcquireTimeout)` (5 seconds — chosen as generous relative to this package's real critical-section cost, a handful of parameterized queries plus at most one local-filesystem read/write, normally low-single-digit milliseconds, while still short enough that a client sees a failure within a reasonable patience window rather than a silent multi-minute hang) instead of `context.Background()`. The returned unlock closure's `pg_advisory_unlock` call is separately bounded by its own fresh `lockReleaseTimeout` (also 5 seconds) — a much rarer failure mode (the connection is already held, not contended for) but bounded for the same "no unbounded `context.Background()` wait anywhere in this file" discipline. pgx propagates context cancellation into a genuine Postgres-side query cancellation for a blocked `pg_advisory_lock` (not merely a client-side give-up that leaves the server-side wait running), so a timed-out waiter's connection is actually freed back to the pool, not abandoned while still consuming a slot. A timeout on either step panics via the same `storeFailure` helper every other `lockBlob` failure mode already used — no new failure-handling philosophy, the timeout is just one more way the existing "fail loudly, never silently proceed unsafely" rule can trigger — and that panic is recovered per-request by the composition root's `chi middleware.Recoverer` (`services/api/main.go`) into a clean `500`, exactly like every other `lockBlob`-originated panic already was, never an unhandled crash.

**What this fix does and does not close, stated plainly (not oversold):** it converts the failure mode from *permanent, unrecoverable, whole-server outage* into *bounded-duration pool contention that self-resolves within `lockAcquireTimeout`, after which every blocked caller fails loudly with a clear error instead of hanging forever.* It does **not** eliminate shared-pool contention itself: a burst of concurrent requests against one hot `blob_ref` can still transiently consume most or all of the shared pool for up to `lockAcquireTimeout`, and an unrelated capability drawing from the same pool can still see elevated latency during that window (every other capability's own `PostgresStore` still calls `pool.Acquire` under `context.Background()`, with no bound of its own, as of this writing). What changed is that the window is now bounded and self-resolving rather than permanent and requiring manual intervention — this is the real, live-verified improvement Security Steward's stated minimum bar asked for, not a claim that shared-pool contention is fully eliminated. Security Steward separately noted a dedicated connection pool for lock-holding (closing the cross-capability blast radius entirely, not just bounding it) as the more complete fix; that remains a real architecture change beyond this fix's mandated minimum bar and is tracked as a follow-up, not implemented here.

**Live re-verification (not just unit tests):** built a throwaway Go client (`services/api/cmd/zz-lockblob-probe`, deleted immediately after use, never committed) that drove the real `go run .` binary end to end — `CreateIdentity` → `GetSessionChallenge` → `IssueSession` (real Ed25519 signing) → `StoreBlob` → 20 concurrent duplicate `MoveBlob` requests against that one `blob_ref`, with 5 unrelated `POST /v1/identity` (`CreateIdentity`) requests fired concurrently, staggered across the flood window, to probe cross-capability responsiveness — then one more ordinary request immediately after, to confirm the server needed no restart. Run twice against real Postgres:
- **Run 1:** flood resolved in 5.20s wall-clock for all 20 requests (2 succeeded with real relocations, 18 failed loudly with `500`s at ~5.05-5.20s each — the bounded timeout firing, confirmed in server logs as `storeFailure` panics cleanly recovered by `middleware.Recoverer`, not a crash). All 5 unrelated `CreateIdentity` probes succeeded (`200`), latency 72ms-4.88s (elevated during the flood window, as documented above, but never hung). Post-flood `MoveBlob` succeeded in 12ms — the server was fully live and normal immediately after, no restart.
- **Run 2** (immediately following, same live server, no restart in between): flood resolved in 5.19s (6 succeeded, 14 failed loudly, same bounded shape); all 5 unrelated probes succeeded, latency 19ms-4.84s; post-flood request succeeded in 16ms.

This is the exact proof Security Steward's block asked for: bounded recovery (5.2s, not 3m15s/never), the server self-resolving with no manual kill/restart across two consecutive floods, and the unrelated capability (`POST /v1/identity`) staying responsive throughout both floods (100% success, worst-case latency under 5s, never a hang).

**Severity language corrected in the three places Security Steward named:** (1) `postgres_store.go`'s `lockBlob` doc comment — replaced the "KNOWN POOL-SIZING CAVEAT" section with a "BOUNDED-TIMEOUT FIX (Security Steward block)" section stating the live-proven finding, the fix, and the "what this does and does not close" disclosure verbatim as above. (2) `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` — the Chief Architect's own correction, already applied per the preceding entry, ahead of this code fix landing. (3) This decision log — this entry itself, plus the preceding "Batch B merge gate" entry recording the block as Security Steward and the Chief Architect reported it.

**Verification:** `go build ./... && go vet ./...` (from `services/api`) both clean. `go test ./internal/storage/... -count=1` with `DATABASE_URL` unset: all Postgres tests report `--- SKIP`, every pre-existing and previously-added test passes unchanged. With `DATABASE_URL` set: full package suite passes, including the two `lockBlob`-serialization proofs added alongside the original `PostgresStore` work (`TestPostgresStore_LockBlob_SerializesConcurrentGetThenPutSequences`, `TestPostgresStore_LockBlob_DifferentBlobRefsDoNotBlockEachOther`) — the bounded timeout does not regress correctness under the non-adversarial concurrency level those tests exercise (6 workers, comfortably under `maxPoolConns`). `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`: all 6 checks pass. The live flood re-verification above is the load-bearing proof for this fix specifically — unit tests alone cannot exercise real connection-pool exhaustion.

**Deviation from spec:** none. The more complete fix (a dedicated connection pool for lock-holding) was explicitly not required to unblock and was not attempted here, consistent with Security Steward's own stated minimum bar.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 1 (this fix is what actually restores the property the earlier entry named: no user's own legitimate, ordinary use of a capability they own should be able to take the platform down for every other user — bounding the failure mode is what makes that true again), Art. 5 (the panic-on-timeout path keeps a `lockBlob` failure loud and attributable — a `500` with a logged stack trace — never a silent hang that leaves an operator unable to tell what happened), Art. 7 (security-motivated machinery whose real availability cost is now honestly bounded and disclosed, not merely claimed safe), Art. 14 (the corrected doc comment states plainly what the fix does and does not close, rather than reframing "bounded" as "solved"), Art. 15 (the residual cross-capability latency risk during a flood is disclosed, not hidden, exactly like the original pool-sizing caveat this entry supersedes).

**Made by:** Storage capability-engineer.

## 2026-08-17 — Batch B merge gate closes: Security Steward lifts the lockBlob block

**Decision:** Sent the fix above back to Security Steward for their own re-review (their block, theirs to lift — not the Chief Architect's to declare resolved on their behalf). They independently re-verified live, going beyond both the original finding and the fixing engineer's own re-verification: 3×20-request floods plus a 4-round sustained-attack test (no restart between rounds) against a real `go run .` binary they started themselves, with their own throwaway adversarial client — 0 hangs across 60+ flood requests; 987+ concurrent `POST /v1/identity` probes fired *during* the contention windows (not spaced out afterward) at 100% success, directly proving "stays responsive throughout," the stronger of the two properties their original block required; post-flood recovery in 12-25ms every time; connection-pool state confirmed via `psql`/`pg_stat_activity` returning to a clean idle baseline with no manual intervention, across every round.

**Independent judgment on the 5-second bound:** endorsed as reasonable, not merely accepted — ~50-400x observed normal-case critical-section latency (12-102ms), zero false-positive timeouts under any legitimate-contention scenario tested, short enough that a real user experiences a bounded, clearly-failed request rather than something indistinguishable from a platform outage. Noted a shorter bound (e.g. 2s) would be defensible too, but did not require changing it.

**Documentation check:** confirmed the corrected language in `postgres_store.go`, this log, and (after this entry) `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md` all state plainly what changed and what remains open (residual shared-pool contention latency, not eliminated) — no overclaiming, matching the bar their original block set.

**One follow-up actioned:** Security Steward flagged `docs/CAPABILITY_REGISTRY.md`'s bullet as stale ("fix in progress," not yet reflecting the now-complete, independently-re-verified fix) — updated in this same pass to "Resolved," preserving the full severity history rather than erasing it.

**Outcome:** Security Steward's Batch B block is lifted. Combined with Constitution Warden's earlier PASS (no blocking findings) on the same batch, **Batch B (Storage + File Objects on real Postgres) is now fully merge-gated and closed** — the third capability pair in this database-migration series to clear both guardians, after Audit and Batch A (Identity + Permissions).

**Rationale:** This is the same discipline the Audit charter-overclaiming block modeled: a real finding gets a real fix, the fix gets independently re-verified by the same guardian who found the problem (not just trusted from the fixing engineer's report), and the documentation is corrected to match reality at every stage — found, in-progress, and resolved — rather than only at the end.

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 16 (the same guardian who blocks is the one who lifts the block — consistency in who owns a finding end to end), Art. 14 (independent re-verification by the blocking party, not self-certification by the fixing party, is what makes "resolved" trustworthy rather than merely asserted).

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session), incorporating Security Steward's independent re-verification.

### 2026-08-17 — Batch C (Session/Request Authentication) design

**Decision:** The last capability in the database-migration series, and architecturally the most distinct: `sessionauth` holds two separate in-memory stores (`nonceStore`, `sessionStore`) with genuinely different lifetimes, and per the original "Database setup" design entry, they split across two different backends — **challenge nonces → Redis** (pure ephemeral, single-use, no durability/export requirement, exactly what Redis is for), **sessions → Postgres** (durable; `ExportSessions` must show expired-but-not-yet-revoked sessions, which a Redis TTL would evict and lose). A third store, `failureTracker` (anomaly.go, per-token-fingerprint failure counter), stays in-memory, unmigrated — `DATA_MANIFEST.md` already classifies it as transient operational state, not a persisted field, so it needs no durable backing.

**Nonce store on Redis — designed to lean on Redis's native TTL, not reimplement expiry in Go:** value stored as a plain delimited string (`identityRef + "\x1f" + deviceID`, mirroring Permissions' own `resourceKey` delimiter convention) under key `"sessionauth:nonce:" + nonce`, with `SET key value EX <seconds>` at `put` time — an expired nonce simply stops existing, no manual expiry check needed on read. `peek` (read-only) is a plain `GET` + string comparison. `consumeIfValid` (the anti-replay-critical operation) needs the same atomicity guarantee the in-memory version's single mutex gave it — implemented as a Lua script (`EVAL`) doing GET-compare-DEL as one atomic Redis operation, so two concurrent replay attempts still can't both win. No JSON, no cjson dependency in the script — the plain-string value format is what makes a simple Lua string-equality check sufficient.

**Session store on Postgres:** one table, `sessionauth_sessions` (migration `0006`), full CRUD granted to `ascend_app` (unlike `audit_events`'/`permissions_history`'s append-only discipline — session revocation is a legitimate, real `DELETE`, per the capability's own existing "revocation is real and immediate" doc comment, Art. 7). `get` must keep returning expired-but-not-deleted sessions (no store-level auto-expiry) — `Service`'s own `ValidateSession`/`resolveCaller` logic, which checks `ExpiresAtUnix` itself, is completely unchanged.

**Interface introduction:** both `NonceStore` and `SessionStore` become real, exported interface types (unlike Permissions'/Storage's/File Objects' unexported-method interfaces, these must be nameable from `NewService`'s signature, which the composition root in `main.go` calls directly) — `InMemoryNonceStore`/`RedisNonceStore` and `InMemorySessionStore`/`PostgresSessionStore` respectively, both pairs living inside `internal/sessionauth`, mirroring every prior batch's "the concrete Postgres/Redis type always lives in the capability's own package" rule. `NewService` gains two new leading parameters (`nonces NonceStore, sessions SessionStore`), matching the "store(s) first" convention Batch B established.

**Known, accepted testing limitation:** `RedisNonceStore` cannot use `Service`'s fake-clock (`now func() time.Time`) testing seam the way `InMemoryNonceStore`'s expiry tests do — Redis's own TTL runs on real wall-clock time regardless of what `Service.now` is overridden to. `RedisNonceStore`'s own expiry tests must use a short real TTL and a real (bounded) sleep instead — a different proof strategy for the same guarantee, matching precedent (Storage's `lockBlob` needed an entirely different concurrency proof for its Postgres implementation than the in-memory mutex version).

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 1 (sessions surviving a restart matters — `ExportSessions`/`ListActiveSessions` are how a user actually sees and controls their own active-device footprint), Art. 16 (backend choice follows the same Postgres-for-durable/Redis-for-ephemeral split decided at the start of this series, not re-litigated), Art. 10 (both interface introductions keep `Service`'s own logic unchanged; the concrete Redis/Postgres types stay inside `internal/sessionauth`, never leaking into `internal/platform`).

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session).

---

### 2026-08-17 — Session/Request Authentication: NonceStore/SessionStore interfaces introduced, Redis + Postgres implementations added

**Decision:** Implemented the design the Chief Architect's own "Batch C (Session/Request Authentication) design" entry (above) laid out, against `docs/capabilities/session-authentication.charter.md` and the frozen types in `types.go`. `store.go`'s two concrete in-memory types were renamed and exported (`nonceStore` → `InMemoryNonceStore`/`newNonceStore()` → `NewInMemoryNonceStore()`; `sessionStore` → `InMemorySessionStore`/`newSessionStore()` → `NewInMemorySessionStore()`), and two new exported interfaces introduced (`NonceStore`, 3 methods; `SessionStore`, 7 methods) with unexported method names identical to the concrete types' pre-existing ones — kept unexported so only types inside `internal/sessionauth` can implement either interface, mirroring every prior batch's "the concrete DB-backed type lives inside the capability's own package" rule, while still being nameable from `NewService`'s new signature (unlike Permissions'/Storage's fully-unexported-interface convention, which didn't need that). `Service.nonces`/`Service.sessions` field types changed from the old concrete pointer types to the two new interfaces; `NewService` gained `nonces NonceStore, sessions SessionStore` as its first two parameters, matching the "store(s) first" convention Batch B (Storage/File Objects) established — final signature: `NewService(nonces NonceStore, sessions SessionStore, devices DeviceResolver, emitter AuditEmitter) *Service`. All 13 `NewService(` call sites in `service_test.go` updated to pass `NewInMemoryNonceStore(), NewInMemorySessionStore()` explicitly.

**RedisNonceStore (`redis_nonce_store.go`):** leans on Redis's own native per-key TTL rather than reimplementing expiry in Go. Key `"sessionauth:nonce:" + nonce`; value a plain `identityRef + "\x1f" + deviceID` string (mirroring Permissions' `activeGrantKey` delimiter convention — no JSON needed for a two-field value). `put` issues `SET key value EX <ttl-seconds>`, clamping the TTL to a minimum of 1 second if `time.Until(ExpiresAtUnix)` is already ≤0 — defensive, because Redis's `SET ... EX` rejects a non-positive expire value outright rather than treating it as "expire immediately," and the clamp keeps `put` from ever failing on a borderline-stale expiry while still leaving the nonce functionally worthless within about a second. `peek` is a plain `GET` + string split/compare; its `now` parameter is accepted only to satisfy the `NonceStore` interface and is explicitly documented as unused — Redis's TTL is the sole source of truth for expiry, a `GET` miss already covers "never issued" and "expired" identically. `consumeIfValid` — the anti-replay-critical operation — is a single Lua `EVAL` (`GET`, compare, conditional `DEL`, exactly the script this task specified), which genuinely replicates the in-memory version's single-mutex atomicity: Redis executes one `EVAL` invocation as an atomic, uninterruptible operation, so two concurrent replay attempts on the same nonce can never both return true, and a binding mismatch never deletes the key. Proved live with a 25-goroutine concurrency test (`TestRedisNonceStore_ConsumeIfValid_ConcurrentReplay_ExactlyOneWins`) against a real local Redis: exactly 1 of 25 concurrent calls won every run. Expiry proved with a real ~300ms-to-1s TTL and a real, bounded 2.5s `time.Sleep` (`TestRedisNonceStore_Expiry_RealTTL_BecomesUnusable`) rather than any fake-clock seam — `Service.now`'s fake-clock testing seam cannot influence Redis's own wall-clock-driven TTL, a known, accepted limitation already flagged in the Chief Architect's design entry, matching the precedent Storage's `lockBlob` set (a different concurrency-proof strategy per backend).

**PostgresSessionStore (`postgres_session_store.go`) + migration `0006_sessionauth_sessions`:** one table, full CRUD granted to the pre-existing `ascend_app` role (no new role created) — unlike `audit_events`'/`permissions_history`'s append-only discipline, session revocation is a real, legitimate `DELETE` per this capability's own "revocation is real and immediate" doc comment (Art. 7). `get` performs no store-level expiry filtering whatsoever (no `WHERE expires_at_unix >= now`) — confirmed by a dedicated test (`TestPostgresSessionStore_Get_ReturnsExpiredSessionUnchanged`) that a session with a past `expires_at_unix` round-trips unchanged; `Service`'s own `ValidateSession`/`resolveCaller` expiry checks are completely untouched and remain the only place expiry is enforced. `listActiveForIdentity` is the one method with a real `WHERE expires_at_unix >= $2` clause, distinct from `listAllForIdentity`'s unfiltered scan — proved genuinely different with a mixed active/expired/multi-identity fixture (`TestPostgresSessionStore_ListActiveVsListAll_GenuinelyDiffer`).

**Error-handling design for `PostgresSessionStore`'s 7 error-return-free methods**, following the option Permissions'/Storage's `PostgresStore` already established rather than inventing a third approach: `get` folds a genuine query failure into `(Session{}, false)` — the same shape as a legitimate miss, safe because every caller (`ValidateSession`, `resolveCaller`, `RevokeSession`) already treats `ok=false` as "not valid," never as an implicit allow. `listActiveForIdentity`/`listAllForIdentity` fold a genuine failure into a nil slice, matching Audit's/Permissions' identical precedent for read-only list/export paths. The four mutation methods (`put`, `touchLastUsed`, `delete`, `deleteAllForIdentity`) have no return value (or, for `deleteAllForIdentity`, a plausible-looking `int` count) to silently fold a real failure into — a swallowed error there would let `RevokeAllSessions` report "N sessions revoked" when the `DELETE` never actually ran, exactly the silent-success hazard Art. 5 forbids for a security-relevant, Art.-7-obligated mutation — so all four panic on a genuine query/exec failure via a `sessionStoreFailure` helper, mirroring Permissions' `storeFailure` exactly. `touchLastUsed`/`delete`'s documented "safe no-op for an unknown token" behavior is preserved: an `UPDATE`/`DELETE` matching zero rows is not a Postgres error, so it never reaches the panic path — proved directly by `TestPostgresSessionStore_TouchLastUsed_UnknownToken_SafeNoOp`/`TestPostgresSessionStore_Delete_UnknownToken_SafeNoOp`.

**`failureTracker` (anomaly.go) left completely untouched**, per this task's own brief and `DATA_MANIFEST.md`'s existing classification of it as transient, unmigrated operational state.

**Verification:** `go build ./internal/sessionauth/...` and `go vet ./internal/sessionauth/...` clean. `go test ./internal/sessionauth/... -count=1` with `DATABASE_URL`/`REDIS_URL` unset: all 15 new tests (6 Redis, 9 Postgres) report `--- SKIP` with the documented message; every pre-existing test in the package passes unchanged with the renamed `NewInMemoryNonceStore()`/`NewInMemorySessionStore()` call sites — zero regressions. Docker was already running (`ascend-postgres-1`, `ascend-redis-1`, both healthy, shared with other in-flight parallel work) — migration `0006_sessionauth_sessions` was applied via `platform.RunMigrations` itself, invoked through a temporary, git-ignored `cmd/scratch-migrate` throwaway binary (same technique Permissions'/Storage's/File Objects' own capability engineers used, needed because `internal/` packages aren't importable from outside `services/api`'s own module tree), which completed successfully on the first try (`schema_migrations.version` advanced 5→6, `dirty=false`); confirmed live via `psql` that `ascend_app`'s privileges on `sessionauth_sessions` are exactly `arwd` (SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE) — deleted immediately after use, never committed. With both `DATABASE_URL` and `REDIS_URL` set: all 30 tests in the package pass live (including the concurrency and real-sleep-expiry proofs), rerun twice consecutively to confirm re-runnability; confirmed post-run that `sessionauth_sessions` has 0 rows (`SELECT count(*)`) and Redis has 0 keys matching `sessionauth:*` (`redis-cli --scan --pattern`), proving every test's cleanup actually ran. `go build ./...` from `services/api` fails only at `main.go:121` (`sessionauth.NewService`'s now-changed signature) — confirmed via `git status` that `main.go` was already modified before this task started, entirely the Chief Architect's own parallel, out-of-scope composition-root work, exactly as this task's brief predicted and placed off-limits. `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh`: all 6 checks pass (Art. 5 audit events, Art. 5 audit events TS, Art. 8 data manifests, Art. 9 exportability, Art. 2 file objects, Art. 10 modularity).

**Deviation from spec:** none. The `cmd/scratch-migrate` throwaway used to invoke `platform.RunMigrations` directly was created and deleted entirely within this task's own verification step, never committed, and touches no file this task's constraints place off-limits (`internal/platform/` itself was read-only, never edited, aside from the one new migration file pair `internal/platform/migrations/0006_sessionauth_sessions.{up,down}.sql`).

**Article(s) invoked:** Art. 5 (every `PostgresSessionStore` mutation either succeeds durably or panics loudly — no path silently reports a revocation, issuance, or touch that didn't actually happen), Art. 7 (session revocation stays a real, immediate `DELETE` with full CRUD granted deliberately, and the Redis Lua-script atomicity proof directly defends the anti-replay guarantee the charter's threat model requires), Art. 8 (every column maps 1:1 onto `DATA_MANIFEST.md`'s existing field list; no new field introduced, `DATA_MANIFEST.md` updated only to point at the new Redis-backed mechanics achieving the same "not retained once consumed or expired" commitment), Art. 9 (`ExportSessions`' dependency on `listAllForIdentity` returning expired-but-not-revoked sessions is preserved exactly, proven live against real Postgres), Art. 10 (both concrete backend types live inside `internal/sessionauth`; `Service`'s own logic is completely unchanged beyond the two field/parameter type swaps), Art. 16 (backend split and interface-introduction shape both follow the Chief Architect's own prior design entry and Permissions'/Storage's established precedent, not re-litigated).

**Made by:** Session/Request Authentication capability-engineer.

## 2026-08-17 — Batch C merge gate: both guardians PASS — database-migration series closed

**Decision:** Both Constitution Warden and Security Steward independently reviewed Batch C to the full standard held throughout this series (real rebuild/test, real mechanical checks, live adversarial probing of the real binary with their own throwaway clients — not prose review), specifically because this capability's entire charter is the anti-replay/anti-forgery threat model and the underlying mechanism (a Go mutex → a Redis Lua script) genuinely changed, not just its storage location.

**Constitution Warden: PASS.** Independently proved, live, the one property this whole Postgres-vs-Redis split exists for: set one session's `expires_at_unix` into the past directly via `psql`, called `ExportSessions` — the expired session appeared with its past timestamp unchanged, while `ListActiveSessions` correctly excluded it. Also independently ran 30/50-goroutine concurrent-replay floods (exactly one winner every time) and a restart-survival proof. Confirmed the registry's closing claim — "every backend capability chartered in this build is now backed by real, durable storage" — capability by capability: a `postgres_store.go`/equivalent exists for Audit, Identity, Permissions, Storage, File Objects, and Session/Request Authentication, and `main.go`'s composition root wires every one with no in-memory fallback anywhere. Two non-blocking observations, both sandbox/process-hygiene, not code defects: cross-package test contention on the shared local `audit_events` table beyond what was already documented (resolved by running the affected package in isolation — confirmed unrelated to any Batch C code path); and evidence of concurrent writes to the shared scratchpad directory from another process during this review, affecting only the guardian's own testing workflow, not the server's live behavior once probed in isolation — flagged as worth a harness-level look if concurrent guardian sessions continue sharing one scratchpad/Docker stack, not something this pass's code needs to address.

**Security Steward: PASS.** The most adversarially thorough gate in this entire series: live replay rejection (single + 30-concurrent, exactly one winner every time); cross-nonce interference tested and clean; forged/mismatched identity-device binding rejected, with the nonce's raw Redis value and TTL confirmed untouched by the mismatch attempt (direct `redis-cli GET`/`TTL` inspection, not just the HTTP-layer response) and successfully consumable afterward with the correct binding; nonce values confirmed to be 192 bits of `crypto/rand`, never derived from identity/device/time, closing any "guess another caller's key" concern; SQL injection attempted end-to-end through the real challenge-issuance-to-session-write path, handled safely, with the additional structural observation that `identity_ref`/`device_id` reaching Postgres are always server-generated UUIDs in practice (parameterization is defense-in-depth on an already-narrow surface, not the sole mitigation); revocation re-confirmed immediate with no staleness window; grants/roles confirmed exactly as the migration declares, no new role created; confirmed no connection string or secret ever logged.

**Outcome: the database-migration series that began with Audit is now fully closed.** Every backend capability — Audit, Identity, Permissions, Storage (metadata), File Objects, Session/Request Authentication — has passed an independent, live-adversarial guardian merge gate on real Postgres/Redis, with two genuine findings caught and fixed along the way (Audit's charter overclaiming a guarantee Postgres ownership structurally can't deliver; Storage's `lockBlob` unbounded deadlock, a real single-user-triggerable whole-server DoS) rather than zero findings across six passes, which would have been the more suspicious outcome. Environment reset (all 11 data tables truncated) and full verification (`go build`/`vet`/`test` clean both with and without env vars, `bash scripts/constitution/run-all.sh` all six checks) re-run one final time after this gate closes, before considering the series done.

**Rationale:** Closes the series to the same bar every batch within it was held to — no batch got a lighter gate for being later or smaller, and this last one, arguably the highest-stakes given its threat model, got the most adversarial testing of all six.

**Made by:** Chief Architect (this session), incorporating Constitution Warden's and Security Steward's independent Batch C gate findings.
